## Conclusion / 结语

===Original===
I did not return to England by the most direct route, but took the opportunity of seeing more of men and manners in yet other lands. Arrived in England at last, we set to work bravely at Aldershott to retrieve our fallen fortunes, and stem off the ruin originated in the Crimea, but all in vain; and at last defeated by fortune, but not I think disgraced, we were obliged to capitulate on very honourable conditions. In plain truth, the old Crimean firm of Seacole and Day was dissolved finally, and its partners had to recommence the world anew. And so ended *our* campaign. One of us started only the other day for the Antipodes, while the other is ready to take any journey to any place where a stout heart and two experienced hands may be of use.

Perhaps it would be right if I were to express more shame and annoyance than I really feel at the pecuniarily disastrous issue of my Crimean adventures, but I cannot﻿—I really cannot. When I would try and feel ashamed of myself for being poor and helpless, I only experience a glow of pride at the other and more pleasing events of my career; when I think of the few whom I failed to pay in full (and so far from blaming me some of them are now my firmest friends), I cannot help remembering also the many who profess themselves indebted to me.

===Chinese===
我不是取最直接的路回英国的，而是趁此机会，再到些别的国度，多看看世人与风土。终于抵达英国后，我们雄心勃勃地在奥尔德肖特（Aldershott）重整旗鼓，想挽回败落的家业，堵住在克里米亚种下的祸根，可一切徒劳；最后，我们被命运击垮——但我想并无辱没名誉——不得不在十分体面的条件下投降了事。说白了，克里米亚的老商号「西科尔与戴」就此彻底解散，合伙人只得从头另起炉灶。我们这场征战就这样收了场。一位合伙人前些天刚动身去了对跖之地（the Antipodes，即澳洲），另一位呢，随时准备上路，去任何地方——只要一颗结实的心和两只历练过的手用得上。

倘若我对克里米亚冒险在钱财上的惨淡结局，表现出比真实感受更多的羞愧与懊恼，或许才算得体；可我不能——实在不能。当我试着为自己的穷困无助害臊时，心里反而涌起一阵自豪的热流，为的是我生涯里那些更愉快的遭际；想起少数几个我没能够全额偿付的人（他们非但不怪我，有几个如今还是我最铁的朋友），我便不由得也想起那许多自称欠着我情的人。

===Original===
Let me, in as few words as possible, state the results of my Crimean campaign. To be sure, I returned from it shaken in health. I came home wounded, as many others did. Few constitutions, indeed, were the better for those winters before Sebastopol, and I was too hard worked not to feel their effects; for a little labour fatigues me now﻿—I cannot watch by sickbeds as I could﻿—a week’s want of rest quite knocks me up now. Then I returned bankrupt in fortune. Whereas others in my position may have come back to England rich and prosperous, I found myself poor﻿—beggared. So few words can tell what I have lost.

But what have I gained? I should need a volume to describe that fairly; so much is it, and so cheaply purchased by suffering ten times worse than what I have experienced. I have more than once heard people say that they would gladly suffer illness to enjoy the delights of convalescence, and so, by enduring a few days’ pain, gain the tender love of relatives and sympathy of friends. And on this principle I rejoice in the trials which have borne me such pleasures as those I now enjoy, for wherever I go I am sure to meet some smiling face; every step I take in the crowded London streets may bring me in contact with some friend, forgotten by me, perhaps, but who soon reminds me of our old life before Sebastopol; it seems very long ago now, when I was of use to him and he to me.

===Chinese===
让我用尽量少的几句话，交代我克里米亚征战的成绩。的确，我回来时身体垮了。我是带着伤回家的，同其他许多人一样。说实话，谁的身子骨经得起塞瓦斯托波尔城前那几个冬天还更好？我操劳过度，不可能不落下印记；如今一点小劳作就叫我不堪疲倦——再不能像从前那样守在病榻边——缺上一星期的觉，就能把我彻底撂倒。其次，我回来时钱财上破了产。别人处在我这位置，或许个个衣锦还乡、富足兴旺，我却发现自己一贫如洗——彻底赤贫。寥寥数语，就道尽了我的所失。

可我得了什么呢？要公道地说清这个，恐怕得写上一整卷书；所得如此之多，而代价不过是比我经历过的苦十倍的苦难——何其廉价。我不止一次听人说，他们甘愿生病，好享受康复的欢愉，好以忍受几日的疼痛，换来亲人的疼爱与朋友的同情。循着这个道理，我为那些磨难而欢喜，正是它们给我带来了如今享受的这些快乐；因为无论走到哪里，我准会撞见一张笑脸；在伦敦拥挤的街头，我每走一步，都可能遇见哪位朋友——或许我已忘了他，他很快便提醒我想起我们在塞瓦斯托波尔城前的旧日生涯；如今想来恍如隔世，那时我于他有助，他于我有用。

===Original===
Where, indeed, do I not find friends. In omnibuses, in river steamboats, in places of public amusement, in quiet streets and courts, where taking shortcuts I lose my way ofttimes, spring up old familiar faces to remind me of the months spent on Spring Hill. The sentries at Whitehall relax from the discharge of their important duty of guarding nothing to give me a smile of recognition; the very newspaper offices look friendly as I pass them by; busy Printing-house Yard puts on a cheering smile, and the *Punch* office in Fleet Street sometimes laughs outright. Now, would all this have happened if I had returned to England a rich woman? Surely not.

A few words more ere I bring these egotistical remarks to a close. It is naturally with feelings of pride and pleasure that I allude to the committee recently organized to aid me; and if I indulge in the vanity of placing their names before my readers, it is simply because every one of the following noblemen and gentlemen knew me in the Crimea, and by consenting to assist me now record publicly their opinion of my services there. And yet I may reasonably on other grounds be proud of the fact, that it has been stated publicly that my present embarrassments originated in my charities and incessant labours among the army, by

- Major-General Lord Rokeby, K.C.B.
- H.S.H. Prince Edward of Saxe Weimar, C.B.
- His Grace the Duke of Wellington.
- His Grace the Duke of Newcastle.
- The Right Hon. Lord Ward.
- General Sir John Burgoyne, K.C.B.
- Major-General Sir Richard Airey, K.C.B.
- Rear-Admiral Sir Stephen Lushington, K.C.B.
- Colonel M’Murdo, C.B.
- Colonel Chapman, C.B.
- Lieutenant-Colonel Ridley, C.B.
- Major the Hon. F. Keane.
- W. H. Russell, Esq. (*Times* Correspondent).
- W. T. Doyne, Esq.

===Chinese===
说真的，哪里没有我的朋友呢？在公共马车上，在河轮上，在公共游乐场里，在僻静的街巷和院落——抄近道时我常常在那儿迷路——一张张熟悉的老面孔会突然冒出来，让我想起斯普林希尔上的岁月。白厅（Whitehall）的哨兵，也从他们守着空无一物的重大职责里缓过神来，朝我投来相识的微笑；连报馆的门面，我走过时也显得友善；忙碌的印刷所院子（Printing-house Yard）堆起鼓舞人的笑意，舰队街（Fleet Street）的《笨拙》编辑部有时索性大笑出声。喏，倘若我是作为阔太太回英国的，这一切还会发生吗？断然不会。

收住这些自夸的话之前，再啰嗦几句。提到新近成立来资助我的委员会，我心里自然是又骄傲又欢喜；倘若我放纵这点虚荣，把他们的名字陈列在读者面前，那仅仅因为，下列每一位贵族和绅士都在克里米亚认识我，他们慨然允诺相助，即是公开记录下他们对我当年效劳的评断。而这件事，我还有另一层理由引以为豪：已有公开的说法道明，我今日的窘迫，正源于我对大军的慈善施与和不辍劳瘁。签名者如下：——

- 少将罗克比勋爵（Major-General Lord Rokeby, K.C.B.）
- 萨克森-魏玛的爱德华亲王殿下（H.S.H. Prince Edward of Saxe Weimar, C.B.）
- 威灵顿公爵阁下（His Grace the Duke of Wellington）
- 纽卡斯尔公爵阁下（His Grace the Duke of Newcastle）
- 沃德勋爵阁下（The Right Hon. Lord Ward）
- 上将约翰·伯戈因爵士（General Sir John Burgoyne, K.C.B.）
- 少将理查德·艾尔利爵士（Major-General Sir Richard Airey, K.C.B.）
- 海军少将斯蒂芬·卢欣顿爵士（Rear-Admiral Sir Stephen Lushington, K.C.B.）
- 麦克默多上校（Colonel M'Murdo, C.B.）
- 查普曼上校（Colonel Chapman, C.B.）
- 里德利中校（Lieutenant-Colonel Ridley, C.B.）
- 贵族 F. 基恩少校（Major the Hon. F. Keane）
- W. H. 拉塞尔先生（《泰晤士报》记者）
- W. T. 多因先生（W. T. Doyne）

===Original===
Dedicated, by permission, 

 to 

 **Major-General Lord Rokeby, K.C.B.,** 

 by his Lordship's 

 humble and most grateful servant,
Mary Seacole.

===Chinese===
谨经恩准，将本书献给

**罗克比勋爵少将（Major-General Lord Rokeby, K.C.B.）**

献书者为他勋爵大人

卑微而万分感激的仆人
玛丽·西科尔

## Endnotes / 尾注

===Original===
1. Mrs. Seacole very likely refers to Macbeth. But it was the witches he abused. —Ed. ↩︎
2. Subsequently I saw much of Miss Nightingale, at Balaclava. ↩︎

===Chinese===
1. 西科尔夫人所指极可能是《麦克白》（Macbeth）。但他咒骂的是那几个女巫。——编者 ↩︎
2. 后来在巴拉克拉瓦，我常常见到南丁格尔小姐。 ↩︎

## I / I

===Original===
My birth and parentage—Early tastes and travels—Marriage, and widowhood.

I was born in the town of Kingston, in the island of Jamaica, some time in the present century. As a female, and a widow, I may be well excused giving the precise date of this important event. But I do not mind confessing that the century and myself were both young together, and that we have grown side by side into age and consequence. I am a Creole, and have good Scotch blood coursing in my veins. My father was a soldier, of an old Scotch family; and to him I often trace my affection for a camp-life, and my sympathy with what I have heard my friends call "the pomp, pride, and circumstance of glorious war." Many people have also traced to my Scotch blood that energy and activity which are not always found in the Creole race, and which have carried me to so many varied scenes: and perhaps they are right. I have often heard the term "lazy Creole" applied to my country people; but I am sure I do not know what it is to be indolent. All my life long I have followed the impulse which led me to be up and doing; and so far from resting idle anywhere, I have never wanted inclination to rove, nor will powerful enough to find a way to carry out my wishes. That these qualities have led me into many countries, and brought me into some strange and amusing adventures, the reader, if he or she has the patience to get through this book, will see. Some people, indeed, have called me quite a female Ulysses. I believe that they intended it as a compliment; but from my experience of the Greeks, I do not consider it a very flattering one.

===Chinese===
我的出身与家世——早年趣味与游历——婚姻与孀居。

我出生在牙买加岛的金斯敦城，时间在本世纪的某个当口。身为一个女人，又是个寡妇，我不肯给出这件大事的确切日期，想必情有可原。不过我并不介意坦白：这个世纪和我本人当年都正当年轻，我们是并着肩一起长出岁数与身份来的。我是个克里奥尔人，血管里流着纯正的苏格兰血液。我父亲是个军人，出身古老的苏格兰世家；我常常把自己对军营生活的偏爱，以及对朋友们所说的「辉煌战争的排场、骄傲与煊赫」的共鸣，追溯到他的身上。也有许多人把我的那股干劲与活力归因于我的苏格兰血统——这种劲头在克里奥尔人当中并非总能见到，正是它带着我走过了这么多各不相同的场景：也许他们说对了。我常常听到人们用「懒惰的克里奥尔人」这个字眼称呼我的同胞；可我可以肯定，我全然不知怠惰是什么滋味。我这一生都听从那股催我起而行事的冲动；非但从未在哪儿安坐偷闲，我从来不缺四处闯荡的心气，也从来不缺足以找到门路实现心愿的意志。这些品性把我带进了许多国度，也让我卷入了一些离奇而有趣的奇遇——读者诸君若有耐心读完这本书，自会看到。的确，有些人干脆把我称作女版尤利西斯（Ulysses）。我相信他们是存心恭维；不过以我对希腊人的阅历，我可不觉得这是多么动听的恭维。

===Original===
It is not my intention to dwell at any length upon the recollections of my childhood. My mother kept a boardinghouse in Kingston, and was, like very many of the Creole women, an admirable doctress; in high repute with the officers of both services, and their wives, who were from time to time stationed at Kingston. It was very natural that I should inherit her tastes; and so I had from early youth a yearning for medical knowledge and practice which has never deserted me. When I was a very young child I was taken by an old lady, who brought me up in her household among her own grandchildren, and who could scarcely have shown me more kindness had I been one of them; indeed, I was so spoiled by my kind patroness that, but for being frequently with my mother, I might very likely have grown up idle and useless. But I saw so much of her, and of her patients, that the ambition to become a doctress early took firm root in my mind; and I was very young when I began to make use of the little knowledge I had acquired from watching my mother, upon a great sufferer—my doll. I have noticed always what actors children are. If you leave one alone in a room, how soon it clears a little stage; and, making an audience out of a few chairs and stools, proceeds to act its childish griefs and blandishments upon its doll. So I also made good use of my dumb companion and confidante; and whatever disease was most prevalent in Kingston, be sure my poor doll soon contracted it. I have had many medical triumphs in later days, and saved some valuable lives; but I really think that few have given me more real gratification than the rewarding glow of health which my fancy used to picture stealing over my patient's waxen face after long and precarious illness.

Before long it was very natural that I should seek to extend my practice; and so I found other patients in the dogs and cats around me. Many luckless brutes were made to simulate diseases which were raging among their owners, and had forced down their reluctant throats the remedies which I deemed most likely to suit their supposed complaints. And after a time I rose still higher in my ambition; and despairing of finding another human patient, I proceeded to try my simples and essences upon—myself.

===Chinese===
我不打算在童年回忆上多费笔墨。我母亲在金斯敦开着一间供膳寄宿公寓，而且和许许多多克里奥尔妇女一样，是位令人钦佩的女医士（doctress）；在时常轮驻金斯敦的陆、海两军军官及其太太们中间，声名极好。我继承她的志趣是再自然不过的事；于是从少年时代起，我就对医学知识与行医怀着一种渴望，这渴望从未离开过我。在我还是个很小的孩子时，一位老夫人把我领了过去，在她家里把我同她自己的孙儿孙女们一同抚养；即便我是她亲生的孙辈，她待我也不见得能更疼爱了。的确，我被这位好心的女恩主宠坏了，若不是经常陪伴在母亲身边，我很可能会长成一个游手好闲、百无一用的人。但我见她的面太多了，也见得太多她的病人，以至于当一名女医士的抱负早早在我心里扎下了根；年纪还很小的时候，我就开始把从观察母亲那里学来的一点知识，用在一位重病号身上——我的洋娃娃身上。我一向注意到，孩子们个个是天生的演员。只要让一个孩子独自待在房间里，瞧它多快便清出一个小舞台；拿几把椅子板凳权充观众，便对着洋娃娃演起它那孩子气的悲苦与撒娇来。于是我也好好利用了我这位不会说话的伙伴和知心人；金斯敦流行什么病，放心，我那可怜的洋娃娃很快就染上什么病。后来的岁月里，我取得过许多医学上的胜利，也救过一些宝贵的生命；但我真心认为，很少有什么能比得上那种快慰——我想象着，在漫长而凶险的病痛之后，健康的红晕悄悄爬上我那病人蜡黄的脸庞。

没过多久，我很自然地想要扩大我的行医范围；于是我就在周围的猫狗身上找到了别的病人。许多倒霉的畜生被迫装出它们主人中间正闹得凶的病症，还被掰开不甘不愿的喉咙，硬灌下我认定最对它们假想症候的药剂。又过了一阵子，我的雄心更上了一层；眼看再找不到别的人类病人，我便着手把我那些草药和药露试用到——我自己身上。

===Original===
When I was about twelve years old I was more frequently at my mother's house, and used to assist her in her duties; very often sharing with her the task of attending upon invalid officers or their wives, who came to her house from the adjacent camp at Up-Park, or the military station at Newcastle.

As I grew into womanhood, I began to indulge that longing to travel which will never leave me while I have health and vigour. I was never weary of tracing upon an old map the route to England; and never followed with my gaze the stately ships homeward bound without longing to be in them, and see the blue hills of Jamaica fade into the distance. At that time it seemed most improbable that these girlish wishes should be gratified; but circumstances, which I need not explain, enabled me to accompany some relatives to England while I was yet a very young woman.

===Chinese===
到我大约 12 岁的时候，我更常住在母亲家里，开始帮她料理事务；常常同她一道照顾前来求医的患病军官或军官太太，他们从邻近的上帕克军营（Up-Park）或纽卡斯尔（Newcastle）军事站来到她的家里。

待我长成少女，便开始纵容那种游历的渴望——只要我还有健康与精力，这渴望便不会离开我。我从不厌倦在一张旧地图上描画去英格兰的路线；每当目送那些驶向故国的巍巍海船，无不想置身船上，亲眼看牙买加的青山渐渐隐没在远方。在那时，这些少女心愿要得到满足，似乎是最不可能的事；但一些无须我解释的机缘，使我在年纪还很轻的时候，便随几位亲戚去了英格兰。

===Original===
I shall never forget my first impressions of London. Of course, I am not going to bore the reader with them; but they are as vivid now as though the year 18— (I had very nearly let my age slip then) had not been long ago numbered with the past. Strangely enough, some of the most vivid of my recollections are the efforts of the London street-boys to poke fun at my and my companion's complexion. I am only a little brown—a few shades duskier than the brunettes whom you all admire so much; but my companion was very dark, and a fair (if I can apply the term to her) subject for their rude wit. She was hot-tempered, poor thing! and as there were no policemen to awe the boys and turn our servants' heads in those days, our progress through the London streets was sometimes a rather chequered one.

I remained in England, upon the occasion of my first visit, about a year; and then returned to Kingston. Before long I again started for London, bringing with me this time a large stock of West Indian preserves and pickles for sale. After remaining two years here, I again started home; and on the way my life and adventures were very nearly brought to a premature conclusion. Christmas-day had been kept very merrily on board our ship the *Velusia*; and on the following day a fire broke out in the hold. I dare say it would have resisted all the crew's efforts to put it out, had not another ship appeared in sight; upon which the fire quietly allowed itself to be extinguished. Although considerably alarmed, I did not lose my senses; but during the time when the contest between fire and water was doubtful, I entered into an amicable arrangement with the ship's cook, whereby, in consideration of two pounds—which I was not, however, to pay until the crisis arrived—he agreed to lash me on to a large hen-coop.

===Chinese===
我永远不会忘记我对伦敦的第一印象。当然，我不打算拿这些来烦读者；可它们至今仍栩栩如生，仿佛「18——年」（那一下我差点儿把自己的年龄漏了出去）还不曾早早归入逝去的往昔。说来奇怪，我记忆中最鲜明的片段之一，竟是伦敦街头顽童拿我和同伴的肤色取乐的种种花样。我不过肤色微棕——比你们人人倾慕的深肤色美人深那么几色；可我的同伴肤色很黑，正是他们粗鲁笑谑的上好（如果这个词能用在她身上）靶子。她是个急性子，可怜的人！那时候又没有警察来震慑顽童、搅乱我们仆人的方寸，我们穿行伦敦街头，行进有时颇为一波三折。

我第一次访问英格兰，待了大约一年，随后返回金斯敦。没过多久我又动身去伦敦，这次随行带了一大宗西印度蜜饯和腌菜，打算出售。在那里待了两年之后，我再度启程回家；而这一路上，我的生命和奇遇险些提前画上句号。我们那艘「维卢西娅」号（Velusia）船上的圣诞节过得非常欢畅；第二天底舱便起了火。我敢说，若不是另一艘船出现在视野里，那场火本来会顶住全体船员的扑救——那船一出现，火便乖乖就范，听凭自己被扑灭了。我虽然相当惊惶，却没有乱了方寸；在水火之争胜负未分的当口，我同船上的厨师谈成了一笔友好的交易：作为两镑钱的酬谢——不过说好了，非到危急关头我不必付钱——他同意把我捆缚在一只大鸡笼上。

===Original===
Before I had been long in Jamaica I started upon other trips, many of them undertaken with a view to gain. Thus I spent some time in New Providence, bringing home with me a large collection of handsome shells and rare shell-work, which created quite a sensation in Kingston, and had a rapid sale; I visited also Haiti and Cuba. But I hasten onward in my narrative.

Returned to Kingston, I nursed my old indulgent patroness in her last long illness. After she died, in my arms, I went to my mother's house, where I stayed, making myself useful in a variety of ways, and learning a great deal of Creole medicinal art, until I couldn't find courage to say "no" to a certain arrangement timidly proposed by Mr. Seacole, but married him, and took him down to Black River, where we established a store. Poor man! he was very delicate; and before I undertook the charge of him, several doctors had expressed most unfavourable opinions of his health. I kept him alive by kind nursing and attention as long as I could; but at last he grew so ill that we left Black River, and returned to my mother's house at Kingston. Within a month of our arrival there he died. This was my first great trouble, and I felt it bitterly. For days I never stirred—lost to all that passed around me in a dull stupor of despair. If you had told me that the time would soon come when I should remember this sorrow calmly, I should not have believed it possible: and yet it was so. I do not think that we hot-blooded Creoles sorrow less for showing it so impetuously; but I do think that the sharp edge of our grief wears down sooner than theirs who preserve an outward demeanour of calmness, and nurse their woe secretly in their hearts.

===Chinese===
在牙买加没住多久，我又踏上了别的旅程，其中不少是抱着牟利的打算。就这样，我在新普罗维登斯（New Providence）盘桓了一阵，带回家一大批漂亮的贝壳和珍稀的贝雕工艺品，在金斯敦轰动一时，销路极快；我还到过海地和古巴。不过，我得赶紧往下讲了。

回到金斯敦后，在那位从小娇惯我的老恩主最后缠绵病榻时，我护理了她。她在我的怀抱中去世之后，我搬进母亲家住下，在各方面帮着做事，也学到了许多克里奥尔医术；直到西科尔先生怯生生地提出某项安排时，我再也鼓不起勇气说「不」，便嫁给了他，带着他去了黑河镇（Black River），我们在那里开了一爿店铺。可怜的人！他身子一向孱弱；在我接手照看他之前，好几位医生都对他的健康下过极为不妙的断语。我凭着温柔的护理和悉心的照料，尽可能长久地延续他的生命；但最后他病得实在沉重，我们只得离开黑河镇，回到金斯敦我母亲家。到那里不满一个月，他便去世了。这是我平生第一场大恸，我痛彻心扉。一连数日我浑然不动——在对绝望的麻木恍惚之中，身边发生的一切于我恍如不存在。倘若当时有人告诉我，不久之后我就能平静地回忆这场悲痛，我是绝不会相信有此可能的：然而事实如此。我并不认为，我们这些热血的克里奥尔人把悲痛发泄得那样汹涌，便痛得少了一分；但我确实认为，比起那些外表保持镇定、把哀伤暗暗捂在心里的人，我们悲痛的锋刃磨钝得更快。

## II / II

===Original===
Struggles for life—The cholera in Jamaica—I leave Kingston for the Isthmus of Panama—Chagres, Navy Bay, and Gatun—Life in Panama—Up the river Chagres to Gorgona and Cruces.

I had one other great grief to master—the loss of my mother, and then I was left alone to battle with the world as best I might. The struggles which it cost me to succeed in life were sometimes very trying; nor have they ended yet. But I have always turned a bold front to fortune, and taken, and shall continue to take, as my brave friends in the army and navy have shown me how, "my hurts before." Although it was no easy thing for a widow to make ends meet, I never allowed myself to know what repining or depression was, and so succeeded in gaining not only my daily bread, but many comforts besides from the beginning. Indeed, my experience of the world—it is not finished yet, but I do not think it will give me reason to change my opinion—leads me to the conclusion that it is by no means the hard bad world which some selfish people would have us believe it. It may be as my editor says—

> "That gently comes the world to those
> That are cast in gentle mould;"

hinting at the same time, politely, that the rule may apply to me personally. And perhaps he is right, for although I was always a hearty, strong woman—plainspoken people might say stout—I think my heart is soft enough.

How slowly and gradually I succeeded in life, need not be told at length. My fortunes underwent the variations which befall all. Sometimes I was rich one day, and poor the next. I never thought too exclusively of money, believing rather that we were born to be happy, and that the surest way to be wretched is to prize it overmuch. Had I done so, I should have mourned over many a promising speculation proving a failure, over many a pan of preserves or guava jelly burnt in the making; and perhaps lost my mind when the great fire of 1843, which devastated Kingston, burnt down my poor home. As it was, I very nearly lost my life, for I would not leave my house until every chance of saving it had gone, and it was wrapped in flames. But, of course, I set to work again in a humbler way, and rebuilt my house by degrees, and restocked it, succeeding better than before; for I had gained a reputation as a skilful nurse and doctress, and my house was always full of invalid officers and their wives from Newcastle, or the adjacent Up-Park Camp. Sometimes I had a naval or military surgeon under my roof, from whom I never failed to glean instruction, given, when they learned my love for their profession, with a readiness and kindness I am never likely to forget. Many of these kind friends are alive now. I met with some when my adventures had carried me to the battlefields of the Crimea; and to those whose eyes may rest upon these pages I again offer my acknowledgments for their past kindness, which helped me to be useful to my kind in many lands.

===Chinese===
生存挣扎——牙买加的霍乱——我离开金斯敦前往巴拿马地峡——查格雷斯、海军湾与加通——巴拿马的生活——沿查格雷斯河上溯至戈尔戈纳与克鲁塞斯。

我还有一场大恸需要战胜——母亲的离世；此后便只剩我独自一人，尽己所能与世道周旋。立身处世的一场场挣扎，有时十分磨人；而且至今尚未了结。但我向来都把一副大胆的面孔转向命运，并且正如我勇敢的陆海军朋友们示范的那样，过去一向、今后也要继续把「伤疤亮在前头」。一个寡妇要收支相抵本非易事，但我从不允许自己尝到怨叹或消沉的滋味，所以从一开始便不仅挣得了每日的面包，还挣得了许多额外的舒适。的确，我对这个世界的阅历——它还没有完结，但我认为它不会给我改变看法的理由——引我得出这样的结论：这世界决不是某些自私之人硬要我们相信的那个冷酷的坏世界。或许正像我的编辑说的那样——

> 「凡是天生温厚性子的人，
> 世界待他们也温和；」

同时他还客气地暗示，这条规律或许正适用于我本人。也许他说得对，因为尽管我一向是个精神饱满、身板结实的女人——心直口快的人可能会说胖——我想我的心肠还是够软的。

我在世上如何一步一步慢慢立足，无须细细道来。我的财运经历了人人难免的起起落落。有时今天还富有，明天就贫穷。我从不把心思过多地放在钱上，倒宁愿相信我们生来是为了幸福的，而通往愁苦最稳当的一条路，莫过于把钱看得太重。倘若我果真如此，我早该为许多前景大好的买卖折戟而哀叹，为许多锅熬糊了的蜜饯或番石榴果冻而悲鸣；1843 年那场蹂躏金斯敦的大火烧掉我可怜的家当时，说不定人就疯了。而实际上，我差点连性命都不保，因为非到挽救无望、房子陷入火海，我不肯离开。不过，我自然又以更低微的姿态从头干起，把房子一点点重建起来，重新置办了家什，日子比先前还红火；因为我已挣得了一个手艺高明的护士兼女医士的名声，家里总是住满了从纽卡斯尔或邻近的上帕克军营来的患病军官和他们的太太。我家里时而也住着海军或陆军的军医，我从不放过从他们那里汲取教益的机会；待他们得知我对他们这一行的热爱，传授起来那份爽快和友善，我一辈子也忘不了。这些好心朋友中有许多如今还健在。当我的奇遇把我带到克里米亚战场时，我又同其中几位重逢；倘若有哪位的目光落到这几页纸上，我要再次为他们的旧日恩谊致谢——正是那些恩谊，帮助我在许多地方为我的同胞派上了用场。

===Original===
And here I may take the opportunity of explaining that it was from a confidence in my own powers, and not at all from necessity, that I remained an unprotected female. Indeed, I do not mind confessing to my reader, in a friendly confidential way, that one of the hardest struggles of my life in Kingston was to resist the pressing candidates for the late Mr. Seacole's shoes.

Officers of high rank sometimes took up their abode in my house. Others of inferior rank were familiar with me, long before their bravery, and, alas! too often death, in the Crimea, made them world famous. There were few officers of the 97th to whom Mother Seacole was not well known, before she joined them in front of Sebastopol; and among the best known was good-hearted, loveable, noble H—— V——, whose death shocked me so terribly, and with whose useful heroic life the English public have become so familiar. I can hear the ring of his boyish laughter even now.

In the year 1850, the cholera swept over the island of Jamaica with terrible force. Our idea—perhaps an unfounded one—was, that a steamer from New Orleans was the means of introducing it into the island. Anyhow, they sent some clothes on shore to be washed, and poor Dolly Johnson, the washerwoman, whom we all knew, sickened and died of the terrible disease. While the cholera raged, I had but too many opportunities of watching its nature, and from a Dr. B——, who was then lodging in my house, received many hints as to its treatment which I afterwards found invaluable.

===Chinese===
在这里我不妨借机说明：我之所以一直做个无人护佑的女人，是出于对自身能力的自信，而绝非出于生计所迫。的确，我不介意用友爱知心的口吻向读者坦白：我在金斯敦平生最艰难的斗争之一，就是抵挡那些急切想填补已故西科尔先生空缺的候选人。

高级军官有时会在我家下榻。级别低些的军官同我很熟稔——早在他们的勇武，唉，还常常是他们的死讯！在克里米亚使他们名扬世界之前。第 97 团的军官里，几乎没有谁在西科尔妈妈奔赴塞瓦斯托波尔城下与他们汇合之前不熟知她；其中最熟识的，莫过于好心肠、招人疼、品格高贵的 H—— V——，他的死讯曾令我大为震恸，他有益而英勇的一生，英国公众也已耳熟能详。直到现在，我还能听见他那少年般爽朗笑声的清越回响。

1850 年，霍乱以可怕之势席卷牙买加岛。我们当时的想法——也许并无根据——是，一艘来自新奥尔良（New Orleans）的轮船把瘟疫带进了岛。总之，船上的人送了一些衣服上岸浆洗，我们大家都认识的洗衣妇、可怜的多莉·约翰逊（Dolly Johnson）便染上了病，死于这种可怕的疾病。霍乱肆虐期间，我观察其特性的机会多得叫人受不了；而从当时寄住在我家的 B——医生（Dr. B——）那里，我得到许多治疗方面的指点，日后证明无比宝贵。

===Original===
Early in the same year my brother had left Kingston for the Isthmus of Panama, then the great highroad to and from golden California, where he had established a considerable store and hotel. Ever since he had done so, I had found some difficulty in checking my reviving disposition to roam, and at last persuading myself that I might be of use to him (he was far from strong), I resigned my house into the hands of a cousin, and made arrangements to journey to Chagres. Having come to this conclusion, I allowed no grass to grow beneath my feet, but set to work busily, for I was not going to him empty-handed. My house was full for weeks, of tailors, making up rough coats, trousers, etc., and sempstresses cutting out and making shirts. In addition to these, my kitchen was filled with busy people, manufacturing preserves, guava jelly, and other delicacies, while a considerable sum was invested in the purchase of preserved meats, vegetables, and eggs. It will be as well, perhaps, if I explain, in as few words as possible, the then condition of the Isthmus of Panama.

All my readers must know—a glance at the map will show it to those who do not—that between North America and the envied shores of California stretches a little neck of land, insignificant-looking enough on the map, dividing the Atlantic from the Pacific. By crossing this, the travellers from America avoided a long, weary, and dangerous sea voyage round Cape Horn, or an almost impossible journey by land.

===Chinese===
同年年初，我兄弟离开金斯敦去了巴拿马地峡——当时通往和离开黄金加州的大驿道——在那里开起了颇具规模的店铺兼旅馆。自打他去了之后，我便发现难以按捺那重新抬头的好游历的性子；终于，我说服自己或许能帮上他的忙（他身子远说不上结实），便把房子交到一位表亲手里，张罗着动身去查格雷斯。既已拿定主意，我便脚下生风、不容寸草在脚底生长，立刻忙碌起来，因为我可不打算空着手去见他。一连几个星期，我家里坐满了裁缝，缝制粗呢外套、长裤等等，还有女裁缝裁布做衬衫。除此之外，我的厨房里也挤满了忙碌的人，制作蜜饯、番石榴果冻和各色精美吃食；同时又花了一大笔钱采购罐装肉食、蔬菜和鸡蛋。兴许我最好还是用尽量简少的几句话，说明一下当年巴拿马地峡的情形。

我的读者想必都知道——不知道的，看一眼地图便知——在北美与令人艳羡的加州海岸之间，伸展着一小条地颈，在地图上看起来毫不起眼，却把大西洋和太平洋分隔开来。美国的旅行者只要越过这条地颈，便可以免去绕行合恩角（Cape Horn）的漫长、疲惫而危险的海路，或是走一条几乎走不通的陆路。

===Original===
But that journey across the Isthmus, insignificant in distance as it was, was by no means an easy one. It seemed as if nature had determined to throw every conceivable obstacle in the way of those who should seek to join the two great oceans of the world. I have read and heard many accounts of old endeavours to effect this important and gigantic work, and how miserably they failed. It was reserved for the men of our age to accomplish what so many had died in attempting, and iron and steam, twin giants, subdued to man's will, have put a girdle over rocks and rivers, so that travellers can glide as smoothly, if not as inexpensively, over the once terrible Isthmus of Darien, as they can from London to Brighton. Not yet, however, does civilization rule at Panama. The weak sway of the New Granada Republic, despised by lawless men, and respected by none, is powerless to control the refuse of every nation which meet together upon its soil. Whenever they feel inclined now they overpower the law easily; but seven years ago, when I visited the Isthmus of Panama, things were much worse, and a licence existed, compared to which the present lawless state of affairs is enviable.

When, after passing Chagres, an old-world, tumbledwn town, for about seven miles, the steamer reached Navy Bay, I thought I had never seen a more luckless, dreary spot. Three sides of the place were a mere swamp, and the town itself stood upon a sand-reef, the houses being built upon piles, which someone told me rotted regularly every three years. The railway, which now connects the bay with Panama, was then building, and ran, as far as we could see, on piles, connected with the town by a wooden jetty. It seemed as capital a nursery for ague and fever as Death could hit upon anywhere, and those on board the steamer who knew it confirmed my opinion. As we arrived a steady downpour of rain was falling from an inky sky; the white men who met us on the wharf appeared ghostly and wraithlike, and the very negroes seemed pale and wan. The news which met us did not tempt me to lose any time in getting up the country to my brother. According to all accounts, fever and ague, with some minor diseases, especially dropsy, were having it all their own way at Navy Bay, and, although I only stayed one night in the place, my medicine chest was called into requisition. But the sufferers wanted remedies which I could not give them—warmth, nourishment, and fresh air. Beneath leaky tents, damp huts, and even under broken railway wagons, I saw men dying from sheer exhaustion. Indeed, I was very glad when, with the morning, the crowd, as the Yankees called the bands of pilgrims to and from California, made ready to ascend to Panama.

===Chinese===
然而，横穿地峡这趟旅程，里程虽不起眼，却决不轻松。大自然似乎打定了主意，要给任何试图连通世界两大洋的人设置一切想得到的障碍。关于早年完成这项重大而浩大的工程的种种尝试，以及它们如何惨败，我读过也听过许多记述。这桩无数人为之丧命的伟业，注定要留给我们这个时代的人来完成；钢铁与蒸汽这对孪生巨人，一经驯服于人的意志，便在岩石与河流之上束起了一条腰带，于是旅行者行经昔日令人生畏的达连地峡（Isthmus of Darien），可以像从伦敦到布莱顿一样顺畅——只是未必同样便宜。然而，文明尚未统治巴拿马。新格拉纳达共和国的孱弱管辖，被不法之徒藐视，也无人敬重，根本无力约束各国汇聚于其国土上的人渣。如今他们只要愿意，随时能轻易压倒法律；但七年之前，当我踏访巴拿马地峡时，情形还要糟得多，那时的肆行无忌，比起当今的无政府状态来，简直叫人艳羡。

轮船驶过查格雷斯——一座古旧破败的小镇——约七英里之后，便到了海军湾。我当时想，从没见过比这更倒霉、更凄凉的地方。这地方三面皆是沼泽，镇子本身坐落在一片沙洲上，房子建在木桩上——有人告诉我，那些木桩每三年准烂一回。如今连接海湾与巴拿马的铁路，当时正在修建，就我们目力所及，都架在木桩上，用一座木栈桥同镇子相连。这地方简直是死神在任何地方都挑得出来的头等疟疾与热病温床，轮船上认得此地的人也证实了我的看法。我们抵达时，墨黑的天空正下着连绵的倾盆大雨；到码头迎接我们的白人个个鬼影幢幢、形同幽魂，连黑人看上去都苍白憔悴。迎接我们的种种消息，让我一分钟也不想耽搁，只想赶紧深入内陆去见兄弟。据各方说法，热病与疟疾，外加若干小病，尤其是水肿病，正在海军湾横行无忌；虽然我只在此地待了一夜，我的药箱就被征用了。可病人们需要的是我给不了的药——温暖、滋养和新鲜空气。在漏雨的帐篷下、潮湿的窝棚里，甚至破损的铁路车厢底下，我都见到有人因纯粹的心力衰竭而奄奄一息。说真的，等到清晨来临，那群被北方佬称作「人潮」的往返加州的朝圣队伍准备登程上巴拿马去时，我心里高兴极了。

===Original===
The first stage of our journey was by railway to Gatun, about twelve miles distant. For the greater portion of that distance the lines ran on piles, over as unhealthy and wretched a country as the eye could well grow weary of; but, at last, the country improved, and you caught glimpses of distant hills and English-like scenery. Every mile of that fatal railway cost the world thousands of lives. I was assured that its site was marked thickly by graves, and that so great was the mortality among the labourers that three times the survivors struck in a body, and their places had to be supplied by fresh victims from America, tempted by unheard-of rates of wages. It is a gigantic undertaking, and shows what the energy and enterprise of man can accomplish. Everything requisite for its construction, even the timber, had to be prepared in, and brought from, America.

The railway then ran no further than Gatun, and here we were to take water and ascend the River Chagres to Gorgona, the next stage on the way to Cruces, where my brother was. The cars landed us at the bottom of a somewhat steep cutting through a reddish clay, and deposited me and my suite, consisting of a black servant, named "Mac," and a little girl, in safety in the midst of my many packages, not altogether satisfied with my prospects; for the rain was falling heavily and steadily, and the Gatun porters were possessing themselves of my luggage with that same avidity which distinguishes their brethren on the pier of Calais or the quays of Pera. There are two species of individuals whom I have found alike wherever my travels have carried me—the reader can guess their professions—porters and lawyers.

===Chinese===
我们旅程的第一段是乘火车到加通（Gatun），大约 12 英里远。这一路大半程，铁轨都架在木桩上，穿行于一片令眼睛都要看厌的瘴疠贫瘠之乡；但到最后，乡野渐渐像样起来，可以望见远山的剪影和英格兰式的风光。那条要命的铁路，每一英里都让世界付出几千条人命。有人向我保证说，沿线路基被坟茔密密标记，劳工死亡率之高，使得幸存者曾三次集体罢工，空出的位置只能由从美国新招来的受害者填补——他们是被闻所未闻的高工资诱来的。这是一项浩大的工程，显示了人的干劲与进取精神能成就什么。修路所需的一切，连木料都得在美国备好再运来。

当时的铁路只通到加通，我们要在这里改走水路，沿查格雷斯河上溯到戈尔戈纳——去克鲁塞斯途中的一站，我兄弟就在克鲁塞斯。车厢把我们卸在一道红黏土陡坡路堑的底下，把我和我的随行——一名叫做「麦克」（Mac）的黑人仆人和一个小女孩——连同我那一大堆行李安全地撂在那里；我对前景谈不上满意：雨下得又大又急，而加通的搬运工正以他们加来（Calais）码头或佩拉（Pera）港岸同行的那股贪婪劲头，抢占着我的行李。世上有两类人，我发现无论旅行把我带到哪里，他们都一个样——读者想必猜得到他们的行当——搬运工和律师。

===Original===
It was as much as I could do to gather my packages together, sit in the midst with a determined look to awe the hungry crowd around me, and send "Mac" up the steep slippery bank to report progress. After a little while he returned to say that the riverside was not far off, where boats could be hired for the upward journey. The word given, the porters threw themselves upon my packages; a pitched battle ensued, out of which issued the strongest Spanish Indians, with their hardly earned prizes, and we commenced the ascent of the clayey bank. Now, although the surveyors of the Darien highways had considerately cut steps up the steep incline, they had become worse than useless, so I floundered about terribly, more than once losing my footing altogether. And as with that due regard to personal appearance, which I have always deemed a duty as well as a pleasure to study, I had, before leaving Navy Bay, attired myself in a delicate light blue dress, a white bonnet prettily trimmed, and an equally chaste shawl, the reader can sympathise with my distress. However, I gained the summit, and after an arduous descent, of a few minutes duration, reached the riverside; in a most piteous plight, however, for my pretty dress, from its contact with the Gatun clay, looked as red as if, in the pursuit of science, I had passed it through a strong solution of muriatic acid.

By the waterside I found my travelling companions arguing angrily with the shrewd boatmen, and bating down their fares. Upon collecting my luggage, I found, as I had expected, that the porters had not neglected the glorious opportunity of robbing a woman, and that several articles were missing. Complaints, I knew, would not avail me, and stronger measures seemed hazardous and barely advisable in a lawless out-of-the-way spot, where

> "The simple plan,
> That they should take who have the power,
> And they should keep who can,"

seemed universally practised, and would very likely have been defended by its practitioners upon principle.

===Chinese===
我使出浑身解数，才把行李归拢到一处，自己坐在中间，摆出一副坚决的面色镇住四周那群饥肠辘辘的人，再派「麦克」爬上又陡又滑的河岸去探路。过了一会儿他回来说，河边不远，可以雇到上行的船。一声令下，搬运工们便朝我的行李扑去；一场混战随之而起，从战团里杀出来的，是几个最壮实的西班牙印第安人，攥着他们来之不易的战利品，我们便开始攀登那道黏土坡。虽说达连公路的勘测员们体贴地在陡坡上凿出了台阶，可这些台阶早已破败得不如没有，于是我跌跌撞撞、狼狈不堪，不止一次整个人滑倒。而由于我向来把讲究仪容当作一门功课——我素来视之为义务，也是乐趣——离开海军湾之前，我特意穿上了素雅的浅蓝色连衣裙，戴上了缀饰秀美的白色软帽，还披了一条同样素净的披肩，读者诸君想必能体谅我的窘苦。不过，我总算登上了坡顶，又艰难地下行了几分钟，到了河边；然而模样实在可怜，我那条漂亮的裙子，因为沾了加通的黏土，红得就像我为追求科学、把它泡进了浓盐酸（muriatic acid）溶液里一般。

在河边，我看见同行的旅伴们正同精明的船夫怒气冲冲地争吵，压他们的船价。把行李收拢清点之后，我发现不出所料，搬运工们没有错失打劫一个女人这天赐良机，有好几件东西不翼而飞了。我知道，抱怨无济于事，而更激烈的手段，在这无法无天的偏僻角落，又似乎凶险而未必可取——在这里，

> 「道理很简单：
> 有力量的就去拿，
> 守得住的才能留。」

似乎人人奉为通则，而且它的实践者们多半还会本着原则起而为它辩护。

===Original===
It was not so easy to hire a boat as I had been led to expect. The large crowd had made the boatmen somewhat exorbitant in their demands, and there were several reasons why I should engage one for my own exclusive use, instead of sharing one with some of my travelling companions. In the first place, my luggage was somewhat bulky; and, in the second place, my experience of travel had not failed to teach me that Americans (even from the Northern States) are always uncomfortable in the company of coloured people, and very often show this feeling in stronger ways than by sour looks and rude words. I think, if I have a little prejudice against our cousins across the Atlantic—and I do confess to a little—it is not unreasonable. I have a few shades of deeper brown upon my skin which shows me related—and I am proud of the relationship—to those poor mortals whom you once held enslaved, and whose bodies America still owns. And having this bond, and knowing what slavery is; having seen with my eyes and heard with my ears proof positive enough of its horrors—let others affect to doubt them if they will—is it surprising that I should be somewhat impatient of the airs of superiority which many Americans have endeavoured to assume over me? Mind, I am not speaking of all. I have met with some delightful exceptions.

At length I succeeded in hiring a boat for the modest consideration of ten pounds, to carry me and my fortunes to Cruces. My boat was far from uncomfortable. Large and flat-bottomed, with an awning, dirty it must be confessed, beneath which swung a hammock, of which I took immediate possession. By the way, the Central Americans should adopt the hammock as their national badge; but for sheer necessity they would never leave it. The master of the boat, the padrone, was a fine tall negro, his crew were four common enough specimens of humanity, with a marked disregard of the prejudices of society with respect to clothing. A dirty handkerchief rolled over the head, and a wisp of something, which might have been linen, bound round the loins, formed their attire. Perhaps, however, the thick coating of dirt which covered them kept them warmer than more civilized clothing, besides being indisputably more economical.

===Chinese===
雇船并不像别人让我料想的那样容易。大批人马把船夫的要价抬得有些离谱；而且有几条理由，让我应当独包一条船，而不是同旅伴合乘。第一，我的行李颇为笨重；第二，我的旅行经验不曾忘记教我：美国人（哪怕是北方各州的）同有色人种做伴时总是浑身不自在，而且这份感觉，常常不止用甩脸子和恶言恶语来发泄。我想，倘若我对大西洋对岸的表亲们心存一点偏见——我也确实承认有一点——那也并非无理取闹。我皮肤上那多出来的几重棕色，表明我同那些苦命人有亲——你们曾经奴役过他们，而美利坚至今仍占有他们的躯体——我为这门亲缘感到骄傲。既有这层纽带，又深知奴隶制是什么滋味；亲眼见过、亲耳听过足以坐实其恐怖的证据——别人要装作怀疑就随他们去——那么，许多美国人硬要在我面前摆出一副居高临下的架子，我对他们有点按捺不住火气，这难道奇怪吗？请注意，我说的不是全体。我也遇到过几个令人愉快的例外。

最后，我以十镑这个「厚道」的价钱雇到了一条船，载着我和我的全部身家去克鲁塞斯。我这条船坐起来倒一点也不难受。船身宽大、船底平阔，带一顶遮篷——得承认，脏是脏了点——篷下晃着一张吊床，我立刻将它据为己有。顺便说一句，中美洲人真该把吊床定为国徽；要不是出于纯粹的需要，他们绝不肯离开它。船的主人，即船主（padrone），是个高大出色的黑人；他的船员是四个再普通不过的人类标本，对世人关于衣着的成见全然不屑一顾。脑袋上缠一条脏手帕，腰间围一缕说不上是亚麻还是什么的东西，便是他们的全部行头。不过，也许他们周身那层厚厚的泥垢，比文明的衣裳更保暖，何况还无可争议地更省钱。

===Original===
The boat was generally propelled by paddles, but when the river was shallow, poles were used to punt us along, as on English rivers; the black padrone, whose superior position was indicated by the use of decent clothing, standing at the helm, gesticulating wildly, and swearing Spanish oaths with a vehemence that would have put Corporal Trim's comrades in Flanders to the blush. Very much shocked, of course, but finding it perfectly useless to remonstrate with him, I swung myself in my hammock and leisurely watched the river scene.

The river Chagres lolled with considerable force, now between low marshy shores, now narrowing, between steep, thickly wooded banks. It was liable, as are all rivers in hilly districts, to sudden and heavy floods; and although the padrone, on leaving Gatun, had pledged his soul to land me at Cruces that night, I had not been long afloat before I saw that he would forfeit his worthless pledge; for the wind rose to a gale, ruffling the river here and there into a little sea; the rain came down in torrents, while the river rose rapidly, bearing down on its swollen stream trunks of trees, and similar waifs and strays, which it tossed about like a giant in sport, threatening to snag us with its playthings every moment. And when we came to a sheltered reach, and found that the little fleet of boats which had preceded us had laid to there, I came to the conclusion that, stiff, tired, and hungry, I should have to pass a night upon the river Chagres. All I could get to eat was some guavas, which grew wild upon the banks, and then I watched the padrone curl his long body up among my luggage, and listened to the crew, who had rolled together at the bottom of the boat, snore as peacefully as if they slept between fair linen sheets, in the purest of calico night-gear, and the most unexceptionable of nightcaps, until somehow I fell into a troubled, dreamy sleep.

===Chinese===
船一般用桨划行，但河水浅时，就用篙撑船前进，同英国的河上一样；黑人船主——他身居高位的标志，是身上穿得还算体面——立在舵边，狂热地打着手势，用一股足以让佛兰德斯的特里姆下士（Corporal Trim）的战友们脸红的劲头，起着西班牙语的咒誓。我自然大为骇然，但发现同他讲道理全然无用，便自顾自荡进吊床，悠闲地观赏起河上的风光。

查格雷斯河翻滚着相当有力的浊流，一时流过低洼的沼泽岸，一时又在陡峭、林木繁茂的河岸间收窄。同丘陵地带所有的河流一样，它动辄暴涨成灾；离开加通时，船主虽把灵魂都押了上去，担保当晚送我到克鲁塞斯，可我上船没多久就看出，他那份不值钱的誓约注定要落空：风升为烈风，把河面这里那里吹皱成一片小海；大雨倾盆而下，河水迅猛上涨，膨胀的水流裹挟着树干之类的水上漂泊物冲将下来，把它们像个嬉戏的巨人似的抛来掷去，随时威胁着要拿它这些玩意向我们戳上一记。等我们驶进一处避风的河湾，发现先行的小船队都已停泊在那里，我便得出结论：浑身僵冷、又累又饿的我，只好在查格雷斯河上过一夜了。我能弄到嘴的吃食，只有岸边野生的一些番石榴；然后我看着船主把他那长长的身子蜷进我的行李堆里，听着蜷在船底的船员们鼾声大作，睡得那般安详，仿佛身下是光洁的亚麻床单，身穿最纯正的花布睡衣，头戴最无可挑剔的睡帽——不知怎么，我便坠入了一场烦乱恍惚的睡眠。

===Original===
At daybreak we were enabled to pursue our journey, and in a short time reached Gorgona. I was glad enough to go on shore, as you may imagine. Gorgona was a mere temporary town of bamboo and wood houses, hastily erected to serve as a station for the crowd. In the present rainy season, when the river was navigable up to Cruces, the chief part of the population migrated thither, so that Gorgona was almost deserted, and looked indescribably damp, dirty, and dull. With some difficulty I found a bakery and a butcher's shop. The meat was not very tempting, for the Gorgona butchers did not trouble themselves about joints, but cut the flesh into strips about three inches wide, and of various lengths. These were hung upon rails, so that you bought your meat by the yard, and were spared any difficulty in the choice of joint. I cannot say that I was favourably impressed with this novel and simple way of avoiding trouble, but I was far too hungry to be particular, and buying a strip for a quarter of a real, carried it off to Mac to cook.

Late that afternoon, the padrone and his crew landed me, tired, wretched, and out of temper, upon the miserable wharf of Cruces.

===Chinese===
破晓时分，我们得以继续赶路，不多时便到了戈尔戈纳。可以想见，我巴不得赶紧上岸。戈尔戈纳不过是一座临时城镇，尽是竹屋木舍，是为给「人潮」充当中途站而匆匆搭起来的。眼下正值雨季，河一直可以通航到克鲁塞斯，人口的大半都迁去了那边，于是戈尔戈纳几乎人去城空，看上去湿得、脏得、闷得难以形容。我费了些周折才找到一家面包铺和一家肉铺。那肉实在不大诱人，因为戈尔戈纳的屠夫懒得讲究什么部位，只把肉切成约三英寸宽、长短不一的条条。这些肉条挂在横杆上，所以你买肉论码算钱，倒也省去了挑选部位的种种为难。我不能说自己对这种省事的崭新简便方式有什么好印象，可我实在饿得顾不上挑剔，花四分之一个里亚尔（real）买了一条，拿去让麦克做了吃。

那天傍晚迟暮时分，船主和他的船员把我——疲惫不堪、狼狈至极、满肚子火——送上了克鲁塞斯那座破败的码头。

## III / III

===Original===
My reception at the Independent Hotel—A Cruces table d'hôte—Life in Cruces—Amusements of the crowds—A novel four-post bed.

The sympathising reader, who very likely has been laughing heartily at my late troubles, can fancy that I was looking forward with no little pleasurable anticipation to reaching my brother's cheerful home at Cruces. After the long night spent on board the wretched boat in my stiff, clayey dress, and the hours of fasting, the warmth and good cheer of the Independent Hotel could not fail to be acceptable. My brother met me on the rickety wharf with the kindest welcome in his face, although he did not attempt to conceal a smile at my forlorn appearance, and giving the necessary instructions about my luggage, led the way at once to his house, which was situated at the upper end of the street. A capital site, he said, when the rest of the town was under water—which agreeable variety occurred twice or thrice a year unexpectedly. On our way, he rather damped my hopes by expressing his fears that he should be unable to provide his sister with the accommodation he could wish. For you see, he said, the crowd from Panama has just come in, meeting your crowd from Navy Bay; and I shouldn't be at all surprised if very many of them have no better bed than the store floors. But, despite this warning, I was miserably unprepared for the reception that awaited me. To be sure, I found Cruces as like Gorgona, in its dampness, dirt, and confusion, as it well could be; but the crowd from the goldfields of California had just arrived, having made the journey from Panama on mules, and the street was filled with motley groups in picturesque variety of attire. The hotels were also full of them, while many lounged in the verandahs after their day's journey. Rude, coarse gold-diggers, in gay-coloured shirts, and long, serviceable boots, elbowed, in perfect equality, keen Yankee speculators, as close shaven, neat, and clean on the Isthmus of Panama as in the streets of New York or New Orleans. The women alone kept aloof from each other, and well they might; for, while a very few seemed not ashamed of their sex, it was somewhat difficult to distinguish the majority from their male companions, save by their bolder and more reckless voice and manner. I must say, however, that many of them adopted male attire for the journey across the Isthmus only, as it spared them many compliments which their husbands were often disposed to resent, however flattering they might be to their choice.

===Chinese===
我在独立旅馆受到的接待——克鲁塞斯的包餐桌——克鲁塞斯的生活——人群的消遣——一张新式的四柱床。

同情的读者——您方才多半还在为我的倒霉遭遇开怀大笑——可以想见，我是怀着不小的愉悦期盼，盼着抵达我兄弟在克鲁塞斯那个欢乐的家。在那条破船上穿着硬邦邦的黏土裙熬过长夜、又空了好几个小时肚子之后，独立旅馆（Independent Hotel）的温暖与美食必然不会不受欢迎。我兄弟到摇摇晃晃的码头上来迎我，脸上带着最亲切的欢迎，尽管他没有掩饰对我那副可怜相的一笑；他交代了几句行李的事，便立刻领路往他的房子去，房子坐落在街的上端。他说，这是块顶好的地段——等镇子其余的地方全泡进水里的时候（这种宜人的变化，一年总会出其不意地来上两三回）。一路上，他倒是浇了我的兴致，说他恐怕无法给妹妹提供他想要给予的招待。因为，你瞧，他说，从巴拿马来的人潮刚刚进城，正好撞上你从海军湾带来的这批人潮；他们当中要是有许许多多人除了店堂地板再没有更好的床铺，我一点也不会吃惊。可是，尽管有这番预警，等着我的是怎样一场接待，我还是狼狈得毫无防备。的确，我发现克鲁塞斯在潮湿、肮脏和杂乱上同戈尔戈纳像得不差分毫；但加州金矿来的人潮刚到——他们是骑骡子从巴拿马赶来的——街上满是衣着斑斓、五花八门的一群群人。旅馆里也住满了他们，许多人经过一天的旅程，正在游廊里闲荡。粗鲁的挖金汉子，穿着花哨的衬衫和结实的长靴，同精明的北方佬投机客平起平坐、推推搡搡——这些北方佬在巴拿马地峡同在纽约或新奥尔良的街头一样，脸刮得精光、齐整利落。唯独女人们彼此保持着距离，她们也确实有理由如此；因为，虽然极少数人似乎并不为自己的性别感到羞赧，但要把大多数人和她们的男伴区分开来，实在有些困难，除非凭她们那更放胆、更无所顾忌的嗓门和做派。不过我必须说，她们当中许多人只是为了横穿地峡的旅程才换上男装，因为这样能省去许多殷勤奉承——那些话对她们的眼光再动听，她们的丈夫也常常要动气。

===Original===
Through all these I pressed on, stiff, cold, and hungry, to the Independent Hotel, eagerly anticipating the comforts which awaited me there. At length we reached it. But, rest! warmth! comfort!—miserable delusions! Picture to yourself, sympathising reader, a long, low hut, built of rough, unhewn, unplaned logs, filled up with mud and split bamboo; a long, sloping roof and a large verandah, already full of visitors. And the interior: a long room, gaily hung with dirty calico, in stripes of red and white; above it another room, in which the guests slept, having the benefit of sharing in any orgies which might be going on below them, through the broad chinks between the rough, irregular planks which formed its floor. At the further end, a small corner, partitioned roughly off, formed a bar, and around it were shelves laden with stores for the travellers, while behind it was a little room used by my brother as his private apartment; but three female travellers had hired it for their own especial use for the night, paying the enormous sum of £10 for so exclusive a luxury. At the entrance sat a black man, taking toll of the comers-in, giving them in exchange for coin or gold-dust (he had a rusty pair of scales to weigh the latter) a dirty ticket, which guaranteed them supper, a night's lodging, and breakfast. I saw all this very quickly, and turned round upon my brother in angry despair.

"What am I to do? Why did you ever bring me to this place? See what a state I am in—cold, hungry, and wretched. I want to wash, to change my clothes, to eat, to—"

But poor Edward could only shrug his shoulders and shake his head, in answer to my indignant remonstrances. At last he made room for me in a corner of the crowded bar, set before me some food, and left me to watch the strange life I had come to; and before long I soon forgot my troubles in the novelty of my position.

===Chinese===
穿过这一切，我硬撑着往前挤，又僵又冷又饿，直奔独立旅馆而去，急切地盼着那里等着我的种种舒适。我们终于到了。可是，休息！温暖！舒适！——可怜的错觉！同情的读者，请您想象：一座长而低矮的棚屋，用粗糙的、原木未削未刨的圆木搭成，缝里糊着泥、塞着劈竹；一道长长的斜屋顶，一条宽大的游廊，游廊里已经满是旅客。再看内部：一间长屋，花哨地挂着红白条纹的脏花布；上面还有一层，客人们就睡在那里，有幸透过粗糙不平的地板木板间的大缝，分享楼下可能正在进行的任何狂欢。最里头，一个草草隔出的小角落算是酒柜，四周是堆满旅客用品的货架，酒柜后面是一间小屋，我兄弟用作私人居室；可三位女旅客为这一晚的独享豪华付了 10 镑的巨款，把它包下了。门口坐着一个黑人，向来客收着进门的买路钱，用硬币或金沙换给一张脏票子（他有一杆锈迹斑斑的小秤，专门称金沙），票子保证供应晚饭、一夜住宿和早饭。这一切我都飞快地看在眼里，便带着愠怒的绝望，转过身冲着我兄弟。

「我该怎么办？你到底为什么把我带到这种地方来？瞧瞧我这副样子——又冷、又饿、又狼狈。我要洗漱，要换衣裳，要吃东西，要——」

可怜的爱德华（Edward）对我愤慨的责难，只能耸耸肩、摇摇头。最后他在拥挤的酒柜一角给我腾出个位子，摆了些吃食在我面前，撂下我让我自己去打量我前来投身的这种奇特生活；不多久，我就在境况的新奇里忘掉了自己的烦恼。

===Original===
The difference between the passengers to and from California was very distinguishable. Those bound for the gold country were to a certain extent fresh from civilization, and had scarcely thrown off its control; whereas the homeward bound revelled in disgusting excess of licence. Although many of the women on their way to California showed clearly enough that the life of licence they sought would not be altogether unfamiliar to them, they still retained some appearance of decency in their attire and manner; but in many cases (as I have before said) the female companions of the successful gold-diggers appeared in no hurry to resume the dress or obligations of their sex. Many were clothed as the men were, in flannel shirt and boots; rode their mules in unfeminine fashion, but with much ease and courage; and in their conversation successfully rivalled the coarseness of their lords. I think, on the whole, that those French lady writers who desire to enjoy the privileges of man, with the irresponsibility of the other sex, would have been delighted with the disciples who were carrying their principles into practice in the streets of Cruces.

The chief object of all the travellers seemed to be dinner or supper; I do not know what term they gave it. Down the entire length of the Independent Hotel ran a table covered with a green oilskin cloth, and at proper intervals were placed knives and forks, plates, and cups and saucers turned down; and when a newcomer received his ticket, and wished to secure his place for the coming repast, he would turn his plate, cup, and saucer up; which mode of reserving seats seemed respected by the rest. And as the evening wore on, the shouting and quarrelling at the doorway in Yankee twang increased momentarily; while some seated themselves at the table, and hammering upon it with the handles of their knives, hallooed out to the excited nigger cooks to make haste with the slapjack. Amidst all this confusion, my brother was quietly selling shirts, boots, trousers, etc., to the travellers; while above all the din could be heard the screaming voices of his touters without, drawing attention to the good cheer of the Independent Hotel. Over and over again, while I cowered in my snug corner, wishing to avoid the notice of all, did I wish myself safe back in my pleasant home in Kingston; but it was too late to find out my mistake now.

===Chinese===
去加州的和从加州回来的旅客，差别一望便知。前往黄金国度的人多少刚脱离文明社会，还没能完全甩开文明的约束；而回程的人则沉溺于令人作呕的放纵无度。虽然许多去加州路上的女人已经清清楚楚地表明，她们所寻求的放纵生活对自己并不算全然陌生，她们在衣着和举止上还保留着几分体面；但在许多情形下（如我先前所说），发了财的挖金人的女伴们并不急于恢复她们性别的装束或义务。许多人穿得同男人一样，法兰绒衬衫加长靴；骑骡子的姿势毫不淑女，却轻松自在、胆气十足；谈吐的粗野，也足以同她们的爷们一较高下。我想，总的来看，那些渴望享有男人的特权、又不肯担另一方性别之责的法国女作家们，若是见到克鲁塞斯街头这些把她们的原则付诸实践的门徒，定会喜出望外。

所有旅客的头等大事，似乎是正餐，或者叫晚饭——我不晓得他们用的哪个词。独立旅馆整个长屋摆着一张桌子，铺着绿色油布，桌上按适当的间隔摆着刀叉、盘子，还有倒扣着的杯碟；新来的旅客领到票子后，若想在下顿饭里占个位子，就把自己那份盘子、杯、碟翻过来摆正；这种占座方式，其余人似乎都尊重。随着夜色渐深，门口北方佬腔调的叫嚷和争吵时时升级；一些人已在桌边落座，用刀柄敲着桌子，朝那些忙得团团转的黑鬼厨子高声吆喝，催他们快把烙饼（slapjack）端上来。在这一片混乱中，我兄弟安静地向旅客们售卖衬衫、靴子、长裤等等；而喧闹之上，还听得见他门外那些揽客的尖嗓子，正把大家的注意引到独立旅馆的美餐上来。我一回又一回地蜷在自己那个安稳的小角落里，只想避开众人的注意，一遍又一遍盼着自己已平安回到金斯敦那舒适可爱的家中；可如今要认清自己的失算，已经太迟了。

===Original===
At last the table was nearly filled with a motley assemblage of men and women, and the slapjack, hot and steaming, was carried in by the black cooks. The hungry diners welcomed its advent with a shout of delight; and yet it did not seem particularly tempting. But beyond all doubt it was a capital pièce de résistance for great eaters; and before the dinner was over, I saw ample reasons to induce any hotelkeeper to give it his patronage. In truth, it was a thick substantial pancake of flour, salt, and water—eggs were far too expensive to be used in its composition; and by the time the supply had disappeared, I thought the largest appetites must have been stayed. But it was followed by pork, strips of beef stewed with hard dumplings, hams, great dishes of rice, jugs of molasses and treacle for sauce; the whole being washed down with an abundance of tea and coffee. Chickens and eggs were provided for those who were prepared to pay for these luxuries of Panama life. But, so scarce and expensive were they, that, as I afterwards discovered, those hotelkeepers whose larders were so stocked would hang out a chicken upon their signposts, as a sure attraction for the richer and more reckless diggers; while the touter's cry of "Eggs and chickens here" was a very telling one. Wine and spirits were also obtainable, but were seldom taken by the Americans, who are abstemious abroad as well as at home.

After dinner the store soon cleared. Gambling was a great attraction; but my brother, dreading its consequences with these hot-brained armed men, allowed none to take place in his hotel. So some lounged away to the faro and monte tables, which were doing a busy trade; others loitered in the verandah, smoking, and looking at the native women, who sang and danced fandangos before them. The whole of the dirty, woebegone place, which had looked so wretched by the light of day, was brilliantly illuminated now. Night would bring no rest to Cruces, while the crowds were there to be fed, cheated, or amused. Daybreak would find the faro-tables, with their piles of silver and little heaps of gold-dust, still surrounded by haggard gamblers; daybreak would gleam sickly upon the tawdry finery of the poor Spanish singers and dancers, whose weary night's work would enable them to live upon the travellers' bounty for the next week or so. These few hours of gaiety and excitement were to provide the Cruces people with food and clothing for as many days; and while their transitory sun shone, I will do them the justice to say they gathered in their hay busily. In the exciting race for gold, we need not be surprised at the strange groups which line the racecourse. All that I wondered at was, that I had not foreseen what I found, or that my rage for change and novelty had closed my ears against the warning voices of those who knew somewhat of the highroad to California; but I was too tired to moralise long, and begged my brother to find me a bed somewhere. He failed to do so completely, and in despair I took the matter in my own hands; and stripping the green oilskin cloth from the rough table—it would not be wanted again until tomorrow's breakfast—pinned up some curtains round the table's legs, and turned in with my little servant beneath it. It was some comfort to know that my brother, his servants, and Mac brought their mattresses, and slept upon it above us. It was a novel bed, and required some slight stretch of the imagination to fancy it a four-poster; but I was too tired to be particular, and slept soundly.

===Chinese===
桌上终于差不多坐满了一群男女混杂、形形色色的人，热气腾腾的烙饼由黑人厨子们端了进来。饿汉们一声欢呼，迎接它的降临；可它看上去实在说不上多诱人。但毫无疑问，对大肚汉来说，它是一道顶好的压轴主菜（pièce de résistance）；正餐结束之前，我就看到了充足的理由，足以促使任何旅店老板对它多加照拂。实际上，那是一种用面粉、盐和水做的厚实大煎饼——鸡蛋太贵，舍不得用在里头；等这一轮的供应消灭干净，我以为最大的胃口也该餍足了。但接踵而来的是猪肉、炖牛肉条配硬面疙瘩、火腿、大盘大盘的米饭，还有一壶壶作浇头的糖蜜和糖浆；这一整席，再用大量的茶和咖啡送下肚去。鸡和鸡蛋则是备给那些准备好为巴拿马生活的此类奢侈品掏钱的人的。但它们如此稀缺昂贵，以至于——正如我后来发现的那样——那些储了这些存货的旅店老板会在招牌杆上挂出一只鸡，作为吸引更有钱、更挥霍的挖金人的可靠法宝；而揽客的那一嗓子「这儿有鸡蛋和鸡」也极有号召力。葡萄酒和烈酒也弄得到，但美国人很少沾——他们在国外同在国内一样节制饮食。

饭后店堂很快清空。赌博是个大诱惑；但我兄弟忌惮这些头脑发热、身带武器的人赌出祸事来，一个也不许在他的旅馆里开赌。于是有些人晃到法罗（faro）和蒙特（monte）赌桌那边去了，那边生意兴隆；另一些人在游廊里闲逛，抽着烟，看当地的女人在他们面前唱着、跳着凡丹戈舞（fandango）。这整个白天看上去如此凄惶的肮脏破败之地，此刻已是一片灯火通明。夜不会给克鲁塞斯带来安宁，只要人群还在这里等着被填饱、被讹诈或被取乐。破晓时分，法罗赌桌上成堆的银元和一小堆一小堆的金沙旁边，仍会围着形容枯槁的赌徒；破晓的微光会病恹恹地照在可怜的西班牙歌手舞娘那俗艳的行头上，她们一夜的辛劳，够她们靠旅客的赏钱再活上一个星期上下。这几个小时的欢腾和兴奋，要给克鲁塞斯的人们挣来同样天数的衣食；趁着他们这轮转瞬即逝的太阳还照着，我得凭良心说一句：他们收草打草忙得可勤快。在这场攫金的狂热赛跑里，赛道两旁挤着奇形怪状的人群，我们无须惊讶。我只是奇怪，自己竟没有预见到亲眼所见的一切，或者说，我对变换与新奇的狂热，竟让我堵上了耳朵，不去听那些对通往加州的大道略知一二的人们的警告之声；可我累得没心思长篇大论地感慨，只好求兄弟随便给我找个床位。他彻底没能办到，绝望之下我便亲自动手：把粗糙长桌上那块绿油布扯了下来——反正到明天早饭前它也派不上用场——在桌子四腿周围别上几幅帘子，便带着我的小仆人钻到桌子底下睡下了。知道我兄弟、他的仆人们和麦克把褥子搬到我们头顶的桌面上睡，多少是点安慰。这是一张新奇的床，得稍稍伸展一下想象力，才能把它想象成一张四柱大床；可我累得顾不上挑剔，睡得香甜。

===Original===
We were up right early on the following morning; and refreshed with my night's sleep, I entered heartily into the preparations for breakfast. That meal over, the homeward-bound passengers took boats en route for Gorgona, while those bound for California hired mules for the land journey to Panama. So after awhile all cleared away, and Cruces was left to its unhealthy solitude.

===Chinese===
第二天一早我们就起来了；一夜安眠使我精神复原，我便起劲地投入早饭的准备。那顿饭吃完，回程的旅客们上船往戈尔戈纳去了，而去加州的则雇了骡子，走陆路往巴拿马去。过了一阵，人全都散尽，克鲁塞斯又还给它那不利于健康的冷清。

## IV / IV

===Original===
An unwelcome visitor in Cruces—The cholera—Success of the yellow doctress—Fearful scene at the mule-owner's—The burying parties—The cholera attacks me.

I do not think I have ever known what it is to despair, or even to despond (if such were my inclination, I have had some opportunities recently), and it was not long before I began to find out the bright side of Cruces life, and enter into schemes for staying there. But it would be a week or so before the advent of another crowd would wake Cruces to life and activity again; and in the meanwhile, and until I could find a convenient hut for my intended hotel, I remained my brother's guest.

But it was destined that I should not be long in Cruces before my medicinal skill and knowledge were put to the test. Before the passengers for Panama had been many days gone, it was found that they had left one of their number behind them, and that one—the cholera. I believe that the faculty have not yet come to the conclusion that the cholera is contagious, and I am not presumptuous enough to forestall them; but my people have always considered it to be so, and the poor Cruces folks did not hesitate to say that this new and terrible plague had been a fellow-traveller with the Americans from New Orleans or some other of its favoured haunts. I had the first intimation of its unwelcome presence in the following abrupt and unpleasant manner:—

===Chinese===
克鲁塞斯来了一位不受欢迎的客人——霍乱——黄肤女医士的成功——骡主家的骇人场面——掩埋队——霍乱找上了我。

我不认为自己曾体会过绝望的滋味，甚至连消沉都不曾有过（倘若我有这种倾向，近来倒也不乏机会）；不久，我便开始发现克鲁塞斯生活光明的一面，盘算起在此长住的计划来。但还要等一个星期上下，下一批人潮到来，才会把克鲁塞斯重新唤醒到忙碌与活跃之中；在此期间，在找到适合开我那旅馆的棚屋之前，我一直是兄弟座上的客。

但命中注定，我在克鲁塞斯待不了多久，我的医术和知识就要接受考验。前往巴拿马的旅客走了没几天，人们便发现，他们把同行者中的一个留在了身后，而那一个——就是霍乱。我相信医学界至今尚未得出霍乱会传染的定论，我也不敢狂妄到抢在他们前面下断语；但我的人民历来是这样认为的，克鲁塞斯的穷苦百姓也毫不迟疑地说，这场崭新而可怕的瘟疫，是同美国人结伴同行的旅伴，来自新奥尔良或它的其他惯常巢穴。这位不速之客驾临的头一个讯息，我是在下面这桩突兀而不愉快的事情里得到的：——

===Original===
A Spaniard, an old and intimate friend of my brother, had supped with him one evening, and upon returning home had been taken ill, and after a short period of intense suffering had died. So sudden and so mysterious a death gave rise to the rumour that he had been poisoned, and suspicion rested for a time, perhaps not unnaturally, upon my brother, in whose company the dead man had last been. Anxious for many reasons—the chief one, perhaps, the position of my brother—I went down to see the corpse. A single glance at the poor fellow showed me the terrible truth. The distressed face, sunken eyes, cramped limbs, and discoloured shrivelled skin were all symptoms which I had been familiar with very recently; and at once I pronounced the cause of death to be cholera. The Cruces people were mightily angry with me for expressing such an opinion; even my brother, although it relieved him of the odium of a great crime, was as annoyed as the rest. But by twelve o'clock that morning one of the Spaniard's friends was attacked similarly, and the very people who had been most angry with me a few hours previously, came to me now eager for advice. There was no doctor in Cruces; the nearest approach to one was a little timid dentist, who was there by accident, and who refused to prescribe for the sufferer, and I was obliged to do my best. Selecting from my medicine chest—I never travel anywhere without it—what I deemed necessary, I went hastily to the patient, and at once adopted the remedies I considered fit. It was a very obstinate case, but by dint of mustard emetics, warm fomentations, mustard plasters on the stomach and the back, and calomel, at first in large then in gradually smaller doses, I succeeded in saving my first cholera patient in Cruces.

For a few days the terrible disease made such slow progress amongst us that we almost hoped it had passed on its way and spared us; but all at once it spread rapidly, and affrighted faces and cries of woe soon showed how fatally the destroyer was at work. And in so great request were my services, that for days and nights together I scarcely knew what it was to enjoy two successive hours' rest.

===Chinese===
一天晚上，一位西班牙人——我兄弟相交多年的密友——同他一起用了晚饭，回家路上便病倒了，经过一阵极度的痛苦挣扎便死了。如此突然、如此神秘的死亡，引出了他是被毒死的流言，一时间怀疑落到了我兄弟头上——死者最后正是在他的陪伴之下，这怀疑也许并非全无道理。出于多重理由的焦虑——头一条或许就是我兄弟的处境——我下去看了尸体。朝那可怜人只瞥了一眼，可怕的真相便向我昭然若揭。那张痛苦扭曲的脸、深陷的眼睛、痉挛抽搐的四肢、变色皱缩的皮肤，全是我新近才熟识的症状；我当即断定，死因是霍乱。克鲁塞斯的人们因为我说出这样的意见，对我大为光火；连我兄弟——尽管这让他卸脱了一桩大罪的恶名——也同旁人一样恼火。但当天中午十二点以前，那位西班牙人的一位朋友便以相似的症状倒下了，而几个小时前还对我最为恼火的那帮人，此刻纷纷涌到我这里，急切地讨主意。克鲁塞斯没有医生；最接近医生的是一个胆子小小的牙医，他纯属碰巧在此，又拒绝给病人开方，我只好尽力而为。我从药箱里——我出门从不离它——挑出我认为必需的药，匆匆赶到病人那里，立刻采用我认为合适的疗法。那是一个非常顽固的病例，但凭着芥末催吐剂、温热敷布、敷在胃部和背部的芥子泥，以及甘汞（calomel）——起初大剂量、随后逐渐减小——我终于救活了我在克鲁塞斯的第一位霍乱病人。

有几天，这可怕的疾病在我们中间进展得如此缓慢，我们几乎指望它已顺路走远、放过了我们；但它突然间迅猛蔓延开来，惊惶的面孔和哀恸的哭喊很快表明，这位毁灭者干得多么致命。我的效劳如此抢手，以致接连几天几夜，我几乎不知道连着享受两个钟头的安歇是什么滋味。

===Original===
And here I must pause to set myself right with my kind reader. He or she will not, I hope, think that, in narrating these incidents, I am exalting my poor part in them unduly. I do not deny (it is the only thing indeed that I have to be proud of) that I *am* pleased and gratified when I look back upon my past life, and see times now and then, and places here and there, when and where I have been enabled to benefit my fellow-creatures suffering from ills my skill could often remedy. Nor do I think that the kind reader will consider this feeling an unworthy one. If it be so, and if, in the following pages, the account of what Providence has given me strength to do on larger fields of action be considered vain or egotistical, still I cannot help narrating them, for my share in them appears to be the one and only claim I have to interest the public ear. Moreover I shall be sadly disappointed, if those years of life which may be still in store for me are not permitted by Providence to be devoted to similar usefulness. I am not ashamed to confess—for the gratification is, after all, a selfish one—that I love to be of service to those who need a woman's help. And wherever the need arises—on whatever distant shore—I ask no greater or higher privilege than to minister to it. After this explanation, I resume more freely the account of my labours in Cruces.

It was scarcely surprising that the cholera should spread rapidly, for fear is its powerful auxiliary, and the Cruces people bowed down before the plague in slavish despair. The Americans and other foreigners in the place showed a brave front, but the natives, constitutionally cowardly, made not the feeblest show of resistance. Beyond filling the poor church, and making the priests bring out into the streets figures of tawdry dirty saints, supposed to possess some miraculous influence which they never exerted, before which they prostrated themselves, invoking their aid with passionate prayers and cries, they did nothing. Very likely the saints would have got the credit of helping them if they had helped themselves; but the poor cowards never stirred a finger to clean out their close, reeking huts, or rid the damp streets of the rotting accumulation of months. I think their chief reliance was on "the yellow woman from Jamaica with the cholera medicine." Nor was this surprising; for the Spanish doctor, who was sent for from Panama, became nervous and frightened at the horrors around him, and the people soon saw that he was not familiar with the terrible disease he was called upon to do battle with, and preferred trusting to one who was.

===Chinese===
在这里我必须停一停，向好心的读者把自己交代清楚。我希望他或她不要以为，我在讲述这些事件时，是在不适当地抬高自己那份可怜的作用。我不否认（这确实也是我唯一可引以为傲的东西）——当我回顾往昔岁月，看到时时处处、这里那里，我都能凭一技之长为受苦的同胞解难除病时，我*确实*感到欣喜和快慰。我也不认为好心的读者会把这种情感看作有失体面。倘若果真如此，倘若在后面的篇章里，我讲述天意赋予我力量、在更大的行动舞台上所做成的事，被认为虚荣或自恋，我也仍然不能不写——因为我在其中的那份参与，似乎是我能吸引公众耳朵的唯一凭据。再者，倘若天意不许我把可能尚存的余年投入同样的用处，我会深深失望。我不怕承认——这份快慰毕竟是一种自私的快慰——我爱为那些需要女人帮助的人效劳。而且无论何处起了这种需要——无论多么遥远的海岸——我只求获准前去效力，别无更大、更高的特权。交代过这些，我便可以更无顾忌地继续讲述我在克鲁塞斯的劳作了。

霍乱蔓延得迅速，几乎不足为怪，因为恐惧是它的得力帮凶，而克鲁塞斯的人们在瘟疫面前奴颜婢膝地绝望屈服。当地 的美国人和其他外国人摆出勇敢的面孔，但本地人生性怯懦，连最微弱的抵抗姿态也不做。他们无非是把那座可怜的教堂挤得满满的，让神父们把一个个俗艳肮脏的圣像抬上街头——据说这些圣像拥有某种从未施展过的神力——在像前五体投地，用狂热的祷告和哭喊祈求救助；除此之外，他们什么也没做。很可能，倘若他们自救，圣像们本可以落个救助了他们的美名；可这些可怜的懦夫从不肯动一根手指，去打扫他们那密闭发臭的棚屋，或清掉潮湿街道上积攒了几个月的腐臭垃圾。我想，他们主要的指望，是「那个带着霍乱药的、牙买加来的黄皮肤女人」。这也不奇怪；因为从巴拿马请来的那位西班牙医生，被周围的恐怖场面弄得心慌胆怯，人们很快看出，他对他受召来对抗的可怕疾病并不熟识，便宁愿信赖一个熟识它的人。

===Original===
It must be understood that many of those who could afford to pay for my services did so handsomely, but the great majority of my patients had nothing better to give their doctress than thanks. The best part of my practice lay amongst the American store and hotel keepers, the worst among the native boatmen and muleteers. These latter died by scores, and among them I saw some scenes of horror I would fain forget, if it were possible. One terrible night, passed with some of them, has often haunted me. I will endeavour to narrate it, and should the reader be supposed to think it highly coloured and doubtful, I will only tell him that, terrible as it seems, I saw almost as fearful scenes on the Crimean peninsula among British men, a few thousand miles only from comfort and plenty.

===Chinese===
必须说明，那些付得起诊费的人，许多都付得颇为慷慨；但我的病人中的绝大多数，能给他们这位女医士的，无非是道谢。我行医的对象里最好的一部分，是美国的店主和旅店老板们，最糟的一部分在本地船夫和骡夫中间。后者成打地死去，在他们中间，我见过一些但愿能够忘却、却终究忘不掉的恐怖场面。同他们一起度过的一个可怕夜晚，时常萦绕在我心头。我来试着把它讲出来；倘若读者觉得它渲染过甚、令人生疑，我只想告诉他：它尽管看似骇人，在克里米亚半岛上、在距离舒适丰足只有几千英里的英国人中间，我见过几乎同样可怕的场面。

===Original===
It was late in the evening when the largest mule-owner in Cruces came to me and implored me to accompany him to his kraal, a short distance from the town, where he said some of his men were dying. One in particular, his head muleteer, a very valuable servant, he was most selfishly anxious for, and, on the way thither, promised me a large remuneration if I should succeed in saving him. Our journey was not a long one, but it rained hard, and the fields were flooded, so that it took us some time to reach the long, low hut which he called his home. I would rather not see such another scene as the interior of that hut presented. Its roof scarcely sheltered its wretched inmates from the searching rain; its floor was the damp, rank turf, trodden by the mules' hoofs and the muleteers' feet into thick mud. Around, in dirty hammocks, and on the damp floor, were the inmates of this wretched place, male and female, the strong and the sick together, breathing air that nearly choked me, accustomed as I had grown to live in impure atmosphere; for beneath the same roof the mules, more valuable to their master than his human servants, were stabled, their forefeet locked, and beside them were heaps of saddles, packs, and harness. The groans of the sufferers and the anxiety and fear of their comrades were so painful to hear and witness, that for a few minutes I felt an almost uncontrollable impulse to run out into the stormy night, and flee from this plague-spot. But the weak feeling vanished, and I set about my duty. The mule-owner was so frightened that he did not hesitate to obey orders, and, by my directions, doors and shutters were thrown open, fires were lighted, and every effort made to ventilate the place; and then, with the aid of the frightened women, I applied myself to my poor patients. Two were beyond my skill. Death alone could give them relief. The others I could help. But no words of mine could induce them to bear their terrible sufferings like men. They screamed and groaned, not like women, for few would have been so craven-hearted, but like children; calling, in the intervals of violent pain, upon Jesu, the Madonna, and all the saints of heaven whom their lives had scandalised. I stayed with them until midnight, and then got away for a little time. But I had not long been quiet, before the mule-master was after me again. The men were worse; would I return with him. The rain was drifting heavily on the thatched roof, as it only does in tropical climates, and I was tired to death; but I could not resist his appeal. He had brought with him a pair of tall, thick boots, in which I was to wade through the flooded fields; and with some difficulty I again reached the kraal. I found the worst cases sinking fast, one of the others had relapsed, while fear had paralysed the efforts of the rest. At last I restored some order; and, with the help of the bravest of the women, fixed up rude screens around the dying men. But no screens could shut out from the others their awful groans and cries for the aid that no mortal power could give them. So the long night passed away; first a deathlike stillness behind one screen, and then a sudden silence behind the other, showing that the fierce battle with death was over, and who had been the victor. And, meanwhile, I sat before the flickering fire, with my last patient in my lap—a poor, little, brown-faced orphan infant, scarce a year old, was dying in my arms, and I was powerless to save it. It may seem strange, but it is a fact, that I thought more of that little child than I did of the men who were struggling for their lives, and prayed very earnestly and solemnly to God to spare it. But it did not please Him to grant my prayer, and towards morning the wee spirit left this sinful world for the home above it had so lately left, and what was mortal of the little infant lay dead in my arms. Then it was that I began to think—how the idea first arose in my mind I can hardly say—that, if it were possible to take this little child and examine it, I should learn more of the terrible disease which was sparing neither young nor old, and should know better how to do battle with it. I was not afraid to use my baby patient thus. I knew its fled spirit would not reproach me, for I had done all I could for it in life—had shed tears over it, and prayed for it.

===Chinese===
那天夜已深，克鲁塞斯最大的骡主来找我，恳求我随他去他的骡栏（kraal）——离镇子不远——他说他的一些伙计快死了。其中一个，他的领班骡夫，是个非常得力的仆人，他自私地最是挂心，一路向我许诺：只要我救得活他，必有一笔厚报。我们走的路不算长，但雨下得很大，田地都被淹了，我们花了不少工夫才走到他称作家的那座低矮长棚。那样的场面——那棚屋内部的景象——我宁愿此生不再见第二回。屋顶几乎遮不住棚里可怜的住户，挡不住无孔不入的雨水；地面是潮湿丰茂的草皮，被骡蹄和骡夫的脚踩成了厚厚的烂泥。四下里，在肮脏的吊床上和潮湿的地面上，躺着这悲惨之地的住户，男男女女，强壮的和患病的挤在一处，呼吸着几乎令我窒息的空气——尽管我早已过惯了不洁空气里的日子；因为在同一片屋顶下还厩着骡子——在主人眼里，它们比他的人身仆役更值钱——骡子前脚被拴锁着，旁边堆着一摞摞鞍子、驮包和挽具。病人们的呻吟、他们同伴的焦灼惊惧，听起来、看上去都那样令人痛苦，有那么几分钟，我几乎按捺不住一股冲动，想冲进风雨大作的黑夜，从这块瘟疫之地逃走。但这软弱的念头消散了，我开始履行我的职责。骡主吓坏了，对我吩咐的事二话不说照办；按我的指示，门窗板壁尽数敞开，生起了火，千方百计让这地方通风；然后，我借着那些吓坏了的女人的帮手，着手医治我可怜的病人。有两个已非我的医术所能及，唯有死亡能给他们解脱。其余的我还能帮上忙。但无论我说什么话，都没法劝他们像男子汉那样忍受那可怕的痛苦。他们尖叫、呻吟，不像女人——因为很少有女人会那样怯懦——倒像孩子；在剧痛的间歇里，呼喊着耶稣、圣母，和他们一生的行止早已亵渎净尽的满天众圣。我陪他们守到半夜，然后抽身离开了一会儿。可我安生没多久，骡主义追了来。病人们更糟了；我肯不肯随他回去。雨重重地打在茅草顶上，只有热带的雨才那样下，而我已累得要死；可我拒绝不了他的恳求。他随身带来一双高筒厚靴，好让我穿着蹚过淹水的田野；我又费了些周折才回到那座骡栏。我发现最重的病例正迅速沉落，另一个已经复发，其余的人则被恐惧瘫痪了努力。我终于整顿出一些秩序，借着女人们里头最勇敢的几个帮忙，在垂死者的周围支起了简陋的围屏。但无论什么围屏，都挡不住那可怕的呻吟和哭喊传进其余人的耳朵——他们在呼求任何人力都无法给予的救助。漫漫长夜就这样过去：先是这一扇屏后死一般的寂静，接着是那一扇屏后陡然的沉默，表明同死神的恶战已经收场，也表明谁是胜者。与此同时，我坐在闪烁的火堆前，怀里抱着我最后一个病人——一个可怜的、小小的、棕脸的孤儿，还不到一岁，正在我怀中死去，而我无力回天。说来也许奇怪，但这是实情：我对那个小小孩子的牵挂，超过了那些为活命挣扎的汉子，我极其恳切、郑重地祈求上帝饶它一命。但祂没有应允我的祷告；临近清晨，那小小的魂灵离开了这个罪孽的世界，回到它不久前才离开的天家，而那婴孩的血肉之躯，躺在我怀中死去了。就是在那时，我开始想——这念头最初如何在我脑中升起，我实在说不上来——倘若能把这个小孩子拿来查验，我或许能对这场不放过老幼的可怕疾病多懂得一分，也更懂得如何同它作战。我不怕这样使用我的小病人。我知道它那飞走的魂灵不会怪我，因为我在它生前已为它尽了全力——为它流过泪，为它做过祷告。

===Original===
It was cold grey dawn, and the rain had ceased, when I followed the man who had taken the dead child away to bury it, and bribed him to carry it by an unfrequented path down to the riverside, and accompany me to the thick retired bush on the opposite bank. Having persuaded him thus much, it was not difficult, with the help of silver arguments to convince him that it would be for the general benefit and his own, if I could learn from this poor little thing the secret inner workings of our common foe; and ultimately he stayed by me, and aided me in my first and last post mortem examination. It seems a strange deed to accomplish, and I am sure I could not wield the scalpel or the substitute I then used now, but at that time the excitement had strung my mind up to a high pitch of courage and determination; and perhaps the daily, almost hourly, scenes of death had made me somewhat callous. I need not linger on this scene, nor give the readers the results of my operation; although novel to me, and decidedly useful, they were what every medical man well knows.

We buried the poor little body beneath a piece of luxuriant turf, and stole back into Cruces like guilty things. But the knowledge I had obtained thus strangely was very valuable to me, and was soon put into practice. But that I dreaded boring my readers, I would fain give them some idea of my treatment of this terrible disease. I have no doubt that at first I made some lamentable blunders, and, may be, lost patients which a little later I could have saved. I know I came across, the other day, some notes of cholera medicines which made me shudder, and I dare say they have been used in their turn and found wanting. The simplest remedies were perhaps the best. Mustard plasters, and emetics, and calomel; the mercury applied externally, where the veins were nearest the surface, were my usual resources. Opium I rather dreaded, as its effect is to incapacitate the system from making any exertion, and it lulls the patient into a sleep which is often the sleep of death. When my patients felt thirsty, I would give them water in which cinnamon had been boiled. One stubborn attack succumbed to an additional dose of ten grains of sugar of lead, mixed in a pint of water, given in doses of a tablespoonful every quarter of an hour. Another patient, a girl, I rubbed over with warm oil, camphor, and spirits of wine. Above all, I never neglected to apply mustard poultices to the stomach, spine, and neck, and particularly to keep my patient warm about the region of the heart. Nor did I relax my care when the disease had passed by, for danger did not cease when the great foe was beaten off. The patient was left prostrate; strengthening medicines had to be given cautiously, for fever, often of the brain, would follow. But, after all, one great conclusion, which my practice in cholera cases enabled me to come to, was the old one, that few constitutions permitted the use of exactly similar remedies, and that the course of treatment which saved one man, would, if persisted in, have very likely killed his brother.

===Chinese===
天色是清冷的灰白黎明，雨已经停了。我跟着那个抱走死孩去掩埋的男人，买通他走一条人迹罕至的小路下到河边，陪我到对岸茂密幽僻的树丛里去。既然说动了他到这里，再借「银弹」之力让他相信——倘若我能从这可怜的小东西身上弄清我们共同仇敌的内在秘密运作，于众人、于他自己都有好处——便不难了；最终他留在了我身边，协助我做了我此生第一次、也是最后一次尸体解剖（post mortem examination）。这看来是一桩奇怪的举动，而且我肯定，如今的我再也拿不起那把解剖刀，或我当时用作替代的什么器具；可在那时，兴奋已把我的心弦绷到了勇气与决断的顶点；何况那日日、几乎时时的死亡场面，也许已让我有几分麻木了。这一幕我不必细述，解剖所见也不必一一告知读者；它们虽于我新颖，也确有益处，却都是每个行医者烂熟于心的东西。

我们把那可怜的小小尸身埋在一块丰茂的草皮下，像做了亏心事似的溜回克鲁塞斯。但我以这奇异方式获得的知识对我极为宝贵，很快便派上了用场。若不是怕烦扰读者，我很想让他们对这可怕疾病的治疗略知一二。我毫不怀疑，起初我犯过一些可悲的差错，说不定还耽误了一些再晚些本可救回的病人。我记得前些天翻到几页霍乱用药的笔记，看得我直打寒噤；想必它们也曾被轮番用过，并被发现不管用。最简单的药或许就是最好的。芥子泥敷剂、催吐剂和甘汞；水银外敷在静脉最贴近表皮之处——这些是我惯常的法宝。鸦片我则相当忌惮，因为它的作用是叫身体使不出一点力气，把病人哄入一场往往即是死亡之眠的睡眠。病人觉得口渴时，我给他们喝煮过肉桂的水。有一回顽固的发作，是被额外一剂 10 格令的铅糖（sugar of lead）降伏的——溶在一品脱水中，每刻钟服一汤匙。另一个病人是个姑娘，我用温油、樟脑和酒精给她周身揉搓。最重要的，我从不疏忽把芥子泥敷剂敷在胃部、脊柱和颈上，尤其注意让病人心口一带保持温暖。疾病过去之后，我的照料也不松懈，因为大敌虽被打退，危险并未止息。病人仍虚弱不堪；滋补的药须审慎给予，因为接踵而来的常是热症，而且往往发在脑部。但归根结底，我的霍乱临床经验使我得出的一大结论，还是那句老话：极少有两种体质容得下一模一样的疗法；救得了这个人的疗程，倘若原样不变地用下去，很可能就会要了他兄弟的命。

===Original===
Generally speaking, the cholera showed premonitory symptoms; such as giddiness, sickness, diarrhoea, or sunken eyes and distressed look; but sometimes the substance followed its forecoming shadow so quickly, and the crisis was so rapid, that there was no time to apply any remedies. An American carpenter complained of giddiness and sickness—warning signs—succeeded so quickly by the worst symptoms of cholera, that in less than an hour his face became of an indigo tint, his limbs were doubled up horribly with violent cramps, and he died.

To the convicts—and if there could be grades of wretchedness in Cruces, these poor creatures were the lowest—belonged the terrible task of burying the dead; a duty to which they showed the utmost repugnance. Not unfrequently, at some fancied alarm, they would fling down their burden, until at last it became necessary to employ the soldiers to see that they discharged the task allotted to them. Ordinarily, the victims were buried immediately after death, with such imperfect rites of sepulture as the harassed frightened priests would pay them, and very seldom was time afforded by the authorities to the survivors to pay those last offices to the departed which a Spaniard and a Catholic considers so important.

===Chinese===
一般而言，霍乱会先露出前驱症状，比如眩晕、恶心、腹泻，或眼窝深陷、面容愁苦；但有时实体紧随着它先行的影子来得那样快，病情转折那样急，根本来不及施用任何药物。一个美国木匠诉说头晕恶心——这是警报——紧接着便是霍乱最凶险的症状，不出一个小时，他的脸已变成靛蓝色，四肢被剧烈的痉挛可怕地蜷缩起来，人就死了。

埋葬死者的可怕差事，落在了苦役犯们身上——倘若克鲁塞斯的悲惨也分等级，这些可怜虫便在最底层——他们对这项义务表现出极端的憎恶。常常只是出于某种臆想的惊慌，他们便撂下肩上的担子一跑了之，以致最后不得不动用士兵监督，他们才肯完成分派给他们的任务。通常，死者一断气便被草草下葬，只有被搅扰得惊恐不已的神父们草草应付的可怜仪节；当局也很少给幸存者留出时间，去为亡者尽一个西班牙人和天主教徒看得极重的身后之礼。

===Original===
It was when the cholera was at its height, and yet he had left—perhaps on important business—his wife and family, and gone to Panama for three days. On the day after his departure, the plague broke out in his house, and my services were required promptly. I found the miserable household in terrible alarm, and yet confining their exertions to praying to a coarse black priest in a black surplice, who, kneeling beside the couch of the Spanish lady, was praying (in his turn) to some favourite saint in Cruces. The sufferer was a beautiful woman, suffering from a violent attack of cholera, with no one to help her, or even to take from her arms the poor little child they had allowed her to retain. In her intervals of comparative freedom from pain, her cries to the Madonna and her husband were heartrending to hear. I had the greatest difficulty to rout the stupid priest and his as stupid worshippers, and do what I could for the sufferer. It was very little, and before long the unconscious Spaniard was a widower. Soon after, the authorities came for the body. I never saw such passionate anger and despair as were shown by her relatives and servants, old and young, at the intrusion—rage that she, who had been so exalted in life, should go to her grave like the poor, poor clay she was. Orders were given to bar the door against the convict gang who had come to discharge their unpleasant duty, and while all were busy decking out the unconscious corpse in gayest attire, none paid any heed to me bending over the fire with the motherless child, journeying fast to join its dead parent. I had made more than one effort to escape, for I felt more sick and wretched than at any similar scene of woe; but finding exit impossible, I turned my back upon them, and attended to the dying child. Nor did I heed their actions until I heard orders given to admit the burial party, and then I found that they had dressed the corpse in rich white satin, and decked her head with flowers.

The agitation and excitement of this scene had affected me as no previous horror had done, and I could not help fancying that symptoms were showing themselves in me with which I was familiar enough in others. Leaving the dying infant to the care of its relatives (when the Spaniard returned he found himself widowed and childless), I hastened to my brother's house. When there, I felt an unpleasant chill come over me, and went to bed at once. Other symptoms followed quickly, and, before nightfall, I knew full well that my turn had come at last, and that the cholera had attacked me, perhaps its greatest foe in Cruces.

===Chinese===
那正是霍乱最猖獗的时候，他却丢下——也许有什么要紧营生——妻子和一家老小，去巴拿马待三天。他走后第二天，瘟疫便在他家中发作，立刻就需要我出力了。我发现那凄凄惶惶的一家人陷入了极大的惊恐，忙来忙去也只是对着一个穿黑色法衣的粗俗黑人神父祷告；那神父跪在西班牙太太的卧榻旁，（轮着他）向克鲁塞斯某位得宠的圣徒祈祷。患者是个美丽的妇人，霍乱正凶猛地发作，身边无人相助，甚至连她怀里那个被大家准许她留着的小孩子，也无人从她臂弯里接过去。在她相对缓解的间歇里，她对圣母、对丈夫的声声哭喊，听得人肝肠寸断。我费了九牛二虎之力，才轰走那愚蠢的神父和他同样愚蠢的信众，能为病人做的，我尽力做了。可实在太少，没过多久，那位毫不知情的西班牙人便做了鳏夫。随后当局来收尸。她的亲属和仆役，老老少少，对这闯入所表现的暴怒与绝望，我从未见过——他们愤恨的是：她生前何等尊贵，死后竟要像她本就是的、那可怜又可怜的泥土一样进坟墓。他们下令顶住大门，抵挡前来履行这不愉快差事的苦役队；正当所有人都忙着给那无知无觉的尸体穿戴最华丽的衣饰时，没有一个人理会我抱着那个没了娘的孩子俯在火边——孩子正快步赶去追随它死去的母亲。我不止一次设法脱身，因为我觉得比在任何类似的惨景中都要恶心难受；但发觉无路可出，我便转过身去背对他们，照料那垂死的孩子。直到听见吩咐放掩埋队进来，我才留意起他们的举动——这时我才发现，他们已给尸体穿上了华贵的白缎子，头上簪满了花。

这一场面的激动与刺激，对我胜过了此前任何一场恐怖，我不由得疑心，那些我在别人身上再熟悉不过的症状，正在我自己身上显形。我把垂死的婴儿交给它的亲属照管（那位西班牙人回来时，发现自己妻死子亡），便匆匆赶回兄弟家。一到那里，我就感到一阵不适的寒意袭来，立刻上了床。其他症状接踵而至，天黑之前，我已完全明白：终于轮到我了，霍乱找上了我——也许，找上了它在克鲁塞斯最大的对头。

## IX / IX

===Original===
