Voyage to Constantinople—Malta—Gibraltar—Constantinople, and what I thought of it—Visit to Scutari Hospital—Miss Nightingale.

I am not going to risk the danger of wearying the reader with a long account of the voyage to Constantinople, already worn threadbare by book-making tourists. It was a very interesting one, and, as I am a good sailor, I had not even the temporary horrors of seasickness to mar it. The weather, although cold, was fine, and the sea good-humouredly calm, and I enjoyed the voyage amazingly. And as day by day we drew nearer to the scene of action, my doubts of success grew less and less, until I had a conviction of the rightness of the step I had taken, which would have carried me buoyantly through any difficulties.

On the way, of course, I was called up from my berth at an unreasonable hour to gaze upon the Cape of St. Vincent, and expected to feel duly impressed when the long bay where Trafalgar's fight was won came in view, with the white convent walls on the cliffs above bathed in the early sunlight. I never failed to take an almost childish interest in the signals which passed between the *Hollander* and the fleet of vessels whose sails whitened the track to and from the Crimea, trying to puzzle out the language these children of the ocean spoke in their hurried course, and wondering whether any, or what sufficiently important thing *could* happen which would warrant their stopping on their busy way.

===Chinese===
君士坦丁堡之旅——马耳他——直布罗陀——君士坦丁堡，以及我对它的观感——探访斯库塔里医院——南丁格尔小姐。

我不打算冒乏倒读者的险，把去君士坦丁堡（Constantinople）的航程细细道来——出书攒稿子的游客们早把它写烂了。这一路十分有趣，而且我天生不晕船，连晕船那点暂时的罪也没受，旅程毫无瑕疵。天气虽冷却晴好，海面脾气好得风平浪静，这一程我过得快活极了。而随着我们一天天驶近战场，我对成败的疑虑也一天天消减，直到我笃信自己迈出的这一步没有走错——这份笃信，本可以轻快地驮着我越过任何艰难。

一路上，我照例在不成体统的时辰被人从铺位上叫起来，去瞻望圣文森特角（Cape of St. Vincent）；特拉法尔加大捷赢得的那道长湾映入眼帘时，我还被指望要肃然起敬一番——崖顶白色的修道院高墙，正浸在清晨的日光里。「荷兰人号」同那些帆船队之间的旗语，我一向抱着近乎孩子气的兴致盯住不放——那些船帆把往来克里米亚的航道染得雪白；我总想猜出这些海洋的儿女们行色匆匆间说的是什么话，又纳闷究竟有没有、有什么了不得的大事*当真*会发生，值得他们在忙碌的航程中停一停船。

===Original===
We spent a short time at Gibraltar, and you may imagine that I was soon on shore making the best use of the few hours' reprieve granted to the *Hollander*'s weary engines. I had an idea that I should do better alone, so I declined all offers of companionship, and selecting a brisk young fellow from the mob of cicerones who offered their services, saw more of the art of fortification in an hour or so than I could understand in as many years. The pleasure was rather fatiguing, and I was not sorry to return to the marketplace, where I stood curiously watching its strange and motley population. While so engaged, I heard for the first time an exclamation which became familiar enough to me afterwards.

"Why, bless my soul, old fellow, if this is not our good old Mother Seacole!" I turned round, and saw two officers, whose features, set in a broad frame of Crimean beard, I had some difficulty in recognising. But I soon remembered that they were two of the 48th, who had been often in my house at Kingston. Glad were the kindhearted fellows, and not a little surprised withal, to meet their old hostess in the marketplace of Gibraltar, bound for the scene of action which they had left invalided; and it was not long before we were talking old times over some wine—Spanish, I suppose—but it was very nasty.

===Chinese===
我们在直布罗陀（Gibraltar）作短暂停留，你可以想象，我很快便上了岸，把赏给「荷兰人号」疲惫机器的这几个钟头缓刑用到十足。我有个成见：独自一人逛得更划算，于是谢绝了一切作伴的美意，从那群蜂拥上前揽生意的导游里挑了个麻利的小伙子；个把钟头里，我看的筑城学问，比拿同样多的年头去钻研还多。这份乐趣相当累人，所以回到市集上我也不觉可惜；我站在那里，好奇地打量着市集上那群古怪、杂色的人群。正看着，我头一回听见了一声日后听熟了的惊呼。

「哎呀，我的老天，老伙计，这不是咱们的好老人家西科尔妈妈吗！」我回过身，看见两位军官；他们的五官镶在一圈宽宽的克里米亚大胡子里，我费了些劲才认出来。但我很快想起，他们是第 48 团的两位，在金斯敦时常到我家来。这两位好心肠的汉子在直布罗陀的市集上撞见他们的老房东——正要奔赴他们养伤离开的那个战场——又惊又喜；不多时，我们便就着一瓶葡萄酒——我想是西班牙的——叙起旧来；那酒可真难喝。

===Original===
"And you are going to the front, old lady—you, of all people in the world?"

"Why not, my sons?—won't they be glad to have me there?"

"By Jove! yes, mother," answered one, an Irishman. "It isn't many women—God bless them!—we've had to spoil us out there. But it's not the place even for you, who know what hardship is. You'll never get a roof to cover you at Balaclava, nor on the road either." So they rattled on, telling me of the difficulties that were in store for me. But they could not shake my resolution.

"Do you think I shall be of any use to you when I get there?"

"Surely."

"Then I'll go, were the place a hundred times worse than you describe it. Can't I rig up a hut with the packing-cases, and sleep, if need be, on straw, like Margery Daw?"

So they laughed, and drank success to me, and to our next meeting; for, although they were going home invalided, the brave fellows' hearts were with their companions, for all the hardships they had passed through.

===Chinese===
「这么说你要上前线去了，老夫人——普天之下，偏偏是你？」

「为什么不呢，我的儿子们？——他们在那边见了我，还不乐坏了？」

「天哪！可不是嘛，妈妈，」其中一个答道，是个爱尔兰人。「疼我们、惯着我们的女人——上帝保佑她们！——在那边可没几个。可那地方连您也去不得，您是吃过苦的人哪。在巴拉克拉瓦，您休想找着一处遮头的屋顶，路上也一样。」他们就这样滔滔不绝，把等在前头的种种难处一一数给我听。可他们动摇不了我的决心。

「你们觉得我到了那边，能帮上你们的忙吗？」

「准能。」

「那我就去，哪怕那地方比你们讲的再糟上一百倍。我难道不能拿包装箱搭个棚子，必要的话睡草铺，跟玛杰丽·多（Margery Daw）一个样？」

他们于是大笑，为我的成功、为我们的重逢干了杯；因为这两位勇敢的汉子虽是伤病回国，心却还留在伙伴们身边——吃过的那些苦，一样也没有冷了它。

===Original===
We stopped at Malta also, where, of course, I landed, and stared about me, and submitted to be robbed by the lazy Maltese with all a traveller's resignation. Here, also, I met friends—some medical officers who had known me in Kingston; and one of them, Dr. F——, lately arrived from Scutari, gave me, when he heard my plans, a letter of introduction to Miss Nightingale, then hard at work, evoking order out of confusion, and bravely resisting the despotism of death, at the hospital of Scutari.

So on, past beautiful islands and shores, until we are steaming against a swift current, and an adverse wind, between two tower-crested promontories of rock, which they tell me stand in Europe and in Asia, and are connected with some pretty tale of love in days long gone by. Ah! travel where a woman may, in the New World, or the Old, she meets this old, old tale everywhere. It is the one bond of sympathy which I have found existing in three quarters of the world alike. So on, until the cable rattles over the windlass, as the good ship's anchor plunges down fathoms deep into the blue waters of the Bosphorus—her voyage ended.

===Chinese===
我们在马耳他（Malta）也停了一站；我自然上了岸，四下里张望，又以旅人应有的顺从，任凭懒惰的马耳他人宰了一刀。在这里我又遇见了朋友——几位在金斯敦就认得我的军医；其中一位 F——医生（Dr. F——）刚从斯库塔里过来，一听我的计划，便给了我引荐信，把我引荐给南丁格尔小姐——她当时正在斯库塔里医院没日没夜地苦干，从一团乱麻中唤出秩序，勇敢地抗击着死神的暴虐。

就这样继续前行，掠过美丽的岛屿和海岸，直到我们顶着一股湍急的洋流和逆风，在两座塔顶高耸的岩石海角之间破浪而行——他们告诉我，这两座海角，一座立在欧洲，一座立在亚洲，还连着一段久远年代里的旖旎情史。啊！一个女人走到哪里——新大陆也罢，旧大陆也罢——到处都撞见这段老而又老的故事。这是我发现的世界三大洲共通的那一条同情之链。就这样走下去，直到锚链哗啦啦卷过起锚机，好船的铁锚一头扎下几英寻深，落进博斯普鲁斯海峡（Bosphorus）的碧水——它的航程到头了。

===Original===
I do not think that Constantinople impressed me so much as I had expected; and I thought its streets would match those of Navy Bay not unfairly. The caïques, also, of which I had ample experience—for I spent six days here, wandering about Pera and Stamboul in the daytime, and returning to the *Hollander* at nightfall—might be made more safe and commodious for stout ladies, even if the process interfered a little with their ornament. Time and trouble combined have left me with a well-filled-out, portly form—the envy of many an angular Yankee female—and, more than once, it was in no slight danger of becoming too intimately acquainted with the temperature of the Bosphorus. But I will do the Turkish boatmen the justice to say that they were as politely careful of my safety as their astonishment and regard for the well-being of their caïques (which they appear to love as an Arab does his horse, or an Eskimo his dogs, and for the same reason perhaps) would admit. Somewhat surprised, also, seemed the cunning-eyed Greeks, who throng the streets of Pera, at the unprotected Creole woman, who took Constantinople so coolly (it would require something more to surprise her); while the grave English raised their eyebrows wonderingly, and the more vivacious French shrugged their pliant shoulders into the strangest contortions. I accepted it all as a compliment to a stout female tourist, neatly dressed in a red or yellow dress, a plain shawl of some other colour, and a simple straw wide-awake, with bright red streamers. I flatter myself that I woke up sundry sleepy-eyed Turks, who seemed to think that the great object of life was to avoid showing surprise at anything; while the Turkish women gathered around me, and jabbered about me, in the most flattering manner.

How I ever succeeded in getting Mr. Day's letters from the Post-office, Constantinople, puzzles me now; but I did—and I shall ever regard my success as one of the great triumphs of my life. Their contents were not very cheering. He gave a very dreary account of Balaclava and of camp life, and almost dissuaded me from continuing my journey; but his last letter ended by giving me instructions as to the purchases I had best make, if I still determined upon making the adventure; so I forgot all the rest, and busied myself in laying in the stores he recommended.

===Chinese===
君士坦丁堡给我的印象，我看并没有我预想的那么深；依我看，它的街道同海军湾的比一比，也不算太落下风。那些土耳其小划艇（caïque）也是——这方面我经验十足，因为我在这里待了六天，白天在培拉（Pera）和斯坦布尔（Stamboul）四处游逛，日暮时分回「荷兰人号」上去——便是少点装饰，也大可以造得再稳当些、宽绰些，好容得下富态的太太们。岁月同操劳联手，给我留下了一副丰腴富态的身段——叫多少瘦骨嶙峋的北方佬女人眼红——而这身段不止一回，险些同博斯普鲁斯海峡的水温结下太亲密的交情。不过我得为土耳其船夫说句公道话：在他们的惊愕、以及他们对小划艇安康的爱惜（他们爱那船，看来就像阿拉伯人爱他的马、爱斯基摩人爱他的狗，缘故或许也相同）容许的范围之内，他们待我的安全，实在是礼数周到之至。培拉街头熙熙攘攘、眼珠精明的希腊人，对这位把君士坦丁堡看得这样云淡风轻的无人护佑的克里奥尔女人（要惊动她，得来点更大的动静），似乎也颇为讶异；严肃的英国人惊奇地扬起眉毛，活泼些的法国人则把柔韧的肩膀耸成最古怪的形状。这一切，我都当作献给一位富态的女游客的恭维收下了——她穿一身红色或黄色的衣裙，打扮得清清爽爽，披一条别色的素披肩，戴一顶简朴的阔檐草帽（wide-awake），帽上飘着鲜红的帽带。我敢自夸，我惊醒了不少睡眼惺忪的土耳其人——他们似乎认定人生头等大事，便是不为任何事发吃惊之态；土耳其女人们则团团围住我，叽叽喳喳地议论我，那架势奉承极了。

我到底是怎样从君士坦丁堡邮局取出戴先生的信的，如今想来还纳闷；可我取到了——而且我要永远把这份成功列为我生平的大捷之一。信的内容不太提气。他把巴拉克拉瓦和营中生活描得一片凄凉，几乎要劝我别再往下走了；可他最后一封信的结尾，还是交代了我若仍执意冒险、最好采买些什么；于是其余的我一概忘光，一门心思张罗他推荐的那些货色去了。

===Original===
But I found time, before I left the *Hollander*, to charter a crazy caicque, to carry me to Scutari, intending to present Dr. F——'s letter to Miss Nightingale.

It was afternoon when the boatmen set me down in safety at the landing-place of Scutari, and I walked up the slight ascent, to the great dull-looking hospital. Thinking of the many noble fellows who had been borne, or had painfully crept along this path, only to die within that dreary building, I felt rather dull; and directly I entered the hospital, and came upon the long wards of sufferers, lying there so quiet and still, a rush of tears came to my eyes, and blotted out the sight for a few minutes. But I soon felt at home, and looked about me with great interest. The men were, many of them, very quiet. Some of the convalescent formed themselves into little groups around one who read a newspaper; others had books in their hands, or by their side, where they had fallen when slumber overtook the readers, while hospital orderlies moved to and fro, and now and then the female nurses, in their quiet uniform, passed noiselessly on some mission of kindness.

===Chinese===
但离开「荷兰人号」之前，我还匀得出工夫，雇了一条摇摇晃晃的小划艇送我去斯库塔里，打算把 F——医生的引荐信面呈南丁格尔小姐。

船夫们把我平平安安送到斯库塔里码头时，已是下午；我走上那道缓坡，走向那座灰扑扑的大医院。想到多少高贵的汉子曾被抬着、或是一步一挪痛苦地爬过这条小路，只为进到那座阴惨惨的楼里死去，我心里便沉沉的；一进医院，撞见一条条长长的病房里躺着的病人，那样安静、那样一动不动，一股热泪涌上我的眼眶，好几分钟糊住了视线。但我很快就自在起来，兴味十足地四下打量。这些人里，许多都极安静。有些康复期的病人围着一位读报的，围成一小圈一小圈；别的手里拿着书，或是书落在身旁——读着读着睡着了，书就那么滑落；医院的勤务兵来来去去，身穿素净制服的女护士不时悄没声地走过，去办哪一桩善事。

===Original===
I was fortunate enough to find an old acquaintance, who accompanied me through the wards, and rendered it unnecessary for me to trouble the busy nurses. This was an old 97th man—a Sergeant T——, whom I had known in Kingston, and who was slowly recovering from an attack of dysentery, and making himself of use here until the doctors should let him go back and have another "shy at the Rooshians." He is very glad to meet me, and tells me his history very socially, and takes me to the bedsides of some comrades, who had also known me at Up-Park Camp. My poor fellows! how their eyes glisten when they light upon an old friend's face in these Turkish barracks—put to so sad a use, three thousand miles from home. Here is one of them—"hurt in the trenches," says the Sergeant, with shaven bandaged head, and bright, restless, Irish eyes, who hallooes out, "Mother Seacole! Mother Seacole!" in such an excited tone of voice; and when he has shaken hands a score of times, falls back upon his pillow very wearily. But I sit by his side, and try to cheer him with talk about the future, when he shall grow well, and see home, and hear them all thank him for what he has been helping to do, so that he grows all right in a few minutes; but, hearing that I am on the way to the front, gets excited again; for, you see, illness and weakness make these strong men as children, not least in the patient unmurmuring resignation with which they suffer. I think my Irish friend had an indistinct idea of a "muddle" somewhere, which had kept him for weeks on salt meat and biscuit, until it gave him the "scurvy," for he is very anxious that I should take over plenty of vegetables, of every sort. "And, oh! mother!"—and it is strange to hear his almost plaintive tone as he urges this—"take them plenty of eggs, mother; we never saw eggs over there."

At some slight risk of giving offence, I cannot resist the temptation of lending a helping hand here and there—replacing a slipped bandage, or easing a stiff one. But I do not think anyone was offended; and one doctor, who had with some surprise and, at first, alarm on his face, watched me replace a bandage, which was giving pain, said, very kindly, when I had finished, "Thank you, ma'am."

One thought never left my mind as I walked through the fearful miles of suffering in that great hospital. If it is so here, what must it not be at the scene of war—on the spot where the poor fellows are stricken down by pestilence or Russian bullets, and days and nights of agony must be passed before a woman's hand can dress their wounds. And I felt happy in the conviction that *I must* be useful three or four days nearer to their pressing wants than this.

===Chinese===
我运气好，寻到一位旧识，由他领着我在各病房穿行，省得我去搅扰那些忙得团团转的护士。这是第 97 团的一位老人马——T——中士（Sergeant T——），我在金斯敦就认得他；他害了一场痢疾，正在慢慢将养，趁医生放他回营再去「撩一回那帮俄佬」之前，在这里派些用场。他见了我高兴得很，一团热络地给我讲他的经历，又领我到几张病榻前——躺着的也都是在上帕克军营就认得我的人。我可怜的孩子们！在这座被派上这般悲惨用场的土耳其兵营里、在离家 3000 英里的地方，他们一照见一张老朋友的脸，眼睛便怎样地放光啊。这里就有一个——「堑壕里受的伤，」中士说——剃光的头缠着绷带，一双明亮不安分的爱尔兰眼睛，高声喊出来：「西科尔妈妈！西科尔妈妈！」那嗓门激动成那样；握了二十来回手之后，才乏乏地倒回枕上。我在他身边坐下，拿将来的日子宽他的心——说他好起来、回了家，听大家为着他出过力的这桩事业道谢——不出几分钟他便缓过来了；可一听说我正要上前线去，又激动起来；因为你们知道，疾病和虚弱把这些壮汉变回了孩子，尤其是他们受苦时那份不吭一声的忍耐顺从。我想，我这位爱尔兰朋友心里对某处的「一团乱子」有个模模糊糊的影子，就是那乱子叫他们一连几个礼拜嚼咸肉啃饼干，嚼出了「坏血病」；因为他千叮万嘱，要我带足各色蔬菜过去。「还有，哎呀，妈妈！」——他催这一条时，那近乎哀恳的声调听得人心酸——「给他们带足鸡蛋，妈妈；我们在那边从来没见过鸡蛋。」

冒着些许开罪于人的风险，我抵制不了诱惑，这里那里搭一把手——把滑脱的绷带重新缠好，或是把缠得过紧的松上一松。但我看谁也没有见怪；有位医生，先是脸上带着几分惊奇、起初还有点惊慌，看我替一条勒得生痛的绷带换了位置；等我弄完，他很和气地说了一句：「谢谢您，太太。」

走遍那座大医院里令人胆寒的、绵延数英里的苦难，有一个念头再没离开过我：这里尚且如此，到了战争的现场——那可怜人被瘟疫或俄国枪弹击倒的地方，要熬过几天几夜的剧痛，才等得到一双女人的手来裹伤——又该是怎样。而我心中笃定，我*必定*能在比这里近三四天路程的地方、对他们迫在眉睫的需要派上用场，这念头让我快活。

===Original===
It was growing late before I felt tired, or thought of leaving Scutari, and Dr. S——, another Jamaica friend, who had kindly borne me company for the last half-hour agreed with me that the caicque was not the safest conveyance by night on the Bosphorus, and recommended me to present my letter to Miss Nightingale, and perhaps a lodging for the night could be found for me. So, still under the Sergeant's patient guidance, we thread our way through passages and corridors, all used as sick-wards, until we reach the corner tower of the building, in which are the nurses' quarters.

I think Mrs. B——, who saw me, felt more surprise than she could politely show (I never found women so quick to understand me as the men) when I handed her Dr. F——'s kind letter respecting me, and apologized for troubling Miss Nightingale. There is that in the Doctor's letter (he had been much at Scutari) which prevents my request being refused, and I am asked to wait until Miss Nightingale, whose every moment is valuable, can see me. Meanwhile Mrs. B. questions me very kindly, but with the same look of curiosity and surprise.

What object has Mrs. Seacole in coming out? This is the purport of her questions. And I say, frankly, to be of use somewhere; for other considerations I had not, until necessity forced them upon me. Willingly, had they accepted me, I would have worked for the wounded, in return for bread and water. I fancy Mrs. B—— thought that I sought for employment at Scutari, for she said, very kindly—

"Miss Nightingale has the entire management of our hospital staff, but I do not think that any vacancy—"

"Excuse me, ma'am," I interrupt her with, "but I am bound for the front in a few days;" and my questioner leaves me, more surprised than ever. The room I waited in was used as a kitchen. Upon the stoves were cans of soup, broth, and arrowroot, while nurses passed in and out with noiseless tread and subdued manner. I thought many of them had that strange expression of the eyes which those who have gazed long on scenes of woe or horror seldom lose.

===Chinese===
等我觉出乏、想起该离开斯库塔里时，天色已晚；最后半个钟头一直好心陪着我的另一位牙买加朋友 S——医生（Dr. S——）也同意，夜里坐小划艇走博斯普鲁斯海峡实在算不得最稳妥的去处，便劝我把引荐信呈给南丁格尔小姐，兴许还能替我寻一处过夜的地方。于是，仍在那位中士耐心的引领下，我们穿行在一条条过道和长廊之间——它们全都充作了病房——最后到了大楼的角塔，护士们的住处便在那里。

我想，出来见我的 B——夫人（Mrs. B——），心头的惊奇远多过礼数上显示得出的那份（我从不曾发现女人有男人那样快地读懂我）——当我把 F——医生写到我的那封好心的信递给她、为叨扰南丁格尔小姐致歉的时候。医生信里有些话（他在斯库塔里待了很久），叫我的请求无法被拒；他们请我稍候，等南丁格尔小姐——她的每一刻都金贵——得空见我。等候期间，B——夫人很和气地问我话，眼里却仍是那副又好奇又惊奇的神色。

西科尔夫人漂洋过海出来，图的是什么？这便是她那些问题的要旨。而我坦白答道：图的是在哪里派上点用场；别的盘算我原本没有——直到境遇逼上门来才有了。只要他们肯收，我甘愿为伤兵做工，换一口面包一杯白水。我料想 B——夫人以为我是想在斯库塔里谋份差事，因为她很客气地说——

「南丁格尔小姐统管着我们医院的全部护士，可我想眼下并没有空缺——」

「恕我打断您，夫人，」我插话说，「我几天之内就要上前线去了；」于是这位问话的人撇下我走了，比先前更惊奇了。我候见的那间屋子是间厨房。炉灶上坐着一只只汤锅，炖着肉汤、菜汤和竹芋粉糊；护士们脚步无声、举止收敛地进进出出。我看她们许多人眼里都有那种奇异的神情——久久凝望过愁苦或恐怖场面的人，很少能褪掉的那种。

===Original===
In half an hour's time I am admitted to Miss Nightingale's presence. A slight figure, in the nurses' dress; with a pale, gentle, and withal firm face, resting lightly in the palm of one white hand, while the other supports the elbow—a position which gives to her countenance a keen inquiring expression, which is rather marked. Standing thus in repose, and yet keenly observant—the greatest sign of impatience at any time2 a slight, perhaps unwitting motion of the firmly planted right foot—was Florence Nightingale—that Englishwoman whose name shall never die, but sound like music on the lips of British men until the hour of doom.

She has read Dr. F——'s letter, which lies on the table by her side, and asks, in her gentle but eminently practical and businesslike way, "What do you want, Mrs. Seacole—anything that we can do for you? If it lies in my power, I shall be very happy."

So I tell her of my dread of the night journey by caicque, and the improbability of my finding the *Hollander* in the dark; and, with some diffidence, threw myself upon the hospitality of Scutari, offering to nurse the sick for the night. Now unfortunately, for many reasons, room even for one in Scutari Hospital was at that time no easy matter to find; but at last a bed was discovered to be unoccupied at the hospital washerwomen's quarters.

===Chinese===
半个钟头后，我被领到南丁格尔小姐面前。一个纤细的身影，穿着护士的制服；一张苍白、温和、却又坚定的脸，轻轻倚在一只白皙手掌的掌心里，另一只手托着臂肘——这个姿势给她面容添上一副锐利的探询神情，相当显眼。就这样静立着安歇，却依然敏锐地观察四方——她任何时刻最明显的不耐烦迹象2，不过是那只坚定站定的右脚一阵轻微的、或许并不自觉的挪动——这就是弗洛伦斯·南丁格尔——这位英国女人的名字永远不会消亡，它将像音乐一样在英国人的唇边回响，直到末日审判的时辰。

她已读过 F——医生的信，信就搁在她手边的桌上；她用她那温和却又极其务实、公事公办的口吻问：「您想要什么，西科尔夫人——有什么我们能为您效劳的？但凡在我权力之内，我很乐意。」

于是我告诉她，我怕夜里坐小划艇赶路，天一黑也多半寻不见「荷兰人号」；又带着几分忐忑，把自己托付给斯库塔里的款待，主动提出值一夜的班、看护病人。不巧的是，出于种种缘故，那时节斯库塔里医院里连一个人的铺位都不好找；但最后还是发现，医院洗衣妇的住处有一张床空着。

===Original===
My experience of washerwomen, all the world over, is the same—that they are kind softhearted folks. Possibly the soapsuds they almost live in find their way into their hearts and tempers, and soften them. This Scutari washerwoman is no exception to the rule, and welcomes me most heartily. With her, also, are some invalid nurses; and after they have gone to bed, we spend some hours of the night talking over our adventures, and giving one another scraps of our respective biographies. I hadn't long retired to my couch before I wished most heartily that we had continued our chat; for unbidden and most unwelcome companions took the washerwoman's place, and persisted not only in dividing my bed, but my plump person also. Upon my word, I believe the fleas are the only industrious creatures in all Turkey. Some of their relatives would seem to have migrated into Russia; for I found them in the Crimea equally prosperous and ubiquitous.

In the morning, a breakfast is sent to my mangled remains, and a kind message from Mrs. B——, having reference to how I spent the night. And, after an interview with some other medical men, whose acquaintance I had made in Jamaica, I shake hands with the softhearted washerwoman, up to her shoulders in soapsuds already, and start for the *Hollander*.

===Chinese===
普天之下，我对洗衣妇的阅历都是一个样——她们都是心软和善的一路人。兴许是她们差不多日夜泡在其中的肥皂沫，渗进了她们的心和脾气，把它们泡软了。这位斯库塔里的洗衣妇也不出这个规律，热热乎乎地欢迎了我。同她住在一起的，还有几位养病的护士；等她们睡下之后，我们又聊了半夜的奇遇，你一段我一段地交换各自的生平。我上床没多久，就衷心希望我们那场闲谈继续下去才好；因为一群不请自来、极不受欢迎的伙伴，占据了洗衣妇的位置，不但执意要同我分享那张床，还要分享我丰腴的身子。说句实在话，我相信跳蚤是全土耳其仅有的勤勉生灵。它们的一部分亲戚看来还迁居去了俄国；因为在克里米亚，我发现它们同样兴旺，同样无处不在。

第二天早晨，一份早餐送到我这副被折腾得七零八落的残躯跟前，B——夫人还捎来一句关切的问候，问我这一夜过得如何。随后，又会见了几位别的医生——都是我在牙买加结识的熟人——我便同那位心软的洗衣妇道了别（她已泡在肥皂沫里，没到了肩膀），动身回「荷兰人号」去了。

## To the Reader / 致读者

===Original===
I should have thought that no preface would have been required to introduce Mrs. Seacole to the British public, or to recommend a book which must, from the circumstances in which the subject of it was placed, be unique in literature.

If singleness of heart, true charity, and Christian works; if trials and sufferings, dangers and perils, encountered boldly by a helpless woman on her errand of mercy in the camp and in the battlefield, can excite sympathy or move curiosity, Mary Seacole will have many friends and many readers.

===Chinese===
我本以为，要把西科尔夫人介绍给英国公众，本不需要任何序言；要推荐这样一本书，也无需多言——考虑到书中主人公当时所处的境况，它在文学中必然是独一无二的。

倘若赤诚之心、真正的仁爱、基督徒的善行；倘若一位孤立无援的女子，在军营中、在战场上，怀着慈悲使命勇敢迎受的种种磨难与苦楚、危险与艰险——倘若这些能够激起同情、打动好奇之心，那么玛丽·西科尔将会有许多朋友、许多读者。

===Original===
She is no Anna Comnena, who presents us with a verbose history, but a plain truth-speaking woman, who has lived an adventurous life amid scenes which have never yet found a historian among the actors on the stage where they passed.

I have witnessed her devotion and her courage; I have already borne testimony to her services to all who needed them. She is the first who has redeemed the name of "sutler" from the suspicion of worthlessness, mercenary baseness, and plunder; and I trust that England will not forget one who nursed her sick, who sought out her wounded to aid and succour them, and who performed the last offices for some of her illustrious dead.

W. H. Russell.

===Chinese===
她不是安娜·科穆宁娜（Anna Comnena），给我们捧出一部絮絮叨叨的史书；她是一个朴实的、说实话的女人，在种种奇险的经历中生活过——而那些场景，虽曾有演员亲身登台，却还从未出过一位演员之中的历史学家。

我亲眼见证了她的奉献与她的勇气；对于她向一切需要帮助之人提供的援手，我已经作过证。她是第一个为「随军商贩」（sutler）这个名号洗刷嫌疑的人——那嫌疑即无用、唯利是图的卑劣与趁乱劫掠；而且我相信，英国不会忘记这样一个人：她护理过英国的病患，寻访过英国的伤员去扶助救济他们，还为英国几位显赫的亡者料理了最后的身后之事。

W. H. 罗素（W. H. Russell）

## V / V

===Original===
American sympathy—I take an hotel in Cruces—My customers—Lola Montes—Miss Hayes and the Bishop—Gambling in Cruces—Quarrels amongst the travellers—New Granada military—The thieves of Cruces—A narrow escape.

When it became known that their "yellow doctress" had the cholera, I must do the people of Cruces the justice to say that they gave her plenty of sympathy, and would have shown their regard for her more actively, had there been any occasion. Indeed, when I most wanted quiet, it was difficult to keep out the sympathising Americans and sorrowing natives who came to inquire after me; and who, not content with making their inquiries, and leaving their offerings of blankets, flannel, etc., must see with their own eyes what chance the yellow woman had of recovery. The rickety door of my little room could never be kept shut for many minutes together. A visitor would open it silently, poke his long face in with an expression of sympathy that almost made me laugh in spite of my pain, draw it out again, between the narrowest possible opening, as if he were anxious to admit as little air as he could; while another would come in bodily, and after looking at me curiously and inquisitively, as he would eye a horse or nigger he had some thoughts of making a bid for, would help to carpet my room, with the result perhaps of his meditations, and saying, gravely, "Air you better, Aunty Seacole, now? Isn't there a something we can du for you, ma'am?" would as gravely give place to another and another yet, until I was almost inclined to throw something at them, or call them bad names, like the Scotch king does the ghosts in the play.1 But, fortunately, the attack was a very mild one, and by the next day all danger had gone by, although I still felt weak and exhausted.

After a few weeks, the first force of the cholera was spent, and although it lingered with us, as though loath to leave so fine a resting-place, for some months, it no longer gave us much alarm; and before long, life went on as briskly and selfishly as ever with the Cruces survivors, and the terrible past was conveniently forgotten. Perhaps it is so everywhere; but the haste with which the Cruces people buried their memory seemed indecent. Old houses found new masters; the mules new drivers; the great Spaniard chose another pretty woman, and had a grand, poor, dirty wedding, and was married by the same lazy black priest who had buried his wife, dead a few months back; and very likely they would all have hastened as quickly to forget their doctress, had circumstances permitted them: but every now and then one of them sickened and died of the old complaint; and the reputation I had established founded for me a considerable practice. The Americans in the place gladly retained me as their medical attendant, and in one way or other gave me plenty to do; but, in addition to this, I determined to follow my original scheme of keeping an hotel in Cruces.

===Chinese===
美国人的同情——我在克鲁塞斯开旅馆——我的主顾们——洛拉·蒙特斯——海斯小姐与主教——克鲁塞斯的赌风——旅客之间的争斗——新格拉纳达的军警——克鲁塞斯的窃贼——一场虚惊。

当克鲁塞斯人得知他们的「黄肤女医士」染上霍乱时——我必须为克鲁塞斯人说句公道话——他们给了她充分的同情；倘若真有用得着的地方，他们本会更起劲地表达对她的敬意。确实，在我最需要安静的时候，很难把那些满怀同情的美国人和满面悲戚、前来探病的本地人挡在门外；他们光问问还不够，放下毯子、法兰绒等各色馈赠之后，还非要亲眼看看这个黄皮肤女人有几成康复的指望。我那小屋摇摇晃晃的房门，从没有能一连关上几分钟的。一位访客会悄悄把门推开，把他那张长脸探进来，一脸的同情——那神情差点叫我忘了疼痛、笑出声来——再从最窄不过的一条门缝里把脸缩回去，仿佛唯恐多放进一丝空气；另一位则会整个人走进来，好奇地、探究地打量我，就像相看一匹马或者一个黑鬼、盘算着出个价似的，然后把他冥思苦想的产物撒在我的地板上，权当替我铺了地毯，再一本正经地开口：「你今儿个好点了吗，西科尔大婶（Aunty Seacole）？有啥俺们能替你办的没有，太太？」又同样一本正经地给一位、再一位后来者让位，直到我几乎想朝他们扔点什么，或者骂他们几句难听的，就像戏里那位苏格兰国王骂群鬼那样。1所幸我这一场病来势甚轻，到第二天一切危险都已过去，尽管我仍觉得虚弱乏力。

几个星期后，霍乱的第一波凶焰便衰竭了；它虽仍在我们中间流连，仿佛舍不得离开这么好一处歇脚地，一拖数月，却不再叫我们怎样惊慌；不久，克鲁塞斯的幸存者们便照旧热辣辣、自私自利地过起日子来，可怕的往事也被顺顺当当地忘掉了。也许天下处处如此；但克鲁塞斯人埋葬记忆的那份急切，未免有失体面。旧宅子有了新主人；骡子换了新骑手；那位西班牙要人另挑了一位漂亮女人，办了一场场面盛大、穷酸肮脏的婚礼，主婚的正是几个月前埋掉他前妻的那个懒散的黑人神父；倘若境况容许，他们多半也会同样飞快地把他们这位女医士忘掉：但隔三差五，他们中总有人染上旧病死去；于是我挣下的名声，替我撑起了一桩兴旺的营生。当地的美国人乐意留我当他们的医生，变着法儿给我派活干；但除此之外，我还打定主意，要照原先的盘算，在克鲁塞斯开一家旅馆。

===Original===
Right opposite my brother's Independent Hotel there was a place to let which it was considered I could adapt to my purpose. It was a mere tumbledwn hut, with wattled sides, and a rotten thatched roof, containing two rooms, one small enough to serve as a bedroom. For this charming residence—very openly situated, and well ventilated—twenty pounds a month was considered a fair and by no means exorbitant rent. And yet I was glad to take possession of it; and in a few days had hung its rude walls with calico of gayest colour in stripes, with an exuberance of fringes, frills, and bows (the Americans love show dearly), and prepared it to accommodate fifty dinner guests. I had determined that it should be simply a table d'hôte, and that I would receive no lodgers. Once, and once only, I relaxed this rule in favour of two American women, who sent me to sleep by a lengthy quarrel of words, woke me in the night to witness its crisis in a fisticuff duello, and left in the morning, after having taken a fancy to some of my moveables which were most easily removeable. I had on my staff my black servant Mac, the little girl I have before alluded to, and a native cook. I had had many opportunities of seeing how my brother conducted his business; and adopted his tariff of charges. For an ordinary dinner my charge was four shillings; eggs and chickens were, as I have before said, distinct luxuries, and fetched high prices.

Four crowds generally passed through Cruces every month. In these were to be found passengers to and from Chile, Peru, and Lima, as well as California and America. The distance from Cruces to Panama was not great—only twenty miles, in fact; but the journey, from the want of roads and the roughness of the country, was a most fatiguing one. In some parts—as I found when I made the journey, in company with my brother—it was almost impassable; and for more than half the distance, three miles an hour was considered splendid progress. The great majority of the travellers were rough, rude men, of dirty, quarrelsome habits; the others were more civilized and more dangerous. And it was not long before I grew very tired of life in Cruces, although I made money rapidly, and pressed my brother to return to Kingston. Poor fellow! it would have been well for him had he done so; for he stayed only to find a grave on the Isthmus of Panama.

The company at my table d'hôte was not over select; and it was often very difficult for an unprotected female to manage them, although I always did my best to put them in good humour. Among other comforts, I used to hire a black barber, for the rather large consideration of two pounds, to shave my male guests. You can scarcely conceive the pleasure and comfort an American feels in a clean chin; and I believe my barber attracted considerable custom to the British Hotel at Cruces. I had a little outhouse erected for his especial convenience; and there, well provided with towels, and armed with plenty of razors, a brush of extraordinary size, and a foaming sea of lather, José shaved the newcomers. The rivalry to get within reach of his huge brush was very great; and the threats used by the neglected, when the grinning black was considered guilty of any interested partiality, were of the fiercest description.

===Chinese===
正对着我兄弟的独立旅馆（Independent Hotel），有一处待租的房子，人们认为大可以改作我的用途。那不过是座坍败的棚屋，枝条编的墙，霉烂的茅草顶，里头两个房间，一间小得只够当卧房。这样一处迷人的宅邸——地势四敞大开，通风极佳——月租 20 镑，还被认为公道、决不算贵。可我还是乐意把它接了下来；没几天，我就把它粗陋的四壁挂上了条纹最鲜亮的印花布，流苏、褶边、蝴蝶结缀得琳琅满目（美国人顶爱炫耀排场），又把它收拾得能容纳 50 位客人用正餐。我打定主意只做客饭桌（table d'hôte）生意，不收房客。只有一回，仅仅一回，我为两个美国女人破了例；她俩先是一场长篇大论的口角把我吵入了梦乡，半夜又把我叫醒，请我观赏这场口角的最高潮——一场挥拳的决斗——第二天早晨临走时，又看上了我几件最便于搬走的动产，顺手带走了。我手下有我的黑人仆役麦克（Mac）、我前面提到过的那个小姑娘，还有一个本地厨子。兄弟做生意的一套路数我见得多了，便照搬了他的价目。一顿寻常的正餐我收 4 先令；鸡蛋和鸡，如我先前所说，是地地道道的奢侈品，卖得起高价。

每月通常有四拨人潮经过克鲁塞斯。其中可以见到往返智利、秘鲁和利马，以及加州和美国的旅客。克鲁塞斯到巴拿马的路程不算远——其实不过 20 英里；但这段路，因为无路可循、地势崎岖，走起来极其累人。有几段——我同兄弟走这一趟时亲有体会——几乎无法通行；一多半的路程里，一小时走 3 英里已被视为神速。旅客中的大多数是粗野横暴的汉子，习性肮脏，动辄吵闹；其余的更文明些，也更危险。没过多久，我便对克鲁塞斯的生活厌倦透了，尽管我挣钱挣得飞快，于是一再催促兄弟回金斯敦去。可怜的人！他若是回去了，倒好了；因为他留下来，只是为了在巴拿马地峡找一座坟墓。

我那客饭桌上的客人，说不上多么上等；一个无人护佑的女人要辖制住他们，往往十分吃力，尽管我总尽量哄得他们高兴。在种种享受之外，我还惯常雇一名黑人理发师，出颇为可观的一笔酬金——2 镑——替我的男客修面。你几乎无法想象一个美国人下巴干净时那份惬意舒坦；我相信我的理发师为克鲁塞斯的不列颠旅馆招来了不少生意。我专门为他盖了一间小屋，好让他用着方便；在那儿，何塞（José）备足毛巾，手执一摞剃刀、一把硕大无朋的刷子和一片泡沫的海洋，给新到的客人剃脸。众人争先恐后要挤进他那把大刷子的射程，竞争异常激烈；而当这个咧嘴嬉笑的黑人被认定怀了偏心的时候，受了冷落的人们发出的威胁，狠得无以复加。

===Original===
This duty over, they and their coarser female companions—many of them well known to us, for they travelled backwards and forwards across the Isthmus, hanging on to the foolish gold-finders—attacked the dinner, very often with great lack of decency. It was no use giving them carving-knives and forks, for very often they laid their own down to insert a dirty hairy hand into a full dish; while the floor soon bore evidences of the great national American habit of expectoration. Very often quarrels would arise during the progress of dinner; and more than once I thought the knives, which they nearly swallowed at every mouthful, would have been turned against one another. It was, I always thought, extremely fortunate that the reckless men rarely stimulated their excitable passions with strong drink. Tea and coffee were the common beverages of the Americans; Englishmen, and men of other nations, being generally distinguishable by their demand for wine and spirits. But the Yankee's capacity for swilling tea and coffee was prodigious. I saw one man drink ten cups of coffee; and finding his appetite still unsatisfied, I ran across to my brother for advice. There was a merry twinkle in his eyes as he whispered, "I always put in a good spoonful of salt after the sixth cup. It chokes them off admirably."

It was no easy thing to avoid being robbed and cheated by the less scrupulous travellers; although I think it was only the 'cutest Yankee who stood any fair chance of outwitting me. I remember an instance of the biter bit, which I will narrate, hoping it may make my reader laugh as heartily as its recollection makes me. He was a tall, thin Yankee, with a furtive glance of the eyes, and an amazing appetite, which he seemed nothing loath to indulge: his appetite for eggs especially seemed unbounded. Now, I have more than once said how expensive eggs were; and this day they happened to be eightpence apiece. Our plan was to charge every diner according to the number of shells found upon his plate. Now, I noticed how eagerly my thin guest attacked my eggs, and marvelled somewhat at the scanty pile of shells before him. My suspicions once excited, I soon fathomed my Yankee friend's dodge. As soon as he had devoured the eggs, he conveyed furtively the shells beneath the table, and distributed them impartially at the feet of his companions. I gave my little black maid a piece of chalk, and instructions; and creeping under the table, she counted the scattered shells, and chalked the number on the tail of his coat. And when he came up to pay his score, he gave up his number of eggs in a loud voice; and when I contradicted him, and referred to the coat-*tale* in corroboration of *my* score, there was a general laugh against him. But there was a nasty expression in his catlike eyes, and an unpleasant allusion to mine, which were not agreeable, and dissuaded me from playing any more practical jokes upon the Yankees.

I followed my brother's example closely, and forbade all gambling in my hotel. But I got some idea of its fruits from the cases brought to me for surgical treatment from the faro and monte tables. Gambling at Cruces, and on the Isthmus generally, was a business by which money was wormed out of the gold-seekers and gold-finders. No attempt was made to render it attractive, as I have seen done elsewhere. The gambling-house was often plainer than our hotels; and but for the green tables, with their piles of money and gold-dust, watched over by a well-armed determined banker, and the eager gamblers around, you would not know that you were in the vicinity of a spot which the English at home designate by a very decided and extreme name. A Dr. Casey—everybody familiar with the Americans knows their fondness for titles—owned the most favoured table in Cruces; and this, although he was known to be a reckless and unscrupulous villain. Most of them knew that he had been hunted out of San Francisco; and at that time—years before the Vigilance Committee commenced their labours of purification—a man too bad for that city must have been a prodigy of crime: and yet, and although he was violent-tempered, and had a knack of referring the slightest dispute to his revolver, his table was always crowded; probably because—the greatest rogues have some good qualities—he was honest in his way, and played fairly.

===Chinese===
这道工序一完，他们便同他们那些更粗俗的女伴——其中许多我们是熟识的，因为她们在地峡上来回穿行，吊在那些愚蠢的淘金者身上——向正餐发起进攻，那吃相常常大有失体统。给他们上切肉刀叉是白费，因为他们动不动撂下刀叉，把一只脏乎乎、毛茸茸的手直插进满满一盘菜里；地板上很快便留下一摊摊证据，显出美国人那伟大的国民习惯——随地吐痰。席间常常起争执；不止一次，我以为他们每咽一口都险些吞下去的那些刀子，当真会彼此招呼到对方身上。我一向认为万幸的是，这些莽汉极少用烈酒去刺激他们本就一点就着的火气。茶和咖啡是美国人的寻常饮料；英国人和别国的人，一般从他们开口要葡萄酒和烈酒这一点上，便可分辨出来。但北方佬灌茶灌咖啡的能耐实在惊人。我见过一个人连灌了 10 杯咖啡；见他还没吃够，我跑去找兄弟讨教。他眼里闪着狡黠的光，悄声说：「第 6 杯以后我总要加上满满一勺盐。管保把他们呛得服服帖帖。」

要想不被那些不大讲究信义的旅客偷骗，并非易事；不过我想，能有点把握算计过我的，也只有最精明的北方佬了。我记得一桩骗子反被骗的事例，讲出来，盼着它能叫读者笑得像我一想起它来那样痛快。那是个又高又瘦的北方佬，眼珠贼溜溜地转，胃口大得惊人，而且来者不拒：他对鸡蛋的胃口尤其像个无底洞。我前面不止一次说过鸡蛋有多贵；这一天碰巧卖到 8 便士一枚。我们的规矩是，按每位食客盘子里发现的蛋壳数目收钱。我注意到我这位瘦客攻我鸡蛋时的那股急切劲儿，也对他面前那寥寥一摞蛋壳有些诧异。疑心一起，我很快便摸清了这位北方佬朋友的花招。他一吞下鸡蛋，便偷偷把蛋壳递到桌子底下，不偏不倚分撒到同伴们的脚边。我给我的黑人小女仆一支粉笔，外带一番嘱咐；她钻到桌子底下，数清散落的蛋壳，把数目用粉笔写在 他的外套后摆上。等他上前结账，他大声报出自己吃的鸡蛋数；我一反驳他，并援引那截衣摆来佐证*我的*账目，引得众人齐声哄他大笑。但他那双猫眼里有种恶狠狠的神气，还捎带影射了几句我的眼睛，这些都叫人心里不痛快，从此我便打消了再跟北方佬们开这类玩笑的念头。

我处处学我兄弟的样子，严禁在我的旅馆里赌钱。但从那些从法罗牌（faro）和蒙特牌（monte）赌台上送来要我外科处置的伤号身上，我对赌博的恶果也略知了一二。在克鲁塞斯，乃至整个地峡，赌博是一门从寻金的、淘金的人们身上把钱榨出来的生意。没有谁费心把它装点得诱人些，我倒别处见过那样的做法。赌馆常常比我们的旅馆还寒碜；若不是那些铺绿呢的桌子——桌上堆着的钱币和金沙，由一个武器齐全、意志坚决的庄家看守着——以及围在四周的两眼放光的赌客，你根本不会知道自己正置身于一个地方的附近，一个英国人在本土要用一个非常决绝、非常极端的字眼来称呼的地方。一位凯西医生（Dr. Casey）——凡熟悉美国人的，都知道他们多么热衷头衔——开着克鲁塞斯最走红的一张赌台；尽管尽人皆知他是个无所顾忌、不讲信义的恶棍，也照样如此。他们多数人都知道，他是被人从旧金山（San Francisco）撵出来的；在那个年头——离治安委员会（Vigilance Committee）开始他们净化地方的大业还有好些年——一个坏得连那座城市都容不下的家伙，必定是罪恶堆里的一位奇才：然而，尽管他脾气暴烈，动不动就把最轻微的口角交给左轮枪去裁决，他的赌台却始终人满为患；大概是因为——最大的恶棍也总有几分好处——他自有一份诚实，赌得公道。

===Original===
Occasionally some distinguished passengers passed on the upward and downward tides of rascality and ruffianism, that swept periodically through Cruces. Came one day, Lola Montes, in the full zenith of her evil fame, bound for California, with a strange suite. A good-looking, bold woman, with fine, bad eyes, and a determined bearing; dressed ostentatiously in perfect male attire, with shirt-collar turned down over a velvet lapelled coat, richly worked shirtfront, black hat, French unmentionables, and natty, polished boots with spurs. She carried in her hand a handsome riding-whip, which she could use as well in the streets of Cruces as in the towns of Europe; for an impertinent American, presuming—perhaps not unnaturally—upon her reputation, laid hold jestingly of the tails of her long coat, and as a lesson received a cut across his face that must have marked him for some days. I did not wait to see the row that followed, and was glad when the wretched woman rode off on the following morning. A very different notoriety followed her at some interval of time—Miss Catherine Hayes, on her successful singing tour, who disappointed us all by refusing to sing at Cruces; and after her came an English bishop from Australia, who need have been a member of the church militant to secure his pretty wife from the host of admirers she had gained during her day's journey from Panama.

Very quarrelsome were the majority of the crowds, holding life cheap, as all bad men strangely do—equally prepared to take or lose it upon the slightest provocation. Few tales of horror in Panama could be questioned on the ground of improbability. Not less partial were many of the natives of Cruces to the use of the knife; preferring, by the way, to administer sly stabs in the back, when no one was by to see the dastard blow dealt. Terribly bullied by the Americans were the boatmen and muleteers, who were reviled, shot, and stabbed by these free and independent filibusters, who would fain whop all creation abroad as they do their slaves at home. Whenever any Englishmen were present, and in a position to interfere with success, this bullying was checked; and they found, instead of the poor Spanish Indians, foemen worthy of their steel or lead. I must do them credit to say, that they were never loath to fight anyone that desired that passing excitement, and thought little of ending their journey of life abruptly at the wretched wayside town of Cruces. It very often happened so, and over many a hasty head and ready hand have I seen the sod roughly pressed down, their hot hearts stilled suddenly in some senseless quarrel. And so in time I grew to have some considerable experience in the treatment of knife and gunshot wounds.

One night I heard a great noise outside my window, and on rising found a poor boatman moaning piteously, and in a strange jumble of many languages begging me to help him. At first I was afraid to open the door, on account of the noisy mob which soon joined him, for villainy was very shrewd at Cruces; but at last I admitted him, and found that the poor wretch's ears had been cruelly split by some hasty citizen of the United States. I stitched them up as well as I could, and silenced his cries. And at any time, if you happened to be near the river when a crowd were arriving or departing, your ears would be regaled with a choice chorus of threats, of which earsplitting, eye-gouging, cow-hiding, and the application of revolvers were the mildest. Against the negroes, of whom there were many in the Isthmus, and who almost invariably filled the municipal offices, and took the lead in every way, the Yankees had a strong prejudice; but it was wonderful to see how freedom and equality elevate men, and the same negro who perhaps in Tennessee would have cowered like a beaten child or dog beneath an American's uplifted hand, would face him boldly here, and by equal courage and superior physical strength cow his old oppressor.

===Chinese===
偶尔，也有几位显赫的旅客，乘着定期扫过克鲁塞斯的那股奸邪与凶悍的潮水，随涨随落地上下来去。有一天来了个洛拉·蒙特斯（Lola Montes），正值她恶名鼎盛的顶点，要往加州去，带着一队古怪的随从。是个相貌不错、大胆泼辣的女人，一双好看的坏眼睛，一副敢作敢当的气派；招摇地穿着一身地道的男装：衬衫领子翻在丝绒驳领的上衣外面，绣工华丽的衬衫前胸，黑色礼帽，法国式的下装，还有擦得锃亮的时髦马靴，靴上带着马刺。她手里执一柄漂亮的马鞭，在克鲁塞斯的街上使得，在欧洲的城里也一样使得；因为一个无礼的美国人——也许不算太没来由——仗着她的名声，嬉皮笑脸地伸手去拽她长外套的下摆，当场领了一顿教训，脸上挨的那一鞭，没几天消不掉印子。我没等着看随后那场热闹；第二天早晨那个可怜的女人骑马走了，我倒松了口气。隔了一阵子，一位名声截然不同的角色接踵而至——凯瑟琳·海斯小姐（Miss Catherine Hayes），正在她大获成功的巡回演唱途中，却在克鲁塞斯拒绝登台，叫我们大家大失所望；她之后又来了一位从澳大利亚来的英国主教，这位主教若非有战斗教会成员的本事，实在难以保住他那位漂亮的太太——她从巴拿马来的一日旅程里，已经赢得了一大群爱慕者。

这帮人大多数都极好斗，把性命看得不值钱，正如一切坏人不解地那样——随时准备为一点最小的口角去取人性命，或者送掉自己的。在巴拿马，几乎没有几桩骇人听闻的故事会因「不像真的」而遭人质疑。克鲁塞斯的许多本地人同样偏爱用刀；而且说句题外话，他们更乐意趁四下无人时从背后阴恻恻地捅上一记，教人看不见那怯懦的一击是怎么出的手。船夫和骡夫们受尽了美国人的欺凌，被这些「自由而独立的」不法冒险家（filibuster）辱骂、枪击、刀捅——这帮人在国外恨不得像在家里抽自己的黑奴那样，把普天之下的众生统统抽上一顿。但凡有英国人在场、又有力量出面干预成功时，这种欺凌便被刹住；那些美国人遇到的，便不再是可怜的西班牙-印第安人，而是配得上他们刀枪的对手。我必须为英国人说句公道话：他们从不推辞同任何想要这等一时之快的人过招，对把自己的生命旅程猝然了结在克鲁塞斯这等可怜的路边小镇上，也不怎么放在心上。这样的事常常当真发生，我多少次亲眼看着草皮草草压上那颗急躁的脑袋和那只莽撞的手——他们滚烫的心，在某个毫无意义的争执里骤然静止。就这样，我渐渐在刀伤和枪伤的治疗上，积起了相当可观的经验。

一天夜里，我听见窗外一阵大乱，起身一看，是个可怜的船夫在哀哀呻吟，用好几种语言搅成一锅的话求我救他。起初我不敢开门，因为很快就围上来一群吵吵嚷嚷的人——在克鲁塞斯，歹徒精着呢；但我终于还是放他进来了，发现这可怜虫的双耳被某位性急的美国公民残忍地豁开了。我尽力替他把耳朵缝好，止住他的哭喊。而且随便什么时候，你若恰好在河边碰上一批人抵达或出发，你的耳朵保准能饱享一场精选的威胁大合唱，其中撕耳朵、剜眼睛、牛皮鞭伺候、外加左轮枪上阵，还算是最温和的。对地峡上为数众多的黑人——他们几乎无一例外地把持着大小市政职位，样样都领头——北方佬抱有强烈的偏见；但看着自由与平等如何把人抬举起来，真叫人称奇：同一个黑人，在田纳西（Tennessee）兴许会在美国人扬起的手掌底下，像挨了打的孩子或狗一样缩成一团，在这里却敢同他针锋相对，凭着同等的胆气和更胜一筹的筋骨，把昔日的压迫者镇住。

===Original===
When more than ordinary squabbles occurred in the street or at the gambling-tables, the assistance of the soldier-police of New Granada was called in, and the affair sometimes assumed the character of a regular skirmish. The soldiers—I wish I could speak better of them—were a dirty, cowardly, indolent set, more prone to use their knives than their legitimate arms, and bore old rusty muskets, and very often marched unshod. Their officers were in outward appearance a few shades superior to the men they commanded, but, as respects military proficiency, were their equals. Add to this description of their *personnel* the well-known fact, that you might commit the grossest injustice, and could obtain the simplest justice only by lavish bribery, and you may form some idea of our military protectors.

Very practised and skilful in thieving were the native population of Cruces—I speak of the majority, and except the negroes—always more inclined to do a dishonest night's labour at great risk, than an honest day's work for fair wages; for justice was always administered strictly to the poor natives—it was only the foreigners who could evade it or purchase exemption. Punishment was severe; and in extreme cases the convicts were sent to Carthagena, there to suffer imprisonment of a terrible character. Indeed, from what I heard of the New Granada prisons, I thought no other country could match them, and continued to think so until I read how the ingenuity in cruelty of his Majesty the King of Naples put the torturers of the New Granada Republic to the blush.

I generally avoided claiming the protection of the law whilst on the Isthmus, for I found it was—as is the case in civilized England from other causes—rather an expensive luxury. Once only I took a thief caught in the act before the alcalde, and claimed the administration of justice. The courthouse was a low bamboo shed, before which some dirty Spanish-Indian soldiers were lounging; and inside, the alcalde, a negro, was reclining in a dirty hammock, smoking coolly, hearing evidence, and pronouncing judgment upon the wretched culprits, who were trembling before his dusky majesty. I had attended him while suffering from an attack of cholera, and directly he saw me he rose from his hammock, and received me in a ceremonious, grand manner, and gave orders that coffee should be brought to me. He had a very pretty white wife, who joined us; and then the alcalde politely offered me a *cigarito*—having declined which, he listened to my statement with great attention. All this, however, did not prevent my leaving the necessary fee in furtherance of justice, nor his accepting it. Its consequence was, that the thief, instead of being punished as a criminal, was ordered to pay me the value of the stolen goods; which, after weeks of hesitation and delay, she eventually did, in pearls, combs, and other curiosities.

===Chinese===
每当街上或赌台上起了寻常口角之外的乱子，就得请新格拉纳达的士兵警察来帮忙，事情有时便演成一场正经的小规模交火。那些士兵——我但愿能把他们说得好些——是一群肮脏、怯懦、懒惰的货色，比起他们名正言顺的武器来更爱用刀，扛着锈迹斑斑的老式火枪，还常常赤着脚行军。他们的军官，外表上比他们所指挥的人强出那么几分颜色，但论军事本事，同他们的部下半斤八两。再往这幅「人员」的图景上添一笔人所共知的事实——你可以犯下最恶劣的不公，却只有靠大把行贿，才讨得到最简单的公道——你便可对我们这些军事保护者有个大概的印象了。

克鲁塞斯的本地居民——我说的是多数人，黑人除外——偷窃的功夫十分老到娴熟；他们总是宁愿冒着大风险去做一夜不老实的营生，也不愿领公道的工钱干一天老实的活；因为对穷苦的本地人，法律向来严格执行——只有外国人躲得过它，或花钱买到豁免。刑罚很重；情节严重的，苦役犯会被送往卡塔赫纳（Carthagena），在那儿服一种性质可怕的监禁。说真的，凭我对新格拉纳达监狱的耳闻，我曾以为再没有哪个国家比得上它们，并且一直这么想，直到我读到那不勒斯国王陛下那残暴的巧思妙想，如何叫新格拉纳达共和国的行刑者们羞得满面通红。

在地峡期间，我一般不去求法律的保护，因为我发现它——正如文明英国的法律出于别的原因那样——是一种相当昂贵的奢侈品。只有一回，我把一个现行的小偷扭送到镇长（alcalde）面前，要求执法。法院是一座低矮的竹棚，棚前歪着几个肮脏的西班牙-印第安士兵；棚里，黑人镇长正歪在肮脏的吊床上，悠悠地抽着烟，听着证词，对那些在他黢黑的威严面前发抖的可怜罪人宣着判。他害霍乱发作时我照看过他，一看见我，他便从吊床上起身，礼数周全、气派堂皇地接待我，吩咐人给我上咖啡。他有一位很标致的白人太太，也过来作陪；随后镇长客气地敬我一支香烟（*cigarito*）——我谢绝了——他便聚精会神地听我陈述。然而这一切，并不妨碍我留下那笔推动正义所必需的规费，也不妨碍他把它收下。结果是：那小偷没被当作罪犯惩办，倒被判赔偿我被盗货物的价值；拖拖拉拉、犹犹豫豫了几个星期之后，她总算赔了，赔的是珍珠、梳子，还有别的一些稀罕玩意儿。

===Original===
Whenever an American was arrested by the New Granada authorities, justice had a hard struggle for the mastery, and rarely obtained it. Once I was present at the courthouse, when an American was brought in heavily ironed, charged with having committed a highway robbery—if I may use the term where there were no roads—on some travellers from Chile. Around the frightened soldiers swelled an angry crowd of brother Americans, abusing and threatening the authorities in no measured terms, all of them indignant that a nigger should presume to judge one of their countrymen. At last their violence so roused the sleepy alcalde, that he positively threw himself from his hammock, laid down his *cigarito*, and gave such very determined orders to his soldiers that he succeeded in checking the riot. Then, with an air of decision that puzzled everybody, he addressed the crowd, declaring angrily, that since the Americans came the country had known no peace, that robberies and crimes of every sort had increased, and ending by expressing his determination to make strangers respect the laws of the Republic, and to retain the prisoner; and if found guilty, punish him as he deserved. The Americans seemed too astonished at the audacity of the black man, who dared thus to beard them, to offer any resistance; but I believe that the prisoner was allowed ultimately to escape.

I once had a narrow escape from the thieves of Cruces. I had been down to Chagres for some stores, and returning, late in the evening, too tired to put away my packages, had retired to rest at once. My little maid, who was not so fatigued as I was, and slept more lightly, woke me in the night to listen to a noise in the thatch, at the further end of the store; but I was so accustomed to hear the half-starved mules of Cruces munching my thatch, that I listened lazily for a few minutes, and then went unsuspiciously into another heavy sleep. I do not know how long it was before I was again awoke by the child's loud screams and cries of "*Hombro—landro*;" and sure enough, by the light of the dying fire, I saw a fellow stealing away with my dress, in the pocket of which was my purse. I was about to rush forward, when the fire gleamed on a villainous-looking knife in his hand; so I stood still, and screamed loudly, hoping to arouse my brother over the way. For a moment the thief seemed inclined to silence me, and had taken a few steps forward, when I took up an old rusty horse-pistol which my brother had given me that I might look determined, and snatching down the can of ground coffee, proceeded to prime it, still screaming as loudly as my strong lungs would permit, until the rascal turned tail and stole away through the roof. The thieves usually buried their spoil like dogs, as they were; but this fellow had only time to hide it behind a bush, where it was found on the following morning, and claimed by me.

===Chinese===
每逢美国人被新格拉纳达当局逮捕，正义总要费好一番争斗才能占上风，而且十有八九占不了。有一回我正在法院里，眼见一个美国人戴着沉重的镣铐被押了进来，罪名是对几个智利旅客犯下了拦路抢劫——既然那里无路可拦，这个词还请容我借用。吓坏了的士兵们四周，鼓噪着一群怒气冲冲的美国同胞，用毫无节制的字眼辱骂当局、威胁当局，个个义愤填膺：一个黑鬼竟敢审判他们的同胞。最后，他们的横暴终于把打瞌睡的镇长激得当真从吊床上一跃而起，撂下他的香烟，向士兵们下达了态度无比坚决的命令，居然把骚乱压了下去。然后，他带着一副让所有人摸不着头脑的决断神气，向人群讲话，怒气冲冲地宣告：自从这些美国人来了，这个国家就没有过一天安生；抢劫和各式罪案一概有增无减；最后他表示，他决心让外客尊重共和国的法律，要把犯人继续关押，若查明有罪，必按其应得惩处。美国人似乎被这个黑人的胆大妄为惊呆了——他竟敢当面捋他们的虎须——一时竟没想起来抵抗；不过我相信，那犯人最终还是被放跑了。

有一回，我差点吃了克鲁塞斯窃贼的亏。我到查格雷斯去采买了一批货，回来的当晚累得很，包裹都没来得及收拾，倒头便睡下了。我的小女仆没有我那么乏，觉也轻，半夜里把我叫醒，让我听店铺那一头茅草顶上的响动；可我听惯了克鲁塞斯那些半饥不饱的骡子嚼我的茅草，只懒懒听了几分钟，便毫无戒心地又沉入了另一场酣睡。不知过了多久，孩子高声的尖叫和「*Hombro*——*landro*」（「有贼」）的喊叫又把我惊醒；果然，借着将熄的火光，我看见一条汉子正偷了我的连衣裙溜走，裙子的口袋里装着我的钱袋。我正要冲上去，火光一闪，照亮了他手里一把凶相毕露的刀；我便站定不动，放声尖叫，指望把对门的兄弟吵醒。那贼有一瞬间似乎想叫我闭嘴，已经向前跨出了几步；这时我抓起兄长给我的一把老旧生锈的大手枪——给我原是为了叫我看上去有几分狠气——又一把摘下那罐磨好的咖啡粉，动手往枪膛里装填，一面用我这副强健肺腑所能发出的最大力气继续尖叫，直到那泼才掉头转向，从屋顶上溜之大吉。窃贼们惯常像狗一样——他们本就是狗——把赃物刨土埋掉；可这家伙只来得及把它藏进一丛灌木后面，第二天一早，赃物就在那儿被找到，由我认领了回来。

## VI / VI

===Original===
Migration to Gorgona—Farewell dinners and speeches—A building speculation—Life in Gorgona—Sympathy with American slaves—Dr. Casey in trouble—Floods and fires—Yankee independence and freedom.

I remained at Cruces until the rainy months came to an end, and the river grew too shallow to be navigable by the boats higher up than Gorgona; and then we all made preparations for a flitting to that place. But before starting, it appeared to be the custom for the store and hotel keepers to exchange parting visits, and to many of these parties I, in virtue of my recent services to the community, received invitations. The most important social meeting took place on the anniversary of the declaration of American independence, at my brother's hotel, where a score of zealous Americans dined most heartily—as they never fail to do; and, as it was an especial occasion, drank champagne liberally at twelve shillings a bottle. And, after the usual patriotic toasts had been duly honoured, they proposed "the ladies," with an especial reference to myself, in a speech which I thought worth noting down at the time. The spokesman was a thin, sallow-looking American, with a pompous and yet rapid delivery, and a habit of turning over his words with his quid before delivering them, and clearing his mouth after each sentence, perhaps to make room for the next. I shall beg the reader to consider that the blanks express the time expended on this operation. He dashed into his work at once, rolling up and getting rid of his sentences as he went on:—

"Well, gentlemen, I expect you'll all support me in a drinking of this toast that I du—. Aunty Seacole, gentlemen; I give you, Aunty Seacole—. We can't du less for her, after what she's done for us—, when the cholera was among us, gentlemen—, not many months ago—. So, I say, God bless the best yaller woman He ever made—, from Jamaica, gentlemen—, from the Isle of Springs—Well, gentlemen, I expect there are tu things we're vexed for—; and the first is, that she ain't one of us—, a citizen of the great United States—; and the other thing is, gentlemen—, that Providence made her a yaller woman. I calculate, gentlemen, you're all as vexed as I am that she's not wholly white—, but I du reckon on your rejoicing with me that she's so many shades removed from being entirely black—; and I guess, if we could bleach her by any means we would—, and thus make her as acceptable in any company as she deserves to be—. Gentlemen, I give you Aunty Seacole!"

===Chinese===
迁居戈尔戈纳——告别宴与演讲——一场营造生意——戈尔戈纳的生活——对美国黑奴的同情——凯西医生落难——水灾与火灾——北方佬的独立与自由。

我在克鲁塞斯一直待到雨季结束、河水浅得再也无法让船只上溯到戈尔戈纳以上，这时我们大家便都张罗着迁往那地方去。但动身之前，照着惯例，店主和旅店老板们要互相辞行拜望；这些聚会，承蒙我新近为地方上效过劳，许多都请了我。最要紧的一场社交聚会，是在我兄弟的旅馆里庆贺美国独立宣言的周年纪念，二十来个热忱的美国人放开肚皮大吃大喝——这种事他们从不失手；又因为是个特殊的日子，香槟畅饮，每瓶 12 先令。例行爱国祝酒一一敬过之后，他们提议敬「女士们」，还特别提到了我，那篇致辞我当时就觉得值得记下来。发言人是个瘦削、面色蜡黄的美国人，说话装腔作势却又急如连珠，还有个习惯：开口之前，先把他的字句含在嚼烟里翻搅一阵，每讲完一句便清理一回口腔——也许是给下一句腾地方。我要请读者体谅：文中那些空白，便是这番工夫所耗的时间。他一上来便一头扎进活计，一路把句子卷起来、甩出去：——

「各位先生，俺想你们都会支持俺来饮这杯俺要敬的酒——。各位先生，西科尔大婶；俺敬你们西科尔大婶——。为她为俺们做过的事，俺们不能做得再少了——，霍乱在俺们中间那阵子，各位先生——，也就几个月前——。所以俺说，愿上帝保佑祂造出来的最好的黄皮肤女人——，牙买加来的，各位先生——，泉水之岛（Isle of Springs）来的——各位先生，俺想俺们只有两桩事憋屈——；头一桩，她不是俺们中的一员——，不是伟大的合众国的公民——；另一桩是，各位先生——，天意把她造了个黄皮肤的女人。俺琢磨着，各位先生，她不是全白，你们都跟俺一样憋屈——；可俺也指望着你们同俺一道欢喜：她离全黑还隔着好几个色阶——；俺估摸着，只要有法子把她漂白，俺们准漂——，好让她无论在哪路人跟前，都体面得跟她应得的一样——。各位先生，俺敬你们西科尔大婶！」

===Original===
And so the orator sat down amidst much applause. It may be supposed that I did not need much persuasion to return thanks, burning, as I was, to tell them my mind on the subject of my colour. Indeed, if my brother had not checked me, I should have given them my thoughts somewhat too freely. As it was, I said:—

"Gentlemen—I return you my best thanks for your kindness in drinking my health. As for what I have done in Cruces, Providence evidently made me to be useful, and I can't help it. But, I must say, that I don't altogether appreciate your friend's kind wishes with respect to my complexion. If it had been as dark as any nigger's, I should have been just as happy and as useful, and as much respected by those whose respect I value; and as to his offer of bleaching me, I should, even if it were practicable, decline it without any thanks. As to the society which the process might gain me admission into, all I can say is, that, judging from the specimens I have met with here and elsewhere, I don't think that I shall lose much by being excluded from it. So, gentlemen, I drink to you and the general reformation of American manners."

I do not think that they altogether admired my speech, but I was a somewhat privileged person, and they laughed at it good-naturedly enough. Perhaps (for I was not in the best humour myself) I should have been better pleased if they had been angry.

===Chinese===
演讲人就这样在一片掌声中落了座。可以想见，我不必谁来怂恿便起身答谢——我正憋着一团火，想把我对我的肤色这桩题目的想法一吐为快。确实，若不是我兄弟拦着，我那番心里话只怕要说得太过自由了。我最终是这样说的：——

「各位先生——诸位好意祝我健康，我谨致以最诚挚的谢意。至于我在克鲁塞斯做的事，天意显然是要造我来派用场的，我也无可奈何。不过我必须说，你们那位朋友关于我肤色的那番好意，我实在消受不起。就算它黑得像任何一个黑鬼，我照样会快活，照样有用场，照样受我所敬重的人们的敬重；至于他要替我漂白的那番好意，就算办得到，我也会毫领情都谈不上地谢绝。至于那道工序可能让我挤进去的社交圈子，我只想说：从我在这里和别处见过的那些样本看来，被它关在门外，我想我也损失不了什么。所以，各位先生，我敬诸位，也敬美国风气的全面改良。」

我看他们并不怎么欣赏我这篇答辞，但我多少算个有特权的人，他们听罢也都挺和气地笑了。也许是因为（我自己当时心境也不大佳）他们若是恼了，我倒更称心些。

===Original===
Rightly, I ought to have gone down to Gorgona a few weeks before Cruces was deserted, and secured an hotel; but I did not give up all hope of persuading my brother to leave the Isthmus until the very last moment, and then, of course, a suitable house was not to be hired in Gorgona for love or money. Seeing his fixed determination to stay, I consented to remain with him, for he was young and often ill, and set hard to work to settle myself somewhere. With the aid of an old Jamaica friend, who had settled at Gorgona, I at last found a miserable little hut for sale, and bought it for a hundred dollars. It consisted of one room only, and was, in its then condition, utterly unfit for my purpose; but I determined to set to work and build on to it—by no means the hazardous speculation in Gorgona, where bricks and mortar are unknown, that it is in England. The alcalde's permission to make use of the adjacent ground was obtained for a moderate consideration, and plenty of material was procurable from the opposite bank of the river. An American, whom I had cured of the cholera at Cruces, lent me his boat, and I hired two or three natives to cut down and shape the posts and bamboo poles. Directly these were raised, Mac and my little maid set to work and filled up the spaces between them with split bamboo canes and reeds, and before long my new hotel was ready to be roofed. The building process was simple enough, and I soon found myself in possession of a capital dining-room some thirty feet in length, which was gaily hung with coloured calico, concealing all defects of construction, and lighted with large oil lamps; a storeroom, bar, and a small private apartment for ladies. Altogether, although I had to pay my labourers four shillings a day, the whole building did not cost me more than my brother paid for three months' rent of his hotel. I gave the travelling world to understand that I intended to devote my establishment principally to the entertainment of ladies, and the care of those who might fall ill on the route, and I found the scheme answered admirably. And yet, although the speculation paid well, I soon grew as weary of my life in Gorgona as I had been at Cruces; and when I found my brother proof against all persuasion to quit the Isthmus, I began to entertain serious thoughts of leaving him.

Nor was it altogether my old roving inclination which led me to desire a change, although I dare say it had something to do with it. My present life was not agreeable for a woman with the least delicacy or refinement; and of female society I had none. Indeed, the females who crossed my path were about as unpleasant specimens of the fair sex as one could well wish to avoid. With very few exceptions, those who were not bad were very disagreeable, and as the majority came from the Southern States of America, and showed an instinctive repugnance against anyone whose countenance claimed for her kindred with their slaves, my position was far from a pleasant one. Not that it ever gave me any annoyance; they were glad of my stores and comforts, I made money out of their wants; nor do I think our bond of connection was ever closer; only this, if any of them came to me sick and suffering (I say this out of simple justice to myself), I forgot everything, except that she was my sister, and that it was my duty to help her.

===Chinese===
按说，我本该赶在克鲁塞斯人去楼空的几个星期之前先下戈尔戈纳去，把旅馆占下来；可直到最后一刻，我都没有放弃劝兄弟离开地峡的希望，而到那时候，合适的房子自然是出多大的价钱、说多软的话也租不到了。见他留下的决心已定，我便同意陪他留一阵——他还年轻，又时常生病——随即卖力气给自己找落脚处。靠着一位定居戈尔戈纳的牙买加旧友的帮忙，我终于寻到一间待售的破烂小棚屋，花 100 元买了下来。它统共只有一间房，照当时那副样子，全然不合我的用途；但我打定主意动手往上加盖——在戈尔戈纳，这决不是在英国那样冒险的营生，这里根本没人知道砖头和灰浆为何物。花一笔适中的酬金，便取得了镇长的许可，可以使用毗邻的空地；材料呢，河对岸有的是。一位我在克鲁塞斯治好过霍乱的美国人借给我他的船，我又雇了两三个本地人砍削立柱和竹竿。柱子一竖起来，麦克和我的小女仆便动手，用劈开的竹片和芦苇把柱间的空档填严；不多时，我的新旅馆就可以上顶了。营造的工序简单得很，很快我便坐拥一间长约 30 英尺的一流餐厅，四壁挂满鲜亮的印花布，把施工上的一切毛病遮得严严实实，点着几盏大油灯；另有一间货房、一间酒柜，还有一间供女客用的小雅间。统共算下来，虽然我每天要付工人 4 先令，整座房子的花费也没超过兄弟付他旅馆三个月的租金。我向旅行的世人放出话去：我这家店主要用来接待女客，照料路上可能病倒的人；我发现这盘算应验得漂亮极了。然而，尽管这桩营生进账可观，我不久便像在克鲁塞斯一样，对戈尔戈纳的日子厌倦起来；待我看出兄弟对一切劝他离开地峡的说辞都油盐不进，我便开始认真盘算离开他了。

倒也并不全是我那老游荡癖叫我起意换换地方，尽管我敢说它多少也有一份。我眼下的生活，对一个哪怕只有一丝雅致或教养的女人来说都不惬意；女伴的交往，我是一概没有。的确，从我眼前晃过的那些女人，差不多就是一个人巴不得躲开的阴性标本里最不像话的。除了寥寥几个例外，不坏的都极其难缠；她们大多数来自美国南方各州，对任何一张主张同她们的奴隶沾亲带故的面孔，都怀着本能的憎恶，所以我的处境实在说不上愉快。倒不是说这给过我什么烦恼；她们乐得享用我的存货和便利，我从她们的需求里挣钱；我们之间的纽带也从未比这更亲密过；只有这一条——她们中无论谁带着病痛来找我（我说这话纯粹是出于对自己的公道），我便什么都忘了，只记得她是我的姐妹，帮助她是我的本分。

===Original===
I may have before said that the citizens of the New Granada Republic had a strong prejudice against all Americans. It is not difficult to assign a cause for this. In the first place, many of the negroes, fugitive from the Southern States, had sought refuge in this and the other States of Central America, where every profession was open to them; and as they were generally superior men—evinced perhaps by their hatred of their old condition and their successful flight—they soon rose to positions of eminence in New Granada. In the priesthood, in the army, in all municipal offices, the self-liberated negroes were invariably found in the foremost rank; and the people, for some reason—perhaps because they recognised in them superior talents for administration—always respected them more than, and preferred them to, their native rulers. So that, influenced naturally by these freed slaves, who bore themselves before their old masters bravely and like men, the New Granada people were strongly prejudiced against the Americans. And in the second and third places, they feared their quarrelsome, bullying habits—be it remembered that the crowds to California were of the lowest sorts, many of whom have since fertilised Cuban and Nicaraguan soil—and dreaded their schemes for annexation. To such an extent was this amusingly carried, that when the American Railway Company took possession of Navy Bay, and christened it Aspinwall, after the name of their Chairman, the native authorities refused to recognise their right to name any portion of the Republic, and pertinaciously returned all letters directed to Aspinwall, with "no such place known" marked upon them in the very spot for which they were intended. And, in addition to this, the legal authorities refused to compel any defendant to appear who was described as of Aspinwall, and put every plaintiff out of court who described himself as residing in that unrecognised place.

Under these circumstances, my readers can easily understand that when any Americans crossed the Isthmus, accompanied by their slaves, the Cruces and Gorgona people were restlessly anxious to whisper into their ears offers of freedom and hints how easy escape would be. Nor were the authorities at all inclined to aid in the recapture of a runaway slave. So that, as it was necessary for the losers to go on with the crowd, the fugitive invariably escaped. It is one of the maxims of the New Granada constitution—as it is, I believe, of the English—that on a slave touching its soil his chains fall from him. Rather than irritate so dangerous a neighbour as America, this rule was rarely supported; but I remember the following instance of its successful application.

===Chinese===
我前面也许说过，新格拉纳达共和国的公民对一切美国人抱有强烈的偏见。这其中的缘由不难指认。其一，许多从南方各州逃亡出来的黑人，在这个和中美洲其他国家寻得了避难所，那里各行各业都对他们敞开；他们又多半是出类拔萃之辈——他们对旧日处境的憎恨和成功的出逃，或许便是明证——不久便在新格拉纳达升到了显赫的位置。在神职界、在军队、在一切市政职位上，这些自我解放的黑人无一例外位居最前列；这里的民众出于某种缘故——也许因为他们看出了这些人出众的行政才干——敬重他们总胜过、也更愿意选择他们，而不是自己的本地官长。就这样，在这些于旧主人面前挺直腰杆、堂堂正正做人的自由黑奴的天然熏陶下，新格拉纳达民众对美国人抱有强烈的偏见。其二其三，他们畏惧美国人那好斗逞凶的习性——要知道，涌往加州的那些人渣属于最下等的一类，其中不少人后来都拿自己去肥了古巴和尼加拉瓜的泥土——也惧怕他们那套兼并的图谋。这事发展到了很好笑的地步：美国铁路公司接管海军湾，照他们董事长的姓氏给它施洗命名阿斯平沃尔（Aspinwall），本地当局却拒不承认他们有给共和国任何一角土地命名的权利，还固执地把所有寄往阿斯平沃尔的信件原路退回，在信封上原本该写收件地名的地方盖上「查无此地」的戳子。不但如此，司法当局还拒绝传唤任何被写明住所在阿斯平沃尔的被告出庭，凡自称居于这个不获承认之地的原告，也一概被驳回起诉。

在这种情势下，读者诸君不难明白：每当有美国黑人带着他们的奴隶横穿地峡，克鲁塞斯和戈尔戈纳的人便按捺不住，巴不得凑到奴隶们耳边窃窃私语，献上自由的许诺，暗示逃走是何等容易。当局也决计不肯帮着捉回逃奴。于是，失主既然必须随大队继续赶路，逃亡者便回回逃脱。新格拉纳达的宪章里有一则信条——我相信英国的也是——奴隶一踏上它的国土，镣铐便从他身上脱落。为了不去触怒美国这样一个危险的邻居，这条规矩难得援用；但下面这桩成功援用的实例，我记得清清楚楚。

===Original===
A young American woman, whose character can be best described by the word "vicious," fell ill at Gorgona, and was left behind by her companions under the care of a young negro, her slave, whom she treated most inhumanly, as was evinced by the poor girl's frequent screams when under the lash. One night her cries were so distressing, that Gorgona could stand it no longer, but broke into the house and found the chattel bound hand and foot, naked, and being severely lashed. Despite the threats and astonishment of the mistress, they were both carried off on the following morning, before the alcalde, himself a man of colour, and of a very humane disposition. When the particulars of the case were laid before him, he became strongly excited, and called upon the woman to offer an explanation of her cruelty. She treated it with the coolest unconcern—"The girl was her property, worth so many dollars, and a child at New Orleans; had misbehaved herself, and been properly corrected. The alcalde must be drunk or a fool, or both together, to interfere between an American and her property." Her coolness vanished, however, when the alcalde turned round to the girl and told her that she was free to leave her mistress when she liked; and when she heard the irrepressible cheering of the crowded court-hut at the alcalde's humanity and boldness, and saw the slave's face flush with delight at the judge's words, she became terribly enraged; made use of the most fearful threats, and would have wreaked summary vengeance on her late chattel had not the clumsy soldiery interfered. Then, with demoniac refinement of cruelty, she bethought herself of the girl's baby at New Orleans still in her power, and threatened most horrible torture to the child if its mother dared to accept the alcalde's offer.

The poor girl trembled and covered her face with her hands, as though to shut out some fearful sight, and, I think, had we not persuaded her to the contrary, that she would have sacrificed her newly won freedom for the child's sake. But we knew very well that when the heat of passion had subsided, the threatener would be too 'cute to injure her own property; and at once set afloat a subscription for the purchase of the child. The issue of the tale I do not know, as the woman was very properly removed into the interior of the country.

===Chinese===
一个年轻的美国女人——她的品性用「歹毒」二字形容最贴切——在戈尔戈纳病倒了，被同伴们丢在后面，交给一个年轻黑人、她的女奴照看；她对待这女奴狠毒至极，那可怜姑娘挨鞭子时的声声惨叫便是明证。一天夜里，姑娘的哭喊实在凄厉，戈尔戈纳全城再也听不下去，破门而入，发现那件「活财产」被捆住手脚、赤条条地正挨着痛鞭。尽管女主人又惊又怒地威吓，第二天一早，两人还是都被带到了镇长面前——镇长本人就是个有色人，心地极为仁厚。案情细节一摆到他面前，他勃然动容，命那女人就她的残忍作出解释。她却以最冷静的漠然处之——「这丫头是她的财产，值多少多少块钱，在新奥尔良还有个孩子；行为不端，受了应得的管教。镇长要不是喝醉了就是个蠢货，再不就是两样占全了，才来插手一个美国人和她的财产之间的事。」可当镇长转过身去告诉那姑娘，她随时可以离开她的女主人；当她听见挤满法庭棚屋的人们为镇长的仁义和胆气迸发出压抑不住的喝彩，又看见那奴隶的脸因法官的话语而泛起狂喜的红晕，她的冷静登时烟消云散；她变得狂怒已极，口出最骇人的威胁，若不是那帮笨手笨脚的士兵拦着，她当场就要对她昔日的「活财产」痛快地施行私刑。接着，她又以魔鬼一般精雕细琢的残忍，想起了还捏在自己手里的、姑娘留在新奥尔良的婴孩，扬言倘若孩子的母亲胆敢接受镇长的恩典，便叫那孩子尝尝最可怕的酷刑。

可怜的姑娘浑身发抖，双手掩面，仿佛要挡开什么骇人的景象；我想，若不是我们劝阻了她，她真会为了那个孩子，把新到手的自由牺牲掉。但我们心里很清楚：等这股恶火的势头一过，那威吓人的主儿精着呢，决不肯损伤自己的财产；于是我们立刻发起了一项认捐，要把那孩子买下来。这故事后来的结局我不知道，因为那女人被十分得体地移送到了内陆地方去了。

===Original===
Life at Gorgona resembled life at Cruces so nearly that it does not need a separate description. Down with the store and hotel keepers came the muleteers and mules, porters and hangers-on, idlers and thieves, gamblers and dancing women; and soon the monte-tables were fitted up, and plying their deadly trade; and the dancers charmed the susceptible travellers as successfully in the dirty streets of Gorgona as they had previously done in the unwholesome precincts of Cruces. And Dr. Casey was very nearly getting himself into serious trouble, from too great a readiness to use his revolver. Still, he had a better excuse for bloodshed this time than might have been found for his previous breaches of the sixth commandment. Among the desperadoes who frequented his gambling-hut, during their short stay in Gorgona, was conceived the desperate plan of putting out the lights, and upsetting Casey's table—trusting in the confusion to carry off the piles of money upon it. The first part of their programme was successfully carried out; but the second was frustrated by the Doctor promptly firing his revolver into the dark, and hitting an unoffending boy in the hip. And at this crisis the Gorgona police entered, carried off all the parties they could lay hands upon (including the Doctor) to prison, and brought the wounded boy to me.

On the following morning came a most urgent request that I would visit the imprisoned Doctor. I found him desperately angry, but somewhat nervous too, for the alcalde was known to be no friend to the Americans, owed Casey more than one grudge, and had shown recently a disposition to enforce the laws.

"I say, Mrs. Seacole, how's that ——— boy?"

"Oh, Dr. Casey, how could you shoot the poor lad, and now call him bad names, as though he'd injured you? He is very ill indeed—may die; so I advise you to think seriously of your position."

"But, Madame Seacole," (this in a very altered tone), "*you'll* surely help me? *you'll* surely tell the alcalde that the wound's a slight one? He's a friend of yours, and will let me out of this hole. Come, Madame Seacole, you'll never leave me to be murdered by these bloodthirsty savages?"

"What can I do or say, Dr. Casey? I must speak the truth, and the ball is still in the poor lad's hip," I answered, for I enjoyed the fellow's fear too much to help him. However, he sent some of his friends to the boy's father, and bribed him to take the lad from my care, and send him to Navy Bay, to a surgeon there. Of course, he never returned to prosecute Dr. Casey; and he was left with the alcalde only to deal with, who, although he hated the man, could not resist his money, and so set him free.

===Chinese===
戈尔戈纳的生活同克鲁塞斯的生活像得不能再像，无须另行描摹。随着店主和旅店老板们一起下来的，是骡夫和骡子、脚夫和跟班、闲汉和窃贼、赌徒和舞女；不多时，蒙特牌赌台便支了起来，干起那害死人的营生；舞女们在戈尔戈纳肮脏的街道上迷住那些心猿意马的旅客，同她们先前在克鲁塞斯污浊的地界里一样手到擒来。而凯西医生呢，就因为左轮枪拔得太勤，差点给自己惹下大祸。不过这一回，比起他先前那些违犯第六诫的行径，他流血的借口倒算体面些。在他那座赌棚里出出进进的亡命徒们，在戈尔戈纳短暂盘桓期间，合计出了一条亡命的计策：熄掉灯火，掀翻凯斯的赌台——指望趁乱把台上一摞摞的钱搬走。他们计划的第一部分顺利得手；第二部分却被博士挫败了——他当机立断，朝黑暗里放了一枪，正打中一个无辜男孩的胯骨。就在这当口，戈尔戈纳警察进场，把抓得到的当事人（连博士本人在内）统统押进监狱，又把那个挨枪的男孩送到了我这里。

第二天一早，便来了一个十万火急的请求，请我去探望狱中的博士。我发现他气得要命，却也有几分发慌，因为人人都知道那位镇长向来不待见美国人，跟凯西的旧怨不止一桩，近来又显出了要厉行法律的意思。

「我说，西科尔太太，那个———小子怎么样了？」

「哎，凯西医生，你怎么能朝那可怜的孩子开枪，如今又骂他难听的话，倒像是他害了你不成？他病得很重——兴许会死；所以我劝你好好掂量掂量自己的处境。」

「可是，西科尔夫人，」（这回口气大变）「*你*总肯帮我吧？*你*总会跟镇长说那伤不碍事吧？他是你的朋友，会放我出这个窟窿的。来吧，西科尔夫人，你总不能眼看我被这帮嗜血的野人弄死吧？」

「我能做什么、说什么呢，凯西医生？我必须说实话，那颗子弹还嵌在可怜孩子的胯骨里，」我答道——那家伙的恐惧让我受用得很，实在不想帮他。不过，他还是派了几个朋友去找男孩的父亲，买通他把孩子从我手上接走，送到海军湾那儿的一个外科医生那里去。那孩子当然再没有回来控告凯西医生；剩下的只有镇长要应付——他虽然恨这个人，却抵挡不住他的钱，于是把他放了。

===Original===
Gorgona lying lower than Cruces, its inhabitants more frequently enjoyed the excitement of a flood. After heavy rains, the river would rise so rapidly that in a few hours the chief part of the place would be under water. On such occasions the scene was unusually exciting. As the water crept up the street, the frightened householders kept removing their goods and furniture to higher ground; while here and there, where the waters had surrounded them unawares, boats were sent to their rescue. The houses, not made to resist much wind or water, often gave way, and were carried down the Chagres. Meanwhile, the thieves were the busiest—the honest folks, forgetting the true old adage, "God helps those who help themselves," confining their exertions to bringing down their favourite saints to the water's edge, and invoking their interposition.

Fortunately my hotel was at the upper end of the town, where the floods had been rarely known to extend; and although there was a sufficient chance of the water reaching me to compel me to have all my stores, etc., ready packed for removal, I escaped. Some distressing losses occurred. A Frenchman, a near neighbour, whose house was surrounded by the waters before he could remove his goods, grew so frantic at the loss, that he obstinately refused to quit his falling house; and some force had to be used before they could save his life.

Scarcely had the ravages of the last flood been repaired when fire marked Gorgona for its prey. The conflagration began at a store by the riverside; but it spread rapidly, and before long all Gorgona was in danger. The town happened to be very full that night, two crowds having met there, and there was great confusion; but at last the lazy soldier-police, aided by the Americans, succeeded in pulling down some old crazy huts, and checking the fire's progress. The travellers were in sore plight, many of them being reduced to sleep upon their luggage, piled in the drenched streets. My hotel had some interesting inmates, for a poor young creature, borne in from one of the burning houses, became a mother during the night; and a stout little lassie opened its eyes upon this waesome world during the excitement and danger of a Gorgona conflagration.

===Chinese===
戈尔戈纳的地势比克鲁塞斯低，居民们便更常享用水灾的刺激。大雨过后，河水涨得飞快，几个钟头之内，镇上的主要部分便泡在水里。每逢这种时候，场面都格外热闹。水沿着街道悄悄往上爬，吓坏了的住户们把家什家具一个劲儿往高地搬；东一处西一处，还有人被大水围了个措手不及，得派船去救。那些房子本就不是造来抗风抗水的，常常就此垮塌，顺着查格雷斯河漂走。与此同时，最忙的要数窃贼们——老实人呢，把那句真正的古谚「自助者天助」忘了个干净，只顾把自家心爱的圣像抬到水边，祈求它们显灵搭救。

所幸我的旅馆在镇子的高处，大水极少淹到那里；虽然水够得着我的可能性不小，逼得我把全部存货等等都收拾停当、随时准备转移，我终究躲过了。倒是有几起令人痛心的损失。一个法国人，是我的近邻，还没来得及搬走货物，房子就被大水围住了；他为损失急得发了狂，死活不肯离开那座正在倒塌的房子，人们费了好一番力气，才把他的命救下来。

上一场水灾的疮痍还未及修复，大火又把戈尔戈纳标成了自己的猎物。大火是从河边一家店铺烧起来的；但火势蔓延极快，不多时整个戈尔戈纳都告急了。那晚镇上碰巧人满为患——两拨人潮正好在那里会合——一片大乱；但最后，懒惰的士兵警察在美国人的帮助下，总算拆掉了几座摇摇欲坠的老破棚屋，把火势挡住了。旅客们狼狈不堪，许多人只好睡在自己的行李上，行李堆在湿透的街面上。我的旅馆里倒是添了几个有趣的住客：一个可怜的年轻人儿，被人从一栋着火的房子里抬进来，当夜便做了母亲；一个壮实的小丫头，也在戈尔戈纳这场大火的惊险与危难之中，睁眼看到了这个多灾多难的人世。

===Original===
Shortly after this, tired to death of life in Panama, I handed over my hotel to my brother, and returned to Kingston. On the way thither I experienced another instance of American politeness, which I cannot help recording; first reminding my readers of what I have previously said of the character of the Californian travellers. Anxious to get home quickly, I took my passage in the first steamer that left Navy Bay—an American one; and late in the evening said farewell to the friends I had been staying with, and went on board. A very kind friend, an American merchant, doing a large business at Navy Bay, had tried hard to persuade me to delay my journey until the English company's steamer called; without, however, giving any good reasons for his wish. So, with Mac and my little maid, I passed through the crowd of female passengers on deck, and sought the privacy of the saloon. Before I had been long there, two ladies came to me, and in their cool, straightforward manner, questioned me.

"Where air you going?"

"To Kingston."

"And how air you going?"

"By sea."

===Chinese===
这之后不久，我对巴拿马的日子厌倦到了极点，便把旅馆移交给兄弟，回金斯敦去了。归途上我又领教了一回美国人的礼貌，忍不住要记下来；先请读者回想一下我先前对加州旅客品格说过的那些话。我归心似箭，便搭了头一班驶离海军湾的轮船——一班美国船；傍晚时分辞别了留我借宿的朋友们，上了船。一位很要好的朋友，是个在海军湾做大买卖的美国商人，曾竭力劝我把行程推迟到英国公司的轮船停靠时再走；只是对他这份心意，又说不出个所以然来。就这样，我带着麦克和小女仆，穿过甲板上成群的女客，寻到大舱里一处清静地方。我到那里没多久，两位女士便朝我走来，用她们那冷冰冰、直通通的做派盘问我。

「你上哪儿去？」

「去金斯敦。」

「咋个去法？」

「走海路。」

===Original===
"Don't be impertinent, yallow woman. By what conveyance air you going?"

"By this steamer, of course. I've paid for my passage."

They went away with this information; and in a short time eight or nine others came and surrounded me, asking the same questions. My answers—and I was very particular—raised quite a storm of uncomplimentary remarks.

"Guess a nigger woman don't go along with us in this saloon," said one. "I never travelled with a nigger yet, and I expect I shan't begin now," said another; while some children had taken my little servant Mary in hand, and were practising on her the politenesses which their parents were favouring me with—only, as is the wont of children, they were crueller. I cannot help it if I shock my readers; but the *truth* is, that one positively spat in poor little Mary's frightened yellow face.

===Chinese===
「少放肆，黄皮肤女人。你搭的什么船？」

「自然是搭这条船。我买过船票了。」

她们得了这个情报便走开了；不多时又来了八九个，把我团团围住，问的还是那几个问题。我的回答——我可是字字讲究——激起了一阵满不含恭维话的风暴。

「俺估摸着，一个黑鬼女人甭想跟俺们一块儿待在这大舱里，」一个说。「俺还从来没跟黑鬼同船旅行过，俺料着如今也开不得这个头，」另一个说；还有几个孩子也拿我的小仆人玛丽（Mary）开起刀来，把他们父母款待我的那套「礼数」在她身上操练——只是孩子们素来如此，下手更狠。倘若我骇着了读者，我也没法子；可*实情*是，当真有一个孩子，朝可怜的小玛丽那张吓得发慌的黄脸啐了一口。

===Original===
At last an old American lady came to where I sat, and gave me some staid advice. "Well, now, I tell you for your good, you'd better quit this, and not drive my people to extremities. If you do, you'll be sorry for it, I expect." Thus harassed, I appealed to the stewardess—a tall sour-looking woman, flat and thin as a dressed-up broomstick. She asked me sundry questions as to how and when I had taken my passage; until, tired beyond all endurance, I said, "My good woman, put me anywhere—under a boat—in your storeroom, so that I can get to Kingston somehow." But the stewardess was not to be moved.

"There's nowhere but the saloon, and you can't expect to stay with the white people, that's clear. Flesh and blood can stand a good deal of aggravation; but not that. If the Britishers is so took up with coloured people, that's their business; but it won't do here."

This last remark was in answer to an Englishman, whose advice to me was not to leave my seat for any of them. He made matters worse; until at last I lost my temper, and calling Mac, bade him get my things together, and went up to the captain—a good honest man. He and some of the black crew and the black cook, who showed his teeth most viciously, were much annoyed. Muttering about its being a custom of the country, the captain gave me an order upon the agent for the money I had paid; and so, at twelve o'clock at night, I was landed again upon the wharf of Navy Bay.

My American friends were vastly annoyed, but not much surprised; and two days later, the English steamer, the *Eagle*, in charge of my old friend, Captain B——, touched at Navy Bay, and carried me to Kingston.

===Chinese===
最后，一位年长的美国女士走到我坐的地方，给了我一番稳重的忠告。「哎，我说，这是为你好，你顶好趁早罢手，别把我的人逼到绝处。你要是不听，往后有得后悔，俺料着是这样。」受够了这份围攻，我去求女管事——一个高个子、酸脸的女人，又扁又瘦，活像一根装扮起来的扫帚把。她盘问了我一串问题，问我怎么买的票、几时买的；直到我忍无可忍，说道：「我的好大姐，你随便把我搁哪儿——搁船底下——搁你的货舱里，只要能让我到金斯敦就行。」可女管事纹丝不动。

「除了大舱没别处，白人堆里你别想待，明摆着的。血肉之躯受得了的窝囊气多得是；可受不了这个。英国佬要是这么看得起有色人，那是他们的事；在这儿行不通。」

这最后一句话是回给一位英国人的，他劝我休要为她们中任何一个挪窝。他反而把事情搅得更糟；直到最后我火了，唤来麦克，吩咐他收拾好我的东西，便去找船长——一个正直的好人。他同几个黑人船员、还有那个呲牙咧嘴、一脸凶相的黑人厨师，都气得不行。船长一面咕哝说这是此地的风气，一面给我开了张向代理商支回船钱的凭条；就这样，半夜十二点，我又被送回了海军湾的码头上。

我的美国朋友们气恼得很，倒不怎么意外；两天之后，英国轮船「鹰号」（*Eagle*）在我老友 B——船长（Captain B——）的执掌下停靠海军湾，把我载到了金斯敦。

## VII / VII

===Original===
The yellow fever in Jamaica—My experience of deathbed scenes—I leave again for Navy Bay, and open a store there—I am attacked with the gold fever, and start for Escribanos—Life in the interior of the republic of New Granada—A revolutionary conspiracy on a small scale—The dinner delicacies of Escribanos—Journey up the Palmilla River—A few words on the present aspect of affairs on the Isthmus of Panama.

I stayed in Jamaica eight months out of the year 1853, still remembered in the island for its suffering and gloom. I returned just in time to find my services, with many others, needful; for the yellow fever never made a more determined effort to exterminate the English in Jamaica than it did in that dreadful year. So violent was the epidemic, that some of my people fell victims to its fury, a thing rarely heard of before. My house was full of sufferers—officers, their wives and children. Very often they were borne in from the ships in the harbour—sometimes in a dying state, sometimes—after long and distressing struggles with the grim foe—to recover. Habituated as I had become with death in its most harrowing forms, I found these scenes more difficult to bear than any I had previously borne a part in; and for this reason perhaps, that I had not only to cheer the deathbed of the sufferer, but, far more trying task, to soothe the passionate grief of wife or husband left behind. It was a terrible thing to see young people in the youth and bloom of life suddenly stricken down, not in battle with an enemy that threatened their country, but in vain contest with a climate that refused to adopt them. Indeed, the mother country pays a dear price for the possession of her colonies.

I think all who are familiar with the West Indies will acknowledge that Nature has been favourable to strangers in a few respects, and that one of these has been in instilling into the hearts of the Creoles an affection for English people and an anxiety for their welfare, which shows itself warmest when they are sick and suffering. I can safely appeal on this point to anyone who is acquainted with life in Jamaica.
