This Greek had, it seemed, robbed his enemy, but the Turk was master this time, and had, in order to force from the robber a confession of the place where the stolen things were deposited (like dogs, as they were, these fellows were fond of burying their plunder), resorted to torture. This was effected most ingeniously and simply by means of some packthread, which, bound round the Greek’s two thumbs, was tightened on the tourniquet principle, until the pain elicited a confession. But the Turk, stimulated to retaliation by his triumph, bagged the Greek’s basket, which contained amongst other things two watches, which their present owner had no doubt stolen. Driven to the most ludicrous show of despair, the Greek was about to attempt another desperate struggle for the recovery of his goods, when two Zouaves elbowed their small persons upon the crowded stage, and were eagerly referred to by all the parties concerned in the squabble. How they contrived it, I cannot say, so prompt were their movements; but, in a very few minutes, the watches were in their possession, and going much faster than was agreeable either to Turk or Greek, who both combined to arrest this new movement, and thereby added a sharp thrashing to their other injuries. The Zouaves effected their escape safely, while the Greek, with a despair that had in it an equal share of the ludicrous and the tragic, threw himself upon the dusty ground, and tore his thin hair out by handfuls. I believe that the poor wretch, whom we could not help pitying, journeyed to Kamiesch, to discover his oppressors; but I fear he didn’t gain much information there.

Had it not been for the unremitting activity of the authorities, no life would have been safe in Balaclava, with its population of villains of every nation. As it was, murder was sometimes added to robbery, and many of the rascals themselves died suspicious deaths, with the particulars of which the authorities did not trouble themselves. But the officials worked hard, both in the harbour and on shore, to keep order; few men could have worked harder. I often saw the old grey-haired Admiral about before the sun had fairly shown itself; and those of his subordinates must have been somewhat heavy sleepers who could play the sluggard then.

===Chinese===
可怜的土耳其人！一想到他怎样被自己的朋友们劫掠、辱骂、欺凌，就令人叹惋。他为什么不拿出一点血性来？随便哪个粗野的水手，或是精明的男孩——满嘴放肆与偏见的英国男孩，在巴拉克拉瓦活得红红火火——只要他肯抬起头来、哪怕露出一星半点的气概，都会乐意拍拍他的背。可是英国人无法理解懦夫——连可怜他都懒得费那个事；甚至连怯懦的希腊人，也能在那些凶悍阿拉伯人的退化后裔面前称王称霸——据当地人对我说，正是那些阿拉伯人，在我不甚了了的古老年代里，从基督徒手中夺走了君士坦丁堡。时常有受了欺的土耳其人跑到我坐的地方，站在那里，激动地比划着手势，控诉某个面目凶恶的希腊人或意大利人——那种货色，一个壮实的英国小伙子五分钟就能把他连那身脏皮一起抖散。

不过有一次，我看见风水轮转了。这段逸事正好有助于说明巴拉克拉瓦各掠食部落之间的相对地位，我不妨讲一讲。一天早晨，我听见一阵喧哗，比寻常买卖成交之后必有的那场争吵还要响，便走上前去，只见一群激动的人围着一个土耳其人，一个希腊人正在他手里徒劳地挣扎。原来这希腊人偷了他的对头，但这回土耳其人占了上风；为了逼这强盗招出赃物藏在哪里（这帮家伙果然狗性难改，最爱把赃物理进土里），他动了刑。用刑的法子做得极其巧妙而简便：拿几股打包线缠住希腊人的两根大拇指，照绞紧止血带的原理收紧，直到疼痛逼出了招供。可那土耳其人被胜利激起了报复心，把希腊人的篮子也一并缴了，篮子里装着各色物件，其中有两块怀表——它们如今的主人无疑也是偷来的。希腊人被逼出一副最滑稽的绝望相，正要为夺回财物再作一次拼命的挣扎，这时两个朱阿夫兵把他们的小身板挤上这座拥挤的舞台，争吵的各方都急切地请他们评理。他们是怎么得手的，我说不上来，动作实在太麻利；总之没几分钟，两块怀表就到了他们手里，转手的速度令土耳其人和希腊人都不好受，两人便联手要拦下这一新动向，结果在原有损失之外，又添了一顿好打。朱阿夫兵安然脱身；那希腊人则满心绝望——那绝望一半滑稽、一半凄惨——扑倒在尘土飞扬的地上，一把一把地薅他那稀疏的头发。我相信，那个我们不由得不同情的可怜虫，是跑到卡米什（Kamiesch）寻访他的欺压者去了；不过恐怕他在那里也没打听到多少消息。

若不是当局不懈的辛劳，在巴拉克拉瓦这个万国恶棍杂居之地，谁的生命都不会安全。即便如此，谋财之外有时还要害命，许多恶棍自己也死得蹊跷，其中详情，当局也懒得深究。但官员们干得卖力，在港里在岸上都竭力维持秩序；很少有人能比他们更卖力的。我常常看见那位头发花白的老海军上将，赶在太阳还没露脸之前就四处巡查；他手下的属员若还有谁敢在那个时辰睡懒觉，必定睡得相当沉。

===Original===
At length the necessary preparations to establish our store were made. We hit upon a spot about two miles from Balaclava, in advance of Kadikoi, close to where the railway engines were stationed, and within a mile of headquarters. Leave having been obtained to erect buildings here, we set to work briskly, and soon altered the appearance of Spring Hill—so we christened our new home. Sometimes on horseback, sometimes getting a lift on the commissariat carts, and occasionally on the ammunition railway-wagons, I managed to visit Spring Hill daily, and very soon fitted up a shed sufficiently large to take up my abode in. But the difficulty of building our store was immense. To obtain material was next to impossible; but that collected (not a little was, by leave of the Admiral, gleaned from the floating rubbish in the harbour), to find workmen to make use of it was still more difficult. I spent days going round the shipping, offering great wages, even, for an invalid able to handle saw and hammer, however roughly, and many a long ride through the camps did I take on the same errand. At length, by dint of hard canvassing, we obtained the aid of two English sailors, whom I nicknamed “Big and Little Chips,” and some Turks, and set to work in good earnest.

I procured the Turks from the Pacha who commanded the division encamped in the neighbourhood of Spring Hill. It was decided that we should apply to him for help, and accordingly I became ambassadress on this delicate mission, and rode over to the Pacha’s quarters, Jew Johnny attending me as interpreter. I was received by the Pacha with considerable kindness and no trifling amount of formality, and after taking coffee I proceeded, through Jew Johnny, to explain the object of my visit, while his Excellency, a tall man, with a dark pleasing face, smoked gravely, and took my request into his gracious consideration.

On the following day came the answer to my request, in the persons of two curious Turkish carpenters, who were placed at our orders. After a little while, too, a Turkish officer, whom I christened Captain Ali Baba, took so great an interest in our labours that he would work like any carpenter, and with a delight and zeal that were astonishing. To see him fall back, and look smilingly at every piece of his workmanship, was a sight to restore the most severely tried temper. I really think that the good-hearted fellow thought it splendid fun, and never wearied of it. But for him I do not know how we should have managed with our other Turkish “chips”—chips of the true old Turkish block they were—deliberate, slow, and indolent, breaking off into endless interruptions for the sacred duties of eating and praying, and getting into out-of-the-way corners at all times of the day to smoke themselves to sleep.

===Chinese===
终于，开设商店的必要筹备都办妥了。我们相中了一块地方，离巴拉克拉瓦约两英里，在卡迪科伊（Kadikoi）再往前，紧挨着铁路机车停放处，距司令部不出一英里。获准在此建造屋舍之后，我们便热火朝天地干了起来，很快就改变了斯普林希尔的面貌——我们就这样给新家命了名。我有时骑马去，有时搭军需处的便车，偶尔坐运送弹药的铁路平板车，总算每天都到斯普林希尔走一趟，很快便搭起一座足够我栖身的宽敞棚屋。可是建造商店的难处是巨大的。要搞到材料几乎不可能；材料凑齐之后（经海军上将许可，从港中漂浮的垃圾里捡回来的就不在少数），要找到肯动工的工人就更难了。我花了好些天绕着各条船转，开出高工钱——哪怕是个能马马虎虎使得动锯子和锤子的病号也行；为着同一桩差事，我也无数次骑马长途穿营过寨。最后，靠着软磨硬泡，我们求得两个英国水手的帮助——我给他们起了绰号，叫「大木片」和「小木片」（Big and Little Chips）——外加几个土耳其人，便认认真真地开工了。

那几个土耳其人，是我从驻扎在斯普林希尔附近的那位帕夏（Pacha）手里讨来的。大家商定去向他求助，于是我便充任这趟微妙使命的大使，骑马去到帕夏的驻地，犹太人约翰尼给我当翻译。帕夏接待了我，相当和蔼，礼数也一点不少；喝过咖啡，我便通过约翰尼之口说明来意；那位大人是个高个子，有一张黝黑而讨人喜欢的脸，一本正经地抽着烟，对我的请求予以亲切的考虑。

第二天，我的请求便有了回音——回音是两个模样古怪的土耳其木匠，被派来听我们差遣。过了不久，又有一位土耳其军官——我给他起名叫阿里巴巴上尉（Captain Ali Baba）——对我们的工程大感兴趣，竟肯像随便哪个木匠一样动手干活，那份兴致和热忱令人惊叹。看他退后两步，笑眯眯地打量自己手里的每一件活计，那情景能让最受煎熬的脾气也缓过来。我真觉得，这位好心肠的家伙把这事儿当成了绝妙的消遣，百做不厌。多亏有他，不然真不知道我们拿另外那几个土耳其「木片」怎么办才好——他们可真是道地老土耳其木头上劈下来的木片——慢条斯理，懒懒散散，动不动就撂下活计，去尽吃饭和祷告的神圣义务，一天里不论什么时辰，都会钻进偏僻的角落，抽烟抽到睡着。

===Original===
In the midst of our work a calamity occurred, which was very nearly becoming a catastrophe. By the giving way of a dam, after some heavy rains, the little stream which threaded its silvery way past Spring Hill swelled without any warning into a torrent, which, sweeping through my temporary hut, very nearly carried us all away, and destroyed stores of between one and two hundred pounds in value. This calamity might have had a tragical issue for me, for seeing a little box which contained some things, valuable as relics of the past, being carried away, I plunged in after it, and losing my balance, was rolled over and over by the stream, and with some difficulty reached the shore. Some of Lord Raglan’s staff passing our wreck on the following day, made inquiries respecting the loss we had sustained, and a messenger was sent from headquarters, who made many purchases, in token of their sympathy.

My visit to the Turkish Pacha laid the foundation of a lasting friendship. He soon found his way to Spring Hill, and before long became one of my best customers and most frequent visitors. It was astonishing to note how completely, now that he was in the land of the Giaours, he adapted himself to the tastes and habits of the infidels. Like a Scotch Presbyterian, on the Continent for a holiday, he threw aside all the prejudices of his education, and drank bottled beer, sherry, and champagne with an appreciation of their qualities that no thirsty-souled Christian could have expressed more gratefully. He was very affable with us all, and would sometimes keep Jew Johnny away from his work for hours, chatting with us or the English officers who would lounge into our as yet unfinished store. Sometimes he would come down to breakfast, and spend the greater part of the day at Spring Hill. Indeed, the wits of Spring Hill used to laugh, and say that the crafty Pacha was throwing his pocket-handkerchief at Madame Seacole, widow; but as the honest fellow candidly confessed he had three wives already at home, I acquit him of any desire to add to their number.

The Pacha’s great ambition was to be familiar with the English language, and at last nothing would do but he must take lessons of me. So he would come down, and sitting in my store, with a Turk or so at his feet, to attend to his most important pipe, by inserting little red-hot pieces of charcoal at intervals, would try hard to sow a few English sentences in his treacherous memory. He never got beyond half a dozen; and I think if we had continued in the relation of pupil and mistress until now, the number would not have been increased greatly. “Madame Seacole,” “Gentlemen, good morning,” and “More champagne,” with each syllable much dwelt upon, were his favourite sentences. It was capital fun to hear him, when I was called away suddenly to attend to a customer, or to give a sick man medicine, repeating gravely the sentence we had been studying, until I passed him, and started him with another.

===Chinese===
工程进行到一半，一场灾祸从天而降，差点酿成大祸。几场大雨过后，一座水坝溃了决，那条从斯普林希尔旁边蜿蜒流过、泛着银光的小溪，没有任何预兆便暴涨成激流，冲过我临时的棚屋，差点把我们全都卷走，毁掉了价值一二百英镑的存货。这场灾祸，对我来说本可能落个悲惨的结局：因为我瞧见一只小箱子——里面装着几件作为往昔遗念而分外珍贵的物件——被水冲走，便一头扎进水里去追，失了平衡，被激流卷着翻滚了好几遭，费了好大的劲才爬上岸。第二天，拉格伦勋爵（Lord Raglan）的几位参谋路过我们的劫后现场，询问我们蒙受的损失，司令部还派来一名信使，买下了不少东西，以表同情之意。

我对土耳其帕夏的那次拜访，奠定了一段持久的友谊。他很快便寻到斯普林希尔来，不久就成了我最好的主顾和最常来的访客之一。令人称奇的是，如今身处异教徒（Giaour）之地的他，竟把自己调教得完全顺应了这些异教徒的口味与习惯。就像到欧洲大陆度假的苏格兰长老会信徒一样，他把自幼所受教育赋予的一切成见统统抛在一边，喝瓶装啤酒、雪利酒和香槟，对其妙处的赏识，连灵魂最干渴的基督徒也未必能表达得更感激。他对我们大家都很和蔼，有时会把犹太人约翰尼从活计上拽开好几个钟头，同我们闲聊，或是跟那些逛进我们尚未完工的商店的英国军官攀谈。有时他下来吃早饭，一待就在斯普林希尔耗掉大半天。说实在的，斯普林希尔的几位俏皮鬼常拿这个打趣，说这位狡黠的帕夏是在向西科尔寡妇夫人抛手绢求爱呢；不过这位老实人坦白承认家里已经有三房太太，我便宣判他无罪，免了他还想再添一房的嫌疑。

帕夏的一大抱负，就是通晓英语；到后来，他非拜我为师不可。于是他常常下来，坐在我店里，脚边蹲着一两个土耳其人，伺候他那管顶要紧的烟斗——隔一阵子往里塞一小块烧红的木炭——然后拼命往他那靠不住的记性里，播下几句英文句子。他从来没有超出六句；我想，就算师生关系维持到今天，这个数目也增加不了多少。「西科尔夫人」（Madame Seacole）、「先生们，早上好」、「再来点香槟」，是他最心爱的句子，每个音节都要咂摸半天。每当我被突然叫走，去招呼顾客或是给病人送药，听他一本正经地反复念着我们刚学的那一句，直到我从他身旁走过，再用另一句把他发动起来——那真是绝妙的乐子。

===Original===
Very frequently he would compliment me by ordering his band down to Spring Hill for my amusement. They played excellently well, and I used to think that I preferred their music to that of the French and English regimental bands. I laughed heartily one day, when, in compliance with the kindhearted Anglo-Turkish Pacha’s orders, they came out with a grand new tune, in which I with difficulty recognised a very distant resemblance to “God save the Queen.”

Altogether he was a capital neighbour, and gave such strict orders to his men to respect our property that we rarely lost anything. On the whole, the Turks were the most honest of the nations there (I except the English and the Sardinians), and the most tractable. But the Greeks hated them, and showed their hate in every way. In bringing up things for the Pacha’s use they would let the mules down, and smash their loads most relentlessly. Now and then they suffered, as was the case one day when I passed through the camp and saw my friend superintending the correction of a Greek who was being bastinadoed. It seemed a painful punishment.

I was sorry, therefore, when my friend’s division was ordered to Kamara, and we lost our neighbours. But my pupil did not forget his schoolmistress. A few days after they had left the neighbourhood of Spring Hill came a messenger, with a present of lambs, poultry, and eggs, and a letter, which I could not decipher, as many of the interpreters could speak English far better than they could write it. But we discovered that the letter contained an invitation, to Mr. Day and myself, to go over to Kamara, and select from the spoil of the village anything that might be useful in our new buildings. And a few days later came over a large araba, drawn by four mules, and laden with a pair of glass-doors, and some window-frames, which the thoughtful kind Pacha had judged—and judged rightly—would be a very acceptable present. And very often the good-natured fellow would ride over from Kamara, and resume his acquaintance with myself and my champagne, and practise his English sentences.

===Chinese===
他还常常抬举我，命他的乐队开到斯普林希尔来给我解闷。他们演奏得非常出色，我一度觉得，比起法国和英国的军乐队，我更爱听他们的音乐。有一天我放声大笑：这支乐队奉好心肠的盎格鲁-土耳其帕夏之命，奏起一支隆重的新曲，我好容易才从中辨认出，它同《天佑女王》（God save the Queen）有那么一点远房亲缘似的相像。

总而言之，他是位顶好的邻居；他严令部下尊重我们的财产，所以我们极少丢东西。大体说来，土耳其人是那里各民族中最诚实的（英国人和撒丁人除外），也最驯顺听话。可是希腊人恨他们，变着法儿表露仇恨。替帕夏运送东西时，他们会故意放倒骡子，毫不留情地砸烂驮载。他们偶尔也吃吃苦头，比如有一天我路过营地，看见我的朋友正监督对一个希腊人执行棒打脚底的刑责（bastinado）。那似乎是一种痛苦的刑罚。

因此，当朋友的师奉调前往卡马拉（Kamara）、我们失去这些邻居时，我心里很难过。不过我的学生没有忘记他的女教师。他们撤出斯普林希尔附近没几天，就来了一位信使，带着羔羊、家禽和鸡蛋做礼物，还有一封信——我怎么也读不明白，因为许多译员讲英语远比写英语在行。但我们弄清楚了，信里是一份邀请：请戴先生和我过去卡马拉一趟，从村中的缴获物里随意挑拣，凡是对我们新建筑有用的东西，尽管拿。几天之后，又驶来一辆四头骡子拉的阿拉巴大车（araba），装着一对玻璃门和几副窗框——那位想得周到的好心帕夏断定（也断定得没错），这会是一件非常受欢迎的礼物。这位好心的家伙还常常从卡马拉骑马过来，同我和我的香槟重叙旧谊，再练练他的英文句子。

===Original===
We felt the loss of our Turkish neighbours in more ways than one. The neighbourhood, after their departure, was left lonely and unprotected, and it was not until a division of the Land Transport Corps came and took up their quarters near us, that I felt at all secure of personal safety. Mr. Day rarely returned to Spring Hill until nightfall relieved him from his many duties, and I depended chiefly upon two sailors, both of questionable character, two black servants, Jew Johnny, and my own reputation for determination and courage—a poor delusion, which I took care to heighten by the judicious display of a double-barrelled pistol, lent me for the purpose by Mr. Day, and which I couldn’t have loaded to save my life.

===Chinese===
失去土耳其邻居，我们从好些方面都感受到了。他们走后，这一带冷冷清清，无人护卫；直到陆运军团的一个分队开来、在我们近旁扎了营，我才略微觉得人身安全有了保障。戴先生公务繁多，不到夜幕替他解脱，很少回到斯普林希尔；我主要仰仗两个水手——两人的品行都靠不住——两个黑人仆役、犹太人约翰尼，还有我自己那份果断与勇悍的名声——那是个可怜的错觉，我却精心维护，办法是有分寸地亮出一把双管手枪——戴先生专为这目的借给我的——可就算拿命来换，我也不会给它装弹药。

## XII / XII

===Original===
The British Hotel—Domestic difficulties—Our enemies—The Russian rats—Adventures in search of a cat—Light-fingered Zouaves—Crimean thieves—Powdering a horse.

===Chinese===
不列颠旅馆——家务烦难——我们的敌人——俄国老鼠——寻猫历险——手脚不干净的朱阿夫兵——克里米亚窃贼——给马扑粉。

===Original===
Summer was fairly advanced before the British Hotel was anything like finished; indeed, it never was completed, and when we left the Hill, a year later, it still wanted shutters. But long before that time Spring Hill had gained a great reputation. Of course, I have nothing to do with what occurred in the camp, although I could not help hearing a great deal about it. Mismanagement and privation there might have been, but my business was to make things right in my sphere, and whatever confusion, and disorder existed elsewhere, comfort and order were always to be found at Spring Hill. When there was no sun elsewhere, some few gleams—so its grateful visitors said—always seemed to have stayed behind, to cheer the weary soldiers that gathered in the British Hotel. And, perhaps, as my kind friend *Punch* said, after all these things had become pleasant memories of the past.

> "The cold without gave a zest, no doubt,
> To the welcome warmth within;
> But her smile, good old soul, lent heat to the coal,
> And power to the pannikin."

===Chinese===
不列颠旅馆要说有点像样的完工，已是盛夏时节；其实它从头到尾就没竣工过，一年之后我们离开山丘时，它还缺着窗板呢。但早在那之前，斯普林希尔就已经名声大噪。当然，营里发生的事与我无关，尽管种种传闻不由我不听见许多。那里或许有过管理不善与匮乏之苦，但我的本分是把自己这一方天地整治妥帖；无论别处多么混乱失序，在斯普林希尔，舒适与井然总是触手可及。用那些心怀感激的来客的话说，别处不见太阳的时候，总还有几缕阳光仿佛留在了这里不肯走，温暖着聚到不列颠旅馆来的疲惫士兵。而这，也许正应了我那位好心的朋友《笨拙》（*Punch*）杂志的话——待这一切统统化作往昔的温馨回忆之后——

> 「户外严寒，毋庸置疑，
> 给屋内可亲的暖意添了兴味；
> 可她的微笑，好心的老妈妈，给炉煤添了热，
> 也给小锡锅添了力。」

===Original===
Let me, in a few words, describe the British Hotel. It was acknowledged by all to be the most complete thing there. It cost no less than £800. The buildings and yards took up at least an acre of ground, and were as perfect as we could make them. The hotel and storehouse consisted of a long iron room, with counters, closets, and shelves; above it was another low room, used by us for storing our goods, and above this floated a large union-jack. Attached to this building was a little kitchen, not unlike a ship’s caboose—all stoves and shelves. In addition to the iron house were two wooden houses, with sleeping apartments for myself and Mr. Day, outhouses for our servants, a canteen for the soldiery, and a large enclosed yard for our stock, full of stables, low huts, and sties. Everything, although rough and unpolished, was comfortable and warm; and there was a completeness about the whole which won general admiration. The reader may judge of the manner in which we had stocked the interior of our store from the remark, often repeated by the officers, that you might get everything at Mother Seacole’s, from an anchor down to a needle.

In addition, we had for our transport service four carts, and as many horses and mules as could be kept from the thieves. To reckon upon being in possession of these, at any future time, was impossible; we have more than once seen a fair stud stabled at nighttime, and on the following morning been compelled to borrow cattle from the Land Transport camp, to fetch our things up from Balaclava.

===Chinese===
让我用几句话描绘一下不列颠旅馆。人人都承认，那是当地最完备的一处。它足足花了 800 英镑。屋舍和场院占地至少一英亩，我们已尽力把它们弄得尽善尽美。旅馆和货栈是一条长长的铁皮屋子，带柜台、壁橱和货架；它上头还有一间低矮的屋子，我们用来存放货物，屋顶上飘着一面大大的联合王国国旗（Union Jack）。附属在这座建筑旁的还有一间小厨房，颇像船上的厨舱——满眼都是炉灶和搁架。铁皮屋之外，还有两座木头房子，内设我和戴先生的卧房，佣仆们住的偏屋，供士兵购用的小卖部，还有一座圈牲口的大场院，里面满是牲口棚、低矮的棚屋和猪圈。样样东西虽说粗糙不加修饰，却舒适而暖和；整处的齐备周全，赢得了众口一词的赞赏。军官们有句常挂在嘴边的话，说在西科尔妈妈这里，从铁锚到缝衣针，什么东西都能买到——读者由此可以想见我们店堂里的备货是何等模样。

此外，我们还有 4 辆运货车，至于马和骡子，有多少全看能从窃贼手里保住多少。指望日后任何时辰还拥有它们，是不可能的事；我们不止一次眼见夜里还满满一棚好马，第二天一早就不得不跑去陆运军团营地借牲口，把我们的货从巴拉克拉瓦拉上来。

===Original===
But it must not be supposed that my domestic difficulties came to an end with the completion of the hotel. True, I was in a better position to bear the Crimean cold and rain, but my other foes were as busy as ever they had been on the beach at Balaclava. Thieves, biped and quadruped, human and animal, troubled me more than ever; and perhaps the most difficult to deal with were the least dangerous. The Crimean rats, for instance, who had the appetites of London aldermen, and were as little dainty as hungry schoolboys. Whether they had left Sebastopol, guided by the instinct which leads their kindred in other parts of the world to forsake sinking ships, or because the garrison rations offended their palates, or whether they had patriotically emigrated, to make war against the English larders, I do not pretend to guess; but, whatever was their motive, it drew them in great abundance to Spring Hill. They occasionally did us damage, in a single night, to the tune of two or three pounds—wasting what they could not devour. You could keep nothing sacred from their strong teeth. When hard pressed they more than once attacked the live sheep; and at last they went so far as to nibble one of our black cooks, Francis, who slept among the flour barrels. On the following morning he came to me, his eyes rolling angrily, and his white teeth gleaming, to show me a mangled finger, which they had bitten, and ask me to dress it. He made a great fuss; and a few mornings later he came in a violent passion this time, and gave me instant notice to quit my service, although we were paying him two pounds a week, with board and rations. This time the rats had, it appeared, been bolder, and attacked his head, in a spot where its natural armour, the wool, was thinnest, and the silly fellow had a notion that the souls of the slain Russian soldiers had entered the bodies of the rats, and made vengeful war upon their late enemies. Driven to such an extremity, I made up my mind to scour the camp, in search of a cat, and, after a long day’s hunt, I came to the conclusion that the tale of Whittington was by no means an improbable one. Indeed, had a brisk young fellow with a cat, of even ordinary skill in its profession, made their appearance at Spring Hill, I would gladly have put them in the way—of laying the foundation, at least—of a fortune. At last I found a benefactor, in the Guards’ camp, in Colonel D———, of the Coldstreams, who kindly promised me a great pet, well known in the camp, and perhaps by some who may read these pages, by the name of Pinkie. Pinkie was then helping a brother officer to clear his hut, but on the following day a Guardsman brought the noble fellow down. He lived in clover for a few days, but he had an English catlike attachment for his old house, and despite the abundance of game, Pinkie soon stole away to his old master’s quarters, three miles off. More than once the men brought him back to me, but the attractions of Spring Hill were never strong enough to detain him long with me.

===Chinese===
但可别以为旅馆一成，我的家务烦难就到了头。不错，对付克里米亚的寒冷和阴雨，我的境况是好多了，可其余的仇敌，还是像当初在巴拉克拉瓦滩头时一样忙碌。窃贼们——两条腿的、四条腿的，人的、兽的——比从前更让我头疼；而最难对付的，恐怕偏偏是最不危险的那些。比如克里米亚的老鼠，胃口赛过伦敦的市政官老爷，又像饿慌了的小学生一样毫不挑嘴。它们究竟是受本能指引撤离了塞瓦斯托波尔——就像世界别处的同类弃下沉船那样——还是因为守军的口粮败坏了它们的口味，抑或它们是出于爱国热忱举族迁来，专程向英国的食品柜开战，我不敢妄猜；但无论动机为何，它们成群结队地涌到了斯普林希尔。它们偶尔在一夜之间就啃掉我们两三英镑的家当——吃不完的也要糟蹋掉。任你把什么东西视为神圣，都挡不住它们的利齿。被逼急了，它们不止一次向活羊下手；最后竟发展到啃起我们的一个黑人厨子弗朗西斯（Francis）来——他睡在面粉桶中间。第二天早晨他来找我，眼珠气得直转，白牙闪闪发亮，给我看一根被咬烂的手指，让我给他包扎。他大惊小怪地闹了一场；过了几个早晨，他又来了一趟，这回是勃然大怒，当场向我提出辞工，尽管我们每周付他 2 英镑，还管吃管住。这一回，老鼠们看来胆子更大，袭击了他的脑袋，专挑那顶天然盔甲——绒毛头发——最稀薄的地方下口；那傻小子还认定，是阵亡俄国士兵的冤魂钻进了老鼠的躯体，正向他们生前的仇敌实施报复。被逼到这般绝境，我横下心来，要把营地搜个遍，找一只猫。经过一整天的搜猎，我得出结论：惠廷顿（Whittington）的故事绝非无稽之谈。说实在的，倘若有个精神抖擞的小伙子带着一只猫——哪怕是本职本领平平的猫——出现在斯普林希尔，我都乐意给他们指条道儿，至少帮他们打下发财的根基。最后，我在近卫军营地里找到一位恩人——冷溪近卫团（Coldstreams）的 D——上校，他好心许给我一只营地里的名宠，或许本书的一些读者也有耳闻，它名叫平基（Pinkie）。当时平基正在帮一位军官兄弟清剿他屋里的鼠患，但第二天，一名近卫兵就把这位高贵的家伙送下来了。它过了几天锦衣玉食的日子，可它对老家怀着一副英国猫式的眷恋，任凭这里猎物丰盛，平基很快便偷偷跑回三英里外旧主的住处去了。弟兄们不止一次把它给我抱回来，但斯普林希尔的魅力，从来不足以把它长久留在我身边。

===Original===
From the human thieves that surrounded Spring Hill I had to stand as sharp a siege as the Russians had in that poor city against which we heard the guns thundering daily; while the most cunning and desperate sorties were often made upon the most exposed parts of my defences, and sometimes with success. Scores of the keenest eyes and hundreds of the sharpest fingers in the world were always ready to take advantage of the least oversight. I had to keep two boys, whose chief occupation was to watch the officers’ horses, tied up to the doorposts of the British Hotel. Before I adopted this safeguard, more than one officer would leave his horse for a few minutes, and on his return find it gone to the neighbourhood of the Naval Brigade, or the horse-fair at Kamiesch. My old friends, the Zouaves, soon found me out at Spring Hill, and the wiry, light-fingered, fighting-loving gentry spent much of their leisure there. Those confounded trousers of theirs offered conveniences of stowage-room which they made rare use of. Nothing was too small, and few things too unwieldy, to ride in them; like the pockets of clown in a pantomime, they could accommodate a well-grown baby or a pound of sausages equally well. I have a firm conviction that they stuffed turkeys, geese, and fowls into them, and I positively know that my only respectable teapot travelled off in the same conveyance, while I detected one little fellow, who had tied them down tight at his ankles, stowing away some pounds of tea and coffee mixed. Some officers, who were present, cut the cords, and, holding up the little scamp by the neck, shook his trousers empty amid shouts of laughter.

Our live stock, from the horses and mules down to the geese and fowls, suffered terribly. Although we kept a sharp lookout by day, and paid a man five shillings a night as watchman, our losses were very great. During the time we were in the Crimea we lost over a score of horses, four mules, eighty goats, many sheep, pigs, and poultry, by thieving alone. We missed in a single night forty goats and seven sheep, and on Mr. Day’s going to headquarters with intelligence of the disaster, they told him that Lord Raglan had recently received forty sheep from Asia, all of which had disappeared in the same manner. The geese, turkeys, and fowls vanished by scores. We found out afterwards that the watchman paid to guard the sheep, used to kill a few occasionally. As he represented them to have died a natural death during the night, he got permission to bury them, instead of which he sold them. King Frost claimed his share of our stock too, and on one December night, of the winter of 1855, killed no less than forty sheep. It is all very well to smile at these things now, but at the time they were heartrending enough, and helped, if they did not cause, the ruin which eventually overtook the firm of Seacole and Day. The determination and zeal which besiegers and besieged showed with respect to a poor pig, which was quietly and unconsciously fattening in its sty, are worthy of record.

===Chinese===
对于围在斯普林希尔四周的人盗，我所承受的围困之紧，不亚于俄国人困守的那座可怜城池——我们每天都能听见大炮朝它轰鸣；而对我的防线上最暴露的地段，最狡猾、最不要命的突击也时常发起，而且有时竟能得手。几十双世上最尖的眼睛、几百根世上最快的手指，随时准备钻我一点疏忽的空子。我不得不雇上两个男孩，他们的主要差事就是看守拴在不列颠旅馆门柱上的军官坐骑。在我采用这道保险之前，不止一位军官把马撂下几分钟，回头就发现马已经去了海军旅（Naval Brigade）驻地一带，或是卡米什的马市。我的老朋友朱阿夫兵，很快就在斯普林希尔寻到了我，这帮筋骨结实、手脚不净、好勇斗狠的家伙，把大把闲暇都泡在了这里。他们那条可恶的裤子所提供的储物便利，被他们利用得淋漓尽致。再小的东西不嫌小，几乎没有什么大到装不下；就像哑剧里小丑的口袋，装一个发育良好的婴孩和装一磅香肠一样不在话下。我坚信不疑，他们往里塞过火鸡、鹅和家禽；我还确凿知道，我唯一一把体面的茶壶就是搭这同一种交通工具远走的；我还当场抓住一个小家伙，他把裤脚在脚踝处扎得紧紧的，正往里塞好几磅茶叶和咖啡的混合物。几位在场军官割断绳索，揪住那小捣蛋的脖子把他提起来，在一片哄笑中把他裤子里的东西抖了个干净。

我们的牲畜，从马骡到鹅和家禽，遭殃遭得厉害。尽管我们白天严加看管，夜里还花 5 先令雇人守望，损失仍然大得吓人。我们在克里米亚期间，光是被偷就丢了 20 多匹马、4 头骡子、80 只山羊，还有许多绵羊、猪和家禽。有一夜我们同时丢了 40 只山羊和 7 只绵羊；戴先生赶到司令部报告这场灾情，人家告诉他，拉格伦勋爵新近从亚洲收到 40 只绵羊，全都不声不响地照此方式失踪了。鹅、火鸡和鸡，成群成群地消失。后来我们才发现，雇来看羊的那个守望人，隔三差五就要宰掉几只。他谎称羊是夜里自然死的，获准把它们埋掉，结果转头就卖了。霜王（King Frost）也来分我们的牲口，1855 年那个冬天，十二月里的一个夜晚，就冻死了不下 40 只绵羊。如今拿这些事笑笑无妨，可在当时，它们桩桩件件都足以让人心碎，即便不是它们直接造成、至少也大大促成了「西科尔与戴」商号最终的败落。围攻者与被围者双方在一头可怜的猪身上表现出的决心与热忱——那猪正在圈里安然不觉地长膘——很值得记录在案。

===Original===
Fresh pork, in the spring of 1855, was certainly one of those luxuries not easily obtainable in that part of the Crimea to which the British army was confined, and when it became known that Mother Seacole had purchased a promising young porker from one of the ships in Balaclava, and that, brave woman! she had formed the courageous resolution of fattening it for her favourites, the excitement among the frequenters of Spring Hill was very great. I could laugh heartily now, when I think of the amount of persuasion and courting I stood out for before I bound myself how its four legs were to be disposed of. I learnt more at that time of the trials and privileges of authority than I am ever likely to experience again. Upon my word, I think if the poor thing had possessed as many legs as my editor tells me somebody called the Hydra (with whom my readers are perhaps more familiar than I am) had heads, I should have found candidates for them. As it was, the contest for those I had to bestow was very keen, and the lucky individuals who were favoured by me looked after their interests most carefully. One of them, to render mistake or misunderstanding impossible, entered my promise in my daybook. The reader will perhaps smile at the following important memorandum in the gallant officer’s writing:—

> "Memorandum that Mrs. Seacole did this day, in the presence of Major A——— and Lieutenant W———, promise Captain H———, R.A., a leg of *the* pig."

===Chinese===
1855 年的春天，鲜猪肉在英军驻地的那片克里米亚，实在是难得一求的奢侈品。等到大家听说西科尔妈妈从巴拉克拉瓦的一条船上购得一头前途无量的年轻猪仔，而且——好勇敢的女人！——毅然下定决心，要把它养肥了孝敬她的心头好们，斯普林希尔的常客们顿时群情鼎沸。如今想起当初我顶着多少游说与巴结，才最终定下那四条腿各自的归属，我还能开怀大笑。那阵子我对权威带来的烦恼与好处所领教的，比这辈子可能再遇到的加起来还多。说实在的，我想，倘若那可怜的东西长的腿，有我的编辑告诉我的那个叫九头蛇（Hydra）的家伙——我的读者们对它想必比我还熟——长的头那么多，我照样能替每一条腿找到主顾。实际情况是，我手里这几条腿的争夺已经十分激烈，蒙我垂青的幸运儿们个个寸利必争、看管极严。其中一位，为了杜绝差错或误解，把我的承诺记进了我的流水账。下面这条出自那位英武军官手笔的重要备忘录，也许会让读者莞尔：——

> 「备忘录：西科尔夫人今日于 A——少校与 W——中尉在场之时，允诺皇家炮兵（R.A.）H——上尉猪腿一条——*那头*猪的腿。」

===Original===
Now it was well known that many greedy eyes and fingers were directed towards the plump fellow, and considerable interest was manifested in the result of the struggle, "Mrs. Seacole versus Thievery." I think they had some confidence in me, and that I was the favourite; but there was a large field against me, which found its backers also; and many a bet was laughingly laid on the ultimate fate of the unconscious porker.

I baffled many a knavish trick to gain possession of the fine fellow; but, after all, I lost him in the middle of the day, when I thought the boldest rogues would not have run the risk. The shouts and laughter of some officers who were riding down from the front first informed me of my loss. Up they rode, calling out—"Mother Seacole! old lady! quick!—*the* pig’s gone!"

I rushed out, injured woman that I was, and saw it all at a glance. But that my straw wide-awake was in the way, I could have torn my hair in my vexation. I rushed to the sty, found the nest warm, and with prompt decision prepared for speedy pursuit. Back I came to the horsemen, calling out—"Off with you, my sons!—they can’t have got very far away yet. Do your best to save my bacon!"

===Chinese===
谁都知道，许多贪婪的眼睛和手指都对准了那胖家伙，「西科尔夫人对窃贼」这场角力的胜负，牵动着广泛的关切。我想大家对我多少有点信心，我是夺标的热门；可跟我作对的是一大片，那一方也找到了肯下注的人；人们在哄笑之中，就那头浑然不觉的猪仔的最终命运，打了不知多少赌。

我挫败了一个又一个想把这位好家伙弄到手的奸计；可到头来，我还是在大白天丢了它——我还以为最大胆的贼也不敢在那个时辰冒这个险呢。几位从前线骑马下来的军官的叫嚷和哄笑，最先报知了我的损失。他们策马而来，一路喊——「西科尔妈妈！老太太！快！——*那头*猪没了！」

我冲了出去——好一个受害的妇人——一眼就看清了全部情形。若不是头上那顶宽檐草帽（wide-awake）碍事，我非要在懊恼中扯乱自己的头发不可。我冲到猪圈，一摸窝还温热，当机立断，准备火速追击。我折回骑手们那里，喊道——「快出发，孩子们！——他们还跑不远。使出你们的全副本事，救救我的猪肉！」

===Original===
Delighted with the fun, the horsemen dispersed, laughing and shouting—"Stole away! hark away!" while I ran indoors, turned out all my available bodyguard, and started in pursuit also. Not half a mile off we soon saw a horseman wave his cap; and starting off into a run, came to a little hollow, where the poor panting animal and two Greek thieves had been run down. The Provost-marshal took the latter in hand willingly, and Piggy was brought home in triumph. But those who had pork expectancies, hearing of the adventure, grew so seriously alarmed at the narrow escape, that they petitioned me to run so desperate a hazard no longer; and the poor thing was killed on the following day, and distributed according to promise. A certain portion was reserved for sausages, which, fried with mashed potatoes, were quite the rage at the British Hotel for some days. Some pork was also sent to headquarters, with an account of the dangers we ran from thieves. It drew the following kind acknowledgment from General B———:

> "My dear Mrs. Seacole—I am very much obliged to you indeed for your pork. I have spoken to Colonel P——— as to the police of your neighbourhood, and he will see what arrangement can be made for the general protection of that line of road. When the highroad is finished, you will be better off. Let me know at the time of any depredations that are committed, and we will try and protect you.—I am, faithfully yours,
> "M. L. B———."

===Chinese===
骑手们被这场乐子哄得兴高采烈，四散开来，又笑又喊——「追啊！撵啊！」我则跑进屋，把能出动的卫兵统统拉了出来，也加入了追击。不出半英里，我们就望见一个骑手挥舞帽子；大家撒腿奔去，赶到一处小洼地，那可怜的、气喘吁吁的猪和两个希腊贼都已被截住。宪兵司令欣然接管了后两位，猪仔则被凯旋般地押回了家。可那些指望着分猪肉的人，听说这场险情，个个惊恐万状，联名恳求我再别冒这种可怕的凶险；于是第二天，那可怜的东西就被宰了，按承诺分派了下去。有一部分留做了香肠，就着土豆泥一煎，好几天里在不列颠旅馆风靡一时。还有些猪肉送到了司令部，随附一份我们遭贼风险的报告。它换来 B——将军下面这封好心的谢函：

> 「亲爱的西科尔夫人——实在非常感谢您的猪肉。关于贵处一带的治安，我已同 P——上校谈过，他会看看能作何安排，把那条道路沿线整体保护起来。公路修成之后，你们的境况会好一些。凡有劫掠发生，请随时告知，我们定当尽力保护你们。——您忠实的，
> 「M. L. B——。」

===Original===
For the truth was—although I can laugh at my fears now—I was often most horribly frightened at Spring Hill; and there was cause for it too. My washerwoman, who, with her family, lived not half a mile from us, was with me one day, and carried off some things for the wash. On the following morning I was horrified to learn that she, her father, husband, and children—in all, seven—had been most foully murdered during the night: only one of the whole family recovered from her wounds, and lived to tell the tale. It created a great sensation at the time, and caused me to pass many a sleepless night, for the murderers were never discovered.

Whilst I am upon the subject of Crimean thievery, I may as well exhaust it without paying any regard to the chronological order of my reminiscences. I have before mentioned what I suffered from the French. One day I caught one of our allies in my kitchen, robbing me in the most ungrateful manner. He had met with an accident near Spring Hill (I believe he belonged to a French regiment lent to assist the English in road-making), and had been doctored by me; and now I found him filling his pockets, before taking "French" leave of us. My black man, Francis, pulled from his pockets a yet warm fowl, and other provisions. We kicked him off the premises, and he found refuge with some men of the Army Works Corps, who pitied him and gave him shelter. He woke them in the middle of the night, laying hands rather clumsily on everything that was removeable; and in the morning they brought him to me, to ask what they should do with him. Unluckily for him, a French officer of rank happened to be in the store, who, on hearing our tale, packed him off to his regiment. I gathered from the expression of the officer’s face, and the dread legible upon the culprit’s, that it might be some considerable time before his itch for breaking the eighth commandment could be again indulged in.

===Chinese===
实情是——尽管如今我可以笑话自己当年的恐惧——我在斯普林希尔常常吓得魂不附体；而且这恐惧并非无缘无故。我的洗衣妇带着一家人住在离我们不出半英里的地方。有一天她来我这里，取走了一些待洗的衣物。第二天早晨我惊悉，她连同她的父亲、丈夫和孩子们——一共 7 口——当夜全部惨遭毒手：全家只有一个人养好了伤，活下来讲述了这段惨事。此事当时轰动一时，害我度过了许多不眠之夜，因为凶手始终没有查明。

既然谈到了克里米亚的偷窃，我不妨把这个话题一次讲尽，也顾不上回忆录的时序了。我先前已讲过法国人给我带来的苦头。有一天，我在自家厨房里当场拿住我们的一位盟友，他正以最忘恩负义的方式偷我。他先前在斯普林希尔附近出过事故（我想他属于派来帮英国人修路的一个法国团），是我给他治的伤；如今却见他临走行「法兰西式告别」之前，正往口袋里塞东西。我的黑人伙计弗朗西斯从他口袋里掏出一只还温热的母鸡，还有别的吃食。我们把他轰出了院子，他跑去军工劳役队的几个士兵那里，他们可怜他，收留了他。半夜里他叫醒了他们——手脚相当笨拙地摸走了每一件拿得动的东西；早晨他们把他扭送到我这里，问我该拿他怎么办。他倒霉的是，店里恰有一位高级法国军官，听完我们的陈述，把他打发回了团里。从那位军官脸上的神情，以及罪犯脸上明明白白的恐惧，我推测，他想再过一把触犯第八诫的瘾，恐怕得等上好一阵子了。

===Original===
The trouble I underwent respecting a useful black mare, for which Mr. Day had given thirty guineas, and which carried me beautifully, was immense. Before it had been many weeks in our store it was gone—whither, I failed to discover. Keeping my eyes wide open, however, I saw "Angelina"—so I christened her—coming quietly down the hill, carrying an elderly naval officer. I was ready to receive the unconscious couple, and soon made my claim good. Of course, the officer was not to blame. He had bought it of a sailor, who in his turn had purchased the animal of a messmate, who of course had obtained it from another, and so on; but eventually it returned to its old quarters, where it only remained about a fortnight. I grew tired of looking for Angelina, and had given her up, when one day she turned up, in capital condition, in the possession of a French officer of Chasseurs. But nothing I could say to the Frenchman would induce him to take the view of the matter I wished, but had no right to enforce. He had bought the horse at Kamiesch, and intended to keep it. We grew hot at last; and our dispute drew out so large an audience that the Frenchman took alarm, and tried to make off. I held on to Angelina for a little while; but at last the mare broke away from me, as Tam o’ Shanter’s Maggie did from the witches (I don’t mean that she left me even her tail), and vanished in a cloud of dust. It was the last I ever saw of Angelina.

More than once the Crimean thievery reduced us to woeful straits. To a Greek, returning to Constantinople, we entrusted (after the murder of our washerwoman) two trunks, containing "things for the wash," which he was to bring back as soon as possible. But neither upon Greek, trunks, nor their contents did we ever set eyes again. It was a serious loss. The best part of our tablecloths and other domestic linen, all my clothes, except two suits, and all of Mr. Day’s linen vanished, and had to be replaced as best we could by fresh purchases from Kamiesch and Kadikoi.

===Chinese===
为一匹得力的黑色母马，我操的心思更是无穷无尽——戴先生花了 30 基尼买下它，它驮起我来稳当极了。它在我们棚屋里还没住满几个星期就没了影——去了哪里，我怎么也查不出来。可我把眼睛瞪得大大的，终于看见「安杰丽娜」（Angelina）——我给它起的名字——驮着一位上了年纪的海军军官，慢悠悠地下山来。我早已恭候这对浑然不觉的组合，三下两下就证明了我的所有权。当然，那位军官没有责任。他是从一个水手手里买的，水手又是从一位同袍那里买的，那位同袍自然又是从另一个人手里弄来的，如此层层往下；不过它最终回到了老家，而老家也只留了它大约两个星期。我找安杰丽娜找累了，已然把它放弃，有一天它却露了面，膘肥体壮，落在一个法国猎骑兵（Chasseurs）军官手里。可任凭我说什么，那法国人都不肯接受我希望的——而我并无权利强求的——那种看法。这匹马是他在卡米什买的，他打算留着。我们终于吵得火起；争执引来了一大群看客，那法国人慌了，想溜。我抓住安杰丽娜不放，僵持了一小会儿；可它终究从我手里挣脱了，就像塔姆·奥香特（Tam o' Shanter）的老马麦吉甩开女巫们那样（我倒不是说它连尾巴都没给我留下），卷起一团尘土，消失得无影无踪。那是我最后一次见到安杰丽娜。

克里米亚的偷窃不止一次把我们逼入愁苦的窘境。（洗衣妇遇害之后）我们把 2 只衣箱托付给一个要回君士坦丁堡的希腊人，箱内装着「待洗衣物」，他本该尽早捎回来。可从此，那希腊人也好，衣箱也好，箱中之物也好，我们再没见过一眼。这是场惨重的损失。我们桌布和家用布草里最好的部分、我的全部衣裳（只剩 2 套）、戴先生的全部内衣衬衣，统统没了踪影，只好尽量从卡米什和卡迪科伊重新购置补上。

===Original===
Perhaps the most ridiculous shift I was ever put to by the Crimean thieves happened when we rose one morning and found the greater part of our stud missing. I had, in the course of the day, urgent occasion to ride over to the French camp on the Tchernaya; the only animal available for my transport was an old grey mare, who had contracted some equine disease of which I do not know the name, but which gave her considerable resemblance to a dog suffering from the mange. Now, go to the French camp I must; to borrow a horse was impossible, and something must be done with the grey. Suddenly one of those happy thoughts, which sometimes help us over our greatest difficulties, entered into my scheming brains. Could I not conceal the poor mare’s worst blemishes. Her colour was grey; would not a thick coating of flour from my dredger make all right? There was no time to be lost; the remedy was administered successfully, and off I started; but, alas! the wind was high and swept the skirts of my riding habit so determinedly against the side of the poor beast, that before long its false coat was transferred to the dark cloth, and my innocent ruse exposed. The French are proverbially and really a polite and considerate nation, but I never heard more hearty peals of laughter from any sides than those which conveyed to me the horrible assurance that my scheme had unhappily failed.

===Chinese===
克里米亚的贼把我逼出来的最滑稽的一次应急，也许要数那天早晨：我们起床发现大半马匹不见了。当天我有一桩急事，必须骑马过河去切尔纳亚河（Tchernaya）边的法国营地；唯一能用的脚力是一匹灰色老母马，它染上了一种我叫不出名字的马病，那模样活像一条害疥癣的狗。可是，法国营地我非去不可；借马又借不到，这匹灰马总得想个法子。忽然，一个妙主意——那种偶尔能助我们渡过最大难关的好主意——钻进了我那惯耍花招的脑袋。我难道不能把这可怜母马最丢人的瑕疵遮一遮？它毛色是灰的，用我的面粉筛给它厚厚扑上一层面粉，岂不万事大吉？事不宜迟；这剂药方施用得非常成功，我便出发了；可是，唉！风刮得紧，把我的骑装裙摆一个劲儿地扫向那可怜牲口的身侧，没过多久，它那件假外衣就转移到我的深色呢料上，我无辜的小计谋当场败露。法国人素有讲究礼貌、体贴入微之名，实际上也确实如此；可我还从来没听见过哪一阵笑声比那一阵更由衷——那笑声向我传达了一个可怕的确证：我的妙计不幸砸了锅。

## XIII / XIII

===Original===
My work in the Crimea.

===Chinese===
我在克里米亚的工作。

===Original===
I hope the reader will give me credit for the assertion that I am about to make, viz., that I enter upon the particulars of this chapter with great reluctance; but I cannot omit them, for the simple reason that they strengthen my one and only claim to interest the public, viz., my services to the brave British army in the Crimea. But, fortunately, I can follow a course which will not only render it unnecessary for me to sound my own trumpet, but will be more satisfactory to the reader. I can put on record the written opinions of those who had ample means of judging and ascertaining how I fulfilled the great object which I had in view in leaving England for the Crimea; and before I do so, I must solicit my readers’ attention to the position I held in the camp as doctress, nurse, and “mother.”

I have never been long in any place before I have found my practical experience in the science of medicine useful. Even in London I have found it of service to others. And in the Crimea, where the doctors were so overworked, and sickness was so prevalent, I could not be long idle; for I never forgot that my intention in seeking the army was to help the kindhearted doctors, to be useful to whom I have ever looked upon and still regard as so high a privilege.

But before very long I found myself surrounded with patients of my own, and this for two simple reasons. In the first place, the men (I am speaking of the “ranks” now) had a very serious objection to going into hospital for any but urgent reasons, and the regimental doctors were rather fond of sending them there; and, in the second place, they could and did get at my store sick-comforts and nourishing food, which the heads of the medical staff would sometimes find it difficult to procure. These reasons, with the additional one that I was very familiar with the diseases which they suffered most from, and successful in their treatment (I say this in no spirit of vanity), were quite sufficient to account for the numbers who came daily to the British Hotel for medical treatment.

===Chinese===
我希望读者能相信我接下来要作的这一句表白：着手细写本章的种种详情，我是极不情愿的；但我不能略去它们，原因很简单——它们能支撑我唯一赖以引起公众关注的主张，也就是我为克里米亚那支英勇英军所尽的服务。所幸，我可以走一条既不必自吹自擂、又更令读者满意的路子：把那些有充分条件判断与查证的人们的书面意见记录在案——查证我离开英国远赴克里米亚，究竟有没有实现我心中怀抱的那个重大目标。而在这样做之前，我必须请读者留意我在营中所处的位置——女医士、看护，还有「妈妈」。

每到一地，用不了多久，我便会发现自己行医的实践经验派得上用场。即便在伦敦，我也发现它于他人有益。而在克里米亚，军医们操劳过度，疾病又如此肆虐，我更不可能长久闲着；因为我从未忘记，我投奔大军的本意就是襄助那些好心的军医——能为他们效力，向来被我视为、至今仍视为一份莫大的荣幸。

但没过多久，我便发现自己身边围起了自己的一群病人，原因说来简单，只有两条。其一，士兵们（我现在说的是行伍士兵）除非事出紧急，极其不愿意进医院，而团里的军医又偏偏爱把他们往那儿送；其二，他们在我的铺子里能够、也确实买得到病号滋补品和营养吃食，而这些东西医务部门的长官们有时还难以弄到。再加上一条：我对他们最常患的那些疾病了如指掌，治起来颇为见效（我说这话绝无自夸之意）——这几条理由，尽足以解释为什么每天都有成群的人到不列颠旅馆来求医问药。

===Original===
That the officers were glad of me as a doctress and nurse may be easily understood. When a poor fellow lay sickening in his cheerless hut and sent down to me, he knew very well that I should not ride up in answer to his message empty-handed. And although I did not hesitate to charge him with the value of the necessaries I took him, still he was thankful enough to be able to *purchase* them. When we lie ill at home surrounded with comfort, we never think of feeling any special gratitude for the sickroom delicacies which we accept as a consequence of our illness; but the poor officer lying ill and weary in his crazy hut, dependent for the merest necessaries of existence upon a clumsy, ignorant soldier-cook, who would almost prefer eating his meat raw to having the trouble of cooking it (our English soldiers are bad campaigners), often finds his greatest troubles in the want of those little delicacies with which a weak stomach must be humoured into retaining nourishment. How often have I felt sad at the sight of poor lads, who in England thought attending early parade a hardship, and felt harassed if their neckcloths set awry, or the natty little boots would not retain their polish, bearing, and bearing so nobly and bravely, trials and hardships to which the veteran campaigner frequently succumbed. Don’t you think, reader, if you were lying, with parched lips and fading appetite, thousands of miles from mother, wife, or sister, loathing the rough food by your side, and thinking regretfully of that English home where nothing that could minister to your great need would be left untried﻿—don’t you think that you would welcome the familiar figure of the stout lady whose bony horse has just pulled up at the door of your hut, and whose panniers contain some cooling drink, a little broth, some homely cake, or a dish of jelly or blancmange﻿—don’t you think, under such circumstances, that you would heartily agree with my friend *Punch*’s remark:﻿—

> “That berry-brown face, with a kind heart’s trace

 Impressed on each wrinkle sly,

 Was a sight to behold, through the snow-clouds rolled
 Across that iron sky.”

===Chinese===
军官们有我这样一位女医士兼看护而感到庆幸，是不难理解的。当一个可怜人蔫蔫地躺在凄清的小屋里，派人下山来叫我，他心里很清楚，我绝不会空着双手策马应召而来。尽管我给他捎去的必需品照价收钱、毫不客气，可他仍旧感激不尽，因为这些东西他总算*买得到*。当我们在家养病，四下舒适周全，我们从不会想到要为病榻上的精致吃食特表感激——那不过是生病顺带得来的东西；可那可怜的军官，病倦交加地躺在摇摇欲坠的小屋里，连活命的最起码需求都得仰仗一个笨手笨脚、一窍不通的士兵厨子——那厨子几乎宁可生吞肉也不肯费事烹煮（我们的英国士兵实在不善打理战地营生）——他最大的苦恼，往往就是缺了那一点点精致吃食，好哄着虚弱的胃口把滋养留得住。多少回，我眼见那些可怜的小伙子们心里发酸：在英国时，早起出操便叫苦连天，领巾歪了一点、锃亮的小军靴失了光泽，便觉浑身不自在；如今他们承受着的——承受得那么高贵、那么勇敢——竟是沙场老兵也常常支撑不住的磨难与艰辛。读者啊，倘若你也唇焦口燥、食欲日消，躺在离家千万英里之外，看不见母亲、妻子或姐妹，厌恶身边粗粝的饭食，满怀眷恋地想着那个英国家园——在那里，但凡能解你燃眉之急的办法，家人没有一样不会替你试遍——你不觉得，你会热切欢迎那位壮实太太的熟悉身影吗？她那匹瘦骨嶙峋的马刚在你的棚屋门口收住脚步，鞍边的驮筐里装着清凉饮品、一小锅肉汤、家常糕点，或者一碟果冻、一碟牛奶冻——这样的情形之下，你不觉得你会由衷赞同我那位好朋友《笨拙》（*Punch*）杂志的诗句吗：——

> 「那张浆果棕红的面庞，带着善心的印记，
> 印在每一道狡黠的皱纹里，
> 透过翻滚的雪云，仍是值得一见的景象，
> 掠过那铁灰色的天宇。」

===Original===
I tell you, reader, I have seen many a bold fellow’s eyes moisten at such a season, when a woman’s voice and a woman’s care have brought to their minds recollections of those happy English homes which some of them never saw again; but many did, who will remember their woman-comrade upon the bleak and barren heights before Sebastopol.

Then their calling me “mother” was not, I think, altogether unmeaning. I used to fancy that there was something homely in the word; and, reader, you cannot think how dear to them was the smallest thing that reminded them of home.

Some of my Crimean patients, who were glad of me as nurse and doctress, bore names familiar to all England, and perhaps, did I ask them, they would allow me to publish those names. I am proud to think that a gallant sailor, on whose brave breast the order of Victoria rests﻿—a more gallant man can never wear it﻿—sent for the doctress whom he had known in Kingston, when his arm, wounded on the fatal 18th of June, refused to heal, and I think that the application I recommended did it good; but I shall let some of my patients’ letters, taken from a large bundle, speak for me. Of course I must suppress most of their names. Here are two from one of my best and kindest sons.

> “My dear Mamma﻿—Will you kindly give the bearer the bottle you promised me when you were here this morning, for my jaundice. Please let me know how much I am to take of it. Yours truly,
> “F. M., C.E.”

You see the medicine does him good, for a few days later comes another from the same writer:﻿—

> “My dear Mrs. Seacole﻿—I have finished the bottle, which has done my jaundice a deal of good. Will you kindly send another by bearer. Truly yours,
> “F. M.”

===Chinese===
我告诉你，读者，这样的时节，我见过多少胆气逼人的汉子眼眶湿润——当一个女人的声音、一个女人的照料，唤起他们对那些幸福英伦家庭的回忆；他们中间有些人，再也没能重见家园；但也有许多人回去了，他们会长久记得塞瓦斯托波尔城前那片荒凉贫瘠的高地上，与他们并肩吃苦的女战友。

那么，他们喊我「妈妈」，我想，绝非全无深意。我常常觉得，这个字眼里有种家的味道；读者啊，你无法想象，凡是能让他们想起家的东西，哪怕再微不足道，在他们眼里有多么珍贵。

我在克里米亚的一些病人，乐得有我做看护和女医士，其中有些名字在全英国家喻户晓；倘若我去征求同意，他们或许会允许我把名字刊印出来。我颇感骄傲地想到：有一位英勇的水兵，那枚维多利亚勋章就佩在他勇敢的胸前——再没有比他更配佩戴它的人了——当他的手臂在那致命的 6 月 18 日所受的创伤迟迟不愈时，他派人请来了这位他在金斯敦便已相识的女医士；我想，我推荐的外敷药剂确见了效。不过，还是让我从一大捆病人来信中挑出几封，让他们替我说话吧。当然，多数人的名字我必须隐去。这里有同一个最好、最贴心的儿子写来的两封。

> 「亲爱的妈妈——今晨您来时答应给我的那瓶治黄疸的药，烦请交给来人。请告知我该服用多少。您真挚的，
> 「F. M.（C.E.）」

你瞧，这药对他有效，因为几天之后，同一位又来了一封：——

> 「亲爱的西科尔夫人——那瓶药我服完了，我的黄疸受益良多。烦请再托来人捎一瓶。您真挚的，
> 「F. M.」

===Original===
It was a capital prescription which had done his jaundice good. There was so great a demand for it, that I kept it mixed in a large pan, ready to ladle it out to the scores of applicants who came for it.

Sometimes they would send for other and no less important medicines. Here is such an application from a sick officer:﻿—

> “Mrs. Seacole would confer a favour on the writer, who is very ill, by giving his servant (the bearer) a boiled or roast fowl; if it be impossible to obtain them, some chicken broth would be very acceptable.
> “I am yours, truly obliged,
> “J. K., 18th R.S.”

Doesn’t that read like a sick man’s letter, glad enough to welcome any woman’s face? Here are some gentlemen of the Commissariat anxious to speak for me:﻿—

===Chinese===
那是一张灵验的药方，治好了他的黄疸。这药的需求量大得很，我索性用一口大锅把它配好备着，随时舀给成群结队前来求药的人。

有时他们也会来讨别的、同样要紧的「药」。这里就有一位病中军官的这样一纸请求：——

> 「鄙人卧病甚重，倘蒙西科尔夫人将一只煮鸡或烤鸡交给鄙人的仆人（即来人），则感激不尽；若实在无法办到，些许鸡汤亦所深谢。
> 「您由衷感激的，
> 「J. K.，第 18 团（R.S.）」

这读起来岂不像一封病人的信——病中之人，见到任何一张女性的面孔都倍感欣喜？这里有几位军需部的先生们急着替我说话：——

===Original===
> “Arthur C﻿—﻿—, Comm. Staff Officer, having been attacked one evening with a very bad diarrhoea at Mrs. Seacole’s, took some of her good medicine. It cured me before the next morning, and I have never been attacked since.﻿—October 17th, 1855.”

> “Archibald R. L﻿—﻿—, Comm. Staff, Crimea, was suffering from diarrhoea for a week or more; after taking Mrs. Seacole’s good medicines for two days, he became quite well, and remained so to this day.﻿—October 17th, 1855.”

Here is Mr. M﻿—﻿—, paymaster of the Land Transport Corps, ready with a good account of my services:﻿—

> “I certify that Madame Seacole twice cured me effectually of dysentery while in the Crimea, and also my clerk and the men of my corps, to my certain knowledge.”

===Chinese===
> 「阿瑟·C——（Arthur C——），军需部参谋军官，一天傍晚在西科尔夫人处突发严重腹泻，服了她的好药。次日早晨之前便已痊愈，此后再未犯过。——1855 年 10 月 17 日。」

> 「阿奇博尔德·R. L——（Archibald R. L——），克里米亚军需部，腹泻一周有余；服用西科尔夫人好药两日，霍然痊愈，直至今日依旧康健。——1855 年 10 月 17 日。」

这位是陆运军团（Land Transport Corps）的军需官 M——先生，正备好一份称道我服务的好证词：——

> 「本人证明，西科尔夫人在克里米亚期间曾两次根治本人的痢疾；据本人确切所知，她还治好过我手下的文书及我团众多士兵。」

===Original===
And some of the men shall speak for themselves:﻿—

> “I certify that I was severely attacked by diarrhoea after landing in the Crimea. I took a great deal of medicine, but nothing served me until I called on Mrs. Seacole. She gave me her medicine but once, and I was cured effectually.
> “Wm. Knollys, Sergt., L.T.C.”

> “This is to certify that Wm. Row, L.T.C., had a severe attack of illness, and was in a short time restored to health by the prompt attention and medical skill of Mrs. Seacole, British Hotel, Spring Hill, Crimea.”

Many of my patients belonged to the Land Transport and Army Works Corps. The former indeed were in my close neighbourhood, and their hospital was nearly opposite to the British Hotel. I did all I could for them, and have many letters expressive of their gratitude. From them I select the following:﻿—

> “I have much pleasure in bearing testimony to Mrs. Seacole’s kindness and attention to the sick of the Railway Labourers’ Army Works Corps and Land Transport Corps during the winters of 1854 and 1855.
> “She not only, from the knowledge she had acquired in the West Indies, was enabled to administer appropriate remedies for their ailments, but, what was of as much or more importance, she charitably furnished them with proper nourishment, which they had no means of obtaining except in the hospital, and most of that class had an objection to go into hospital, particularly the railway labourers and the men of the Army Works Corps.
> “John Hall, 
 “Inspector-General of Hospitals.”

===Chinese===
也让士兵们自己开口说话：——

> 「本人证明，登陆克里米亚后突患严重腹泻。服过许多药，全不见效，直到求助于西科尔夫人。她的药我只服了一次，便彻底痊愈。
> 「威廉·诺利斯（Wm. Knollys），中士，陆运军团（L.T.C.）。」

> 「兹证明陆运军团威廉·罗（Wm. Row）突患重病，蒙克里米亚斯普林希尔不列颠旅馆西科尔夫人及时救治、妙手施药，旋即康复。」

我的病人当中，有许多属于陆运军团和军工劳役队（Army Works Corps）。前者就住在我近旁，他们的医院几乎正对着不列颠旅馆。我为他们尽了全力，手里存着许多表达谢意的信件。从中我选出下面这封：——

> 「本人万分乐意为西科尔夫人在 1854 与 1855 年两个冬天里，对铁路劳工军工劳役队与陆运军团病员的仁慈与照护作证。
> 「她不仅凭其在西印度群岛习得的知识，能为他们的疾患对症下药；而且——这一点同样重要，或更为重要——她还慈善地供给他们适当的滋补吃食。这些吃食，他们除住院外无从获得，而这一类人大多讳疾忌院，铁路劳工与军工劳役队的士兵们尤其如此。
> 「约翰·霍尔（John Hall），
> 「医院总监（Inspector-General of Hospitals）。」

===Original===
I hope that Mr. P﻿—﻿—, of the Army Works Corps, will pardon my laying the following letter before the public:﻿—

> “Dear Mrs. Seacole﻿—It is with feelings of great pleasure that I hear you are safely arrived in England, upon which I beg to congratulate you, and return you many thanks for your kindness whilst in the Crimea.
> “The bitter sherry you kindly made up for me was in truth a great blessing to both myself and my son, and as I expect to go to Bombay shortly, I would feel grateful to you if you would favour me with the receipt for making it, as it appears to be so very grateful a beverage for weakness and bowel complaints in a warm climate. With many kind regards, believe me, dear madam, your obliged servant,
> “Samuel P﻿—﻿—, 
 “Late Superintendent Army Works Corps.”

Here is a certificate from one of the Army Works’ men, to whose case I devoted no little time and trouble:﻿—

> “I certify that I was labouring under a severe attack of diarrhoea last August, and that I was restored to health through the instrumentality and kindness of Mrs. Seacole.
> “I also certify that my fingers were severely jammed whilst at work at Frenchman’s Hill, and Mrs. Seacole cured me after three doctors had fruitlessly attempted to cure them.
> “And I cannot leave the Crimea without testifying to the kindness and skill of Mrs. Seacole, and may God reward her for it.
> “James Wallen, 
 “5th Division Army Works Corps.”

===Chinese===
我希望军工劳役队的 P——先生，能原谅我把下面这封信公之于众：——

> 「亲爱的西科尔夫人——欣闻您已安然抵达英国，谨此致贺，并多谢您在克里米亚期间的诸多好意。
> 「您好心为我配制的那份苦味雪利酒，对我本人和我儿子实在是一大恩物。我不日将赴孟买（Bombay），倘若您肯惠赐其配制配方，我将感激不尽——在炎热地带，它似乎是对体虚与肠胃毛病极其合宜的饮品。谨致诚挚问候，亲爱的夫人，请相信我是您感恩戴德的仆人，
> 「塞缪尔·P——（Samuel P——），
> 「军工劳役队前任总监。」

这是一位军工劳役队士兵的证明，为他的病我花过不少工夫与心血：——

> 「本人证明，去年 8 月我患严重腹泻，全赖西科尔夫人的援手与仁慈，方得康复。
> 「本人并证明，我在法兰西人山（Frenchman's Hill）做工时手指被重物严重压伤，三位医生医治无效，西科尔夫人把我治好了。
> 「离开克里米亚之际，我不能不为西科尔夫人的仁慈与医术作证，愿上帝报答她。
> 「詹姆斯·沃伦（James Wallen），
> 「军工劳役队第 5 师。」

===Original===
Here are three more letters﻿—and the last I shall print﻿—from a sailor, a soldier, and a civilian:﻿—

> “This is to certify that Wm. Adams, caulker, of H.M.S. *Wasp*, and belonging to the Royal Naval Brigade, had a severe attack of cholera, and was cured in a few hours by Mrs. Seacole.”

> “I certify that I was troubled by a severe inflammation of the chest, caused by exposure in the trenches, for about four months, and that Mrs. Seacole’s medicine completely cured me in one month, and may God reward her.
> “Charles Flinn, Sergt. 3rd Co. R.S.M.”

> “Dear Madam﻿—Having been informed by my son, Mr. Edward Gill, of St. George’s Store, Crimea, of his recent illness (jaundice), and of your kind attention and advice to him during that illness, and up to the time he was, by the blessing of God and your assistance, restored to health, permit me, on behalf of myself, my wife, and my family, to return you our most grateful thanks, trusting you may be spared for many years to come, in health of body and vigour of mind, to carry out your benevolent intention. Believe me, my dear madam, yours most gratefully,
> “Edward Gill.”

===Chinese===
这里还有三封信——也是我刊出的最后一批——分别出自一名水兵、一名士兵和一位平民：——

> 「兹证明皇家海军「黄蜂」号（H.M.S. *Wasp*）捻缝工、隶属皇家海军旅（Royal Naval Brigade）的威廉·亚当斯（Wm. Adams）突发重症霍乱，数小时内即为西科尔夫人治愈。」

> 「本人证明，因在战壕中受寒，我胸部患严重炎症约 4 个月，西科尔夫人之药 1 个月内便将我彻底治愈，愿上帝报答她。
> 「查尔斯·弗林（Charles Flinn），中士，皇家工矿兵（R.S.M.）第 3 连。」

> 「亲爱的夫人——据小儿、克里米亚圣乔治商店（St. George's Store）的爱德华·吉尔先生告知，他近日患病（黄疸），病中直至托上帝之福、赖您相助而康复的整个期间，都蒙您慈爱照料、悉心指教。请允许我代表我本人、我的妻子和全家，向您致以最诚挚的谢意，并祈愿上天使您长年康健、精神矍铄，好将您的仁心善愿继续施行下去。请相信我，亲爱的夫人，我是您最感恩戴德的，
> 「爱德华·吉尔（Edward Gill）。」

===Original===
And now that I have made this a chapter of testimonials, I may as well finish them right off, and have done with them altogether. I shall trouble the patient reader with four more only, which I have not the heart to omit.

> “Mrs. Seacole was with the British army in the Crimea from February, 1855, to this time. This excellent woman has frequently exerted herself in the most praiseworthy manner in attending wounded men, even in positions of great danger, and in assisting sick soldiers by all means in her power. In addition, she kept a very good store, and supplied us with many comforts at a time we much required them.
> “Wm. P﻿—﻿—, 
 “Adjutant-General of the British Army in the Crimea.”

> “I have much pleasure in stating that I am acquainted with Mrs. Seacole, and from all that I have seen or heard of her, I believe her to be a useful and good person, kind and charitable.
> “C. A. W﻿—﻿—, 
 “Lt.-Gen. Comm. of Sebastopol.”

The third is from the pen of one who at that time was more looked to, and better known, than any other man in the Crimea. In the 2nd vol. of Russell’s *Letters from the Seat of War*, p. 187, is the following entry:﻿—

> “In the hour of their illness these men (Army Works Corps), in common with many others, have found a kind and successful physician. Close to the railway, halfway between the Col de Balaclava and Kadikoi, Mrs. Seacole, formerly of Kingston and of several other parts of the world, such as Panama and Chagres, has pitched her abode﻿—an iron storehouse with wooden sheds and outlying tributaries﻿—and here she doctors and cures all manner of men with extraordinary success. She is always in attendance near the battlefield to aid the wounded, and has earned many a poor fellow’s blessings.”

===Chinese===
既然本章已写成了证词集，索性一口气收尾，把它们了结干净。我只再劳烦耐心的读者读四封，实在不忍略去。

> 「西科尔夫人自 1855 年 2 月起随英军身处克里米亚，直至此时。这位卓越的女性屡屡以最值得称道的方式尽力救治伤员，甚至身处极度危险之地也不辍，并以她力所能及的一切手段扶助病兵。此外，她还经营着一家极好的铺子，在我们最需要的时节，供给我们诸多慰藉之物。
> 「威廉·P——（Wm. P——），
> 「英国驻克里米亚军副官长（Adjutant-General）。」

> 「本人乐于声明，我与西科尔夫人相识；就我对她的所见所闻而言，我相信她是一位有益于人的良善之人，仁慈而乐善。
> 「C. A. W——，
> 「塞瓦斯托波尔守军司令中将（Lt.-Gen. Comm. of Sebastopol）。」

第三份出自当年克里米亚最受仰望、最负盛名之人的手笔。拉塞尔（Russell）《来自战地的信札》（*Letters from the Seat of War*）第 2 卷第 187 页有这样一段记载：——

> 「这些士兵（军工劳役队）与其他许多人一样，在患病之时找到了一位仁慈而妙手回春的医者。在铁路近旁、巴拉克拉瓦山口（Col de Balaclava）与卡迪科伊之间的半道上，西科尔夫人——她来自金斯敦，也来自巴拿马、查格雷斯等世界上许多别的地方——安下了她的居所：一座铁皮货栈，连带木棚和外围的附属棚屋——在这里，她行医治病，各色人等无不药到病除，成效惊人。她总是守候在战场近旁救助伤员，赢得了不知多少可怜人的祝福。」

===Original===
Yes! I cannot﻿—referring to that time﻿—conscientiously charge myself with doing less for the men who had only thanks to give me, than for the officers whose gratitude gave me the necessaries of life. I think I was ever ready to turn from the latter to help the former, humble as they might be; and they were grateful in their way, and as far as they could be. They would buy me apples and other fruit at Balaclava, and leave them at my store. One made me promise, when I returned home, to send word to his Irish mother, who was to send me a cow in token of her gratitude for the help I had been to her son. I have a book filled with hundreds of the names of those who came to me for medicines and other aids; and never a train of sick or wounded men from the front passed the British Hotel but its hostess was awaiting them to offer comforts to the poor fellows, for whose suffering her heart bled.

*Punch*, who allowed my poor name to appear in the pages which had welcomed Miss Nightingale home﻿—*Punch*, that whimsical mouthpiece of some of the noblest hearts that ever beat beneath black coats﻿—shall last of all raise its voice, that never yet pleaded an unworthy cause, for the Mother Seacole that takes shame to herself for speaking thus of the poor part she bore of the trials and hardships endured on that distant shore, where Britain’s best and bravest wrung hardly Sebastopol from the grasp of Britain’s foe:﻿—

> “No store she set by the epaulette,
 Be it worsted or gold lace;
 For K.C.B. or plain private Smith,
 She had still one pleasant face.
> “And not alone was her kindness shown
 To the hale and hungry lot
 Who drank her grog and ate their prog,
 And paid their honest shot.
> “The sick and sorry can tell the story
 Of her nursing and dosing deeds;
 Regimental M.D. never worked as she,
 In helping sick men’s needs.
> “Of such work, God knows, was as much as she chose
 That dreary winter-tide,
 When Death hung o’er the damp and pestilent camp,
 And his scythe swung far and wide.
> “She gave her aid to all who prayed,
 To hungry and sick and cold;
 Open hand and heart, alike ready to part
 Kind words and acts, and gold.
> “And﻿—be the right man in the right place who can﻿—
 The right woman was Dame Seacole.”

Reader, now that we have come to the end of this chapter, I can say what I have been all anxiety to tell you from its beginning. Please look back to Chapter VIII, and see how hard the right woman had to struggle to convey herself to the right place.

===Chinese===
是的！回望那段岁月，我扪心自问：对于那些只能以道谢回报我的士兵，我自认并不曾比对那些以感激贴补我生计的军官少尽一分心。我想，无论前者多么卑微，我随时乐于撇下后者去帮前者；而他们也以他们自己的方式、在力所能及的范围内感激我。他们会巴巴地在巴拉克拉瓦买来苹果和其他水果，悄悄留在我铺子里。有一个士兵让我许诺，回国后给他爱尔兰的母亲捎个信，他母亲要给我送一头母牛，以答谢我对她儿子的帮助。我有一本册子，写满了数百个来求药求助之人的名字；而每当一列列从前线送下的伤病员经过不列颠旅馆，旅馆的女主人总是早已候在那里，向那些可怜人奉上慰藉——他们的苦难，叫她心都滴血。

《笨拙》——它曾让南丁格尔小姐荣归的名字登上版面，也肯让我这不值一提的名字出现在同一方页面里——《笨拙》，那古灵精怪的喉舌，替几颗曾在黑礼服之下跳动的最高贵的心脏发言——最后，就让它的声音响起吧；这声音还从不曾为一桩不值得的事业辩护过。它要说的是这位西科尔妈妈：她为这般谈论自己在那遥远海岸上分担的那点微末辛劳而羞赧——正是在那里，不列颠最优秀、最勇敢的儿女，才好不容易从不列颠的敌掌中夺下了塞瓦斯托波尔：——

> 「肩章她从不放在眼里，
> 不论毛线还是金绦；
> 巴斯勋爵士（K.C.B.）也好，大兵史密斯也好，
> 她总是一副和悦容貌。
> 「而她的善心并不只向
> 那健壮饕餮的一群
> 喝她的烈酒、吃她的饭食、
> 照价付账的人们。
> 「病中的、愁苦的，才说得清
> 她看护施药的种种事迹；
> 团里的军医从没人像她那样，
> 救治病兵如此尽心竭力。
> 「这样的活计，天知道，她甘愿做了多少
> 在那凄苦的冬日，
> 死神悬在潮湿疫气弥漫的营地上空，
> 镰刀挥得无边无际。
> 「凡有祈求，她都伸出援手，
> 对饥者、病者、寒者；
> 摊开的手和心，同样乐于奉献
> 温言善行，还有金子。
> 「而——若问谁人得居其位、堪当其任——
> 堪当其任的女子，正是西科尔夫人。」

读者啊，本章既已写到末尾，我终于可以说出从开头起就一直急着要告诉你的话了。请回看第 8 章（Chapter VIII），看看这位堪当其任的女子，当年要把自己送到那个属于她的位置上，曾何等艰难地拼搏过。

## XIV / XIV

===Original===
My customers at the British Hotel.

===Chinese===
不列颠旅馆的顾客们。

===Original===
I shall proceed in this chapter to make the reader acquainted with some of the customers of the British Hotel, who came there for its creature comforts as well as its hostess’s medicines when need was; and if he or she should be inclined to doubt or should hesitate at accepting my experience of Crimean life as entirely credible, I beg that individual to refer to the accounts which were given in the newspapers of the spring of 1855, and I feel sure they will acquit me of any intention to exaggerate. If I were to speak of all the nameless horrors of that spring as plainly as I could, I should really disgust you; but those I shall bring before your notice have all something of the humorous in them﻿—and so it ever is. Time is a great restorer, and changes surely the greatest sorrow into a pleasing memory. The sun shines this springtime upon green grass that covers the graves of the poor fellows we left behind sadly a few short months ago: bright flowers grow up upon ruins of batteries and crumbling trenches, and cover the sod that presses on many a mouldering token of the old time of battle and death. I dare say that, if I went to the Crimea now, I should see a smiling landscape, instead of the bloodstained scene which I shall ever associate with distress and death; and as it is with nature so it is with human kind. Whenever I meet those who have survived that dreary spring of 1855, we seldom talk about its horrors; but remembering its transient gleams of sunshine, smile at the fun and good nature that varied its long and weary monotony. And now that I am anxious to remember all I can that will interest my readers, my memory prefers to dwell upon what was pleasing and amusing, although the time will never come when it will cease to retain most vividly the pathos and woe of those dreadful months.

I have said that the winter had not ended when we began operations at the British Hotel; and very often, after we considered we were fairly under spring’s influence, our old enemy would come back with an angry roar of wind and rain, levelling tents, unroofing huts, destroying roads, and handing over May to the command of General Fevrier. But the sun fought bravely for us, and in time always dispersed the leaden clouds and gilded the iron sky, and made us cheerful again. During the end of March, the whole of April, and a considerable portion of May, however, the army was but a little better off for the advent of spring. The military road to the camp was only in progress﻿—the railway only carried ammunition. A few hours’ rain rendered the old road all but impassable, and scarcity often existed in the front before Sebastopol, although the frightened and anxious Commissariat toiled hard to avert such a mishap; so that very often to the British Hotel came officers starved out on the heights above us. The dandies of Rotten Row would come down riding on sorry nags, ready to carry back﻿—their servants were on duty in the trenches﻿—anything that would be available for dinner. A single glance at their personal appearance would suffice to show the hardships of the life they were called upon to lead. Before I left London for the seat of war I had been more than once to the United Service Club, seeking to gain the interest of officers whom I had known in Jamaica; and I often thought afterwards of the difference between those I saw there trimly shaven, handsomely dressed, with spotless linen and dandy air, and these their companions, who in England would resemble them. Roughly, warmly dressed, with great fur caps, which met their beards and left nothing exposed but lips and nose, and not much of those; you would easily believe that soap and water were luxuries not readily obtainable, that shirts and socks were often comforts to dream about rather than possess, and that they were familiar with horrors you would shudder to hear named. Tell me, reader, can you fancy what the want of so simple a thing as a pocket-handkerchief is? To put a case﻿—have you ever gone out for the day without one; sat in a draught and caught a sneezing cold in the head? You say the question is an unnecessarily unpleasant one, and yet what I am about to tell you is true, and the sufferer is, I believe, still alive.

===Chinese===
在本章里，我要让读者认识几位不列颠旅馆的顾客——他们来这儿，既为店里的口腹之慰，必要时也为女店主的药。倘若哪位读者倾向于怀疑、或不愿全然相信我讲述的克里米亚经历，我恳请这人去查一查 1855 年春天报纸上的那些报道，我确信它们会替我洗清任何夸大其词的嫌疑。倘若我要把那个春天种种无以名状的惨状有多直白说多直白，那真要叫你恶心反胃；不过我要摆到你眼前的这些，都带着几分幽默的成分——世事向来如此。时间是一位伟大的疗愈师，必能把最大的悲哀化作一段宜人的回忆。今春的太阳，正照在青青绿草之上，而就在短短几个月前，我们还悲伤地把那些可怜人留在了草下的坟墓里：鲜艳的花朵从坍圮的炮台和崩裂的战壕废墟间生长出来，覆盖住一块块草皮，草皮下压着旧日战火与死亡岁月里渐渐朽坏的遗物。我敢说，如今我若重返克里米亚，看到的会是一片含笑的风景，而不再是那片血染的场景——它在我的记忆里，永远连着苦难与死亡。自然界如此，人亦如此。每当我遇见熬过 1855 年那个凄惨春天的人，我们很少谈起它的惨状；只是记起它偶然透出的几缕阳光，笑谈那些为漫长沉闷添趣的好玩事与好心肠。而今我一心想多记住些能让读者感兴趣的东西，我的记忆也就偏爱流连于那些可乐可笑之事，尽管那几个可怕月份里的哀恸与悲苦，永远鲜明地烙在我的脑海里，任什么年月也磨灭不掉。

我讲过，我们在不列颠旅馆开张营业时，冬天还没有结束；往往我们自认已经稳稳沐浴在春光里，老对头却又带着风雨的怒号杀回来，掀翻帐篷、掀掉屋顶、毁掉道路，把五月拱手交给费夫里耶将军（General Fevrier，法语「二月」）节制。好在太阳为我们奋勇作战，总能及时驱散铅灰色的云层，给铁灰的天空镀上金色，让我们重新振作。然而，直到 3 月底、整个 4 月，还有 5 月的好大一部分，大军的境况并未因春天到来好多少。通往营地的军路还在修筑之中——铁路只运送弹药。几小时的雨就能让老路几乎无法通行，塞瓦斯托波尔城前的前线时常面临匮乏，尽管惶惶不安的军需部拼命苦干，力求避免这种灾祸；于是常有从我们上方高地上饿下来的军官，投奔不列颠旅馆。伦敦朗朗骑马道（Rotten Row）上的花花公子们，骑着瘦马下来，准备驮回去——他们的仆人都在战壕里当值——任何能凑成一餐晚饭的东西。只消看一眼他们的仪表，就足以说明这种生活的艰苦。离开伦敦赴战场之前，我不止一次造访联合军种俱乐部（United Service Club），想争取我在牙买加认识的军官们的关照；此后我常想起两相对照的差别：那边看到的他们，修面整洁、衣冠楚楚、内衣雪白、一副纨绔气派；而这边他们的同袍——在英国时本与他们一般模样——却粗服裹身、臃肿御寒，戴着一顶大毛皮帽，帽沿直抵胡须，除了嘴唇和鼻尖别无所露，连那两样也所剩无几。你会毫不费力地相信：肥皂和清水是轻易到不了手的奢侈品；衬衫和袜子常常是梦中才有、无从到手的慰藉；他们见识过的种种惨状，你光是听人说出名字都会发抖。告诉我，读者，你能想象缺一件手帕这样简单的东西是什么滋味吗？打个比方——你可曾有一天出门忘了带它，坐在过堂风里，伤风鼻塞喷嚏连连？你要说这问题未免叫人不痛快；可我接下来要讲的却是真事，而且那位受苦的当事人，我相信，如今还健在。

===Original===
An officer had ridden down one day to obtain refreshments (this was very early in the spring); some nice fowls had just been taken from the spit, and I offered one to him. Paper was one of the most hardly obtainable luxuries of the Crimea, and I rarely had any to waste upon my customers; so I called out, “Give me your pocket-handkerchief, my son, that I may wrap it up.” You see we could not be very particular out there; but he smiled very bitterly as he answered, “Pocket-handkerchief, mother﻿—by Jove! I wish I had one. I tore my last shirt into shreds a fortnight ago, and there’s not a bit of it left now.”

Shortly after, a hundred dozen of these useful articles came to my store, and I sold them all to officers and men very speedily.

For some time, and until I found the task beyond my strength, I kept up a capital table at the British Hotel; but at last I gave up doing so professedly, and my hungry customers had to make shift with whatever was on the premises. Fortunately they were not over-dainty, and had few antipathies. My duties increased so rapidly, that sometimes it was with difficulty that I found time to eat and sleep. Could I have obtained good servants, my daily labours would have been lightened greatly; but my staff never consisted of more than a few boys, two black cooks, some Turks﻿—one of whom, Osman, had enough to do to kill and pluck the poultry, while the others looked after the stock and killed our goats and sheep﻿—and as many runaway sailors or good-for-noughts in search of employment as we could from time to time lay our hands upon; but they never found my larder entirely empty. I often used to roast a score or so of fowls daily, besides boiling hams and tongues. Either these or a slice from a joint of beef or mutton you would be pretty sure of finding at your service in the larder of the British Hotel.

===Chinese===
有一天，一位军官骑马下山来寻些吃食（那时还是初春）；几只肥美的鸡刚从烤叉上取下，我便奉上一只。纸张是克里米亚最难到手的奢侈品之一，我很少有舍得耗在顾客身上的；于是我喊道：「把手帕给我，孩子，我好把它包上。」你瞧，在那边我们没法太讲究；可他苦笑着一口回绝：「手帕，妈妈——天哪！我要有一块就好了。两个星期前，我把最后一件衬衫撕成了碎条，如今连一片布屑也不剩了。」

没过多久，一百打这种有用的物件运到了我的铺子，我很快把它们卖光给了军官和士兵们。

有一段时期，直到发觉力不从心为止，我在不列颠旅馆一直备着一桌上好的伙食；后来我索性明言不再供应，饥肠辘辘的顾客们只好将就店里的现成东西。所幸他们并不挑剔，忌口也少。我的事务增涨得飞快，有时连吃饭睡觉都挤不出工夫。倘若雇得到好帮佣，我日常的操劳本可大大减轻；可我的人手从来只有寥寥几个男孩、两个黑人厨子、几个土耳其人——其中一个叫奥斯曼（Osman）的，光宰鸡拔毛就够他忙的，其余的照看牲口、宰我们的山羊和绵羊——再加上一些我们时时能抓到手的开小差水手或找活计的闲汉；尽管如此，他们从没见我的食品柜空过。我常常一天要烤上二十来只鸡，外加煮火腿和牛舌。在战地旅馆的食品柜里，你几乎笃定能找到这几样，或是一块牛肉羊肉的剔骨肉，随时听候取用。

===Original===
Would you like, gentle reader, to know what other things suggestive of home and its comforts your relatives and friends in the Crimea could obtain from the hostess of Spring Hill? I do not tell you that the following articles were all obtainable at the commencement, but many were. The time was indeed when, had you asked me for mock turtle and venison, you should have had them, preserved in tins, but that was when the Crimea was flooded with plenty﻿—too late, alas! to save many whom want had killed; but had you been doing your best to batter Sebastopol about the ears of the Russians in the spring and summer of the year before last, the firm of Seacole and Day would have been happy to have served you with (I omit ordinary things) linen and hosiery, saddlery, caps, boots and shoes, for the outer man; and for the inner man, meat and soups of every variety in tins (you can scarcely conceive how disgusted we all became at last with preserved provisions); salmon, lobsters, and oysters, also in tins, which last beaten up into fritters, with onions, butter, eggs, pepper, and salt, were very good; game, wild fowl, vegetables, also preserved, eggs, sardines, curry powder, cigars, tobacco, snuff, cigarette papers, tea, coffee, tooth powder, and currant jelly. When cargoes came in from Constantinople, we bought great supplies of potatoes, carrots, turnips, and greens. Ah! what a rush there used to be for the greens. You might sometimes get hot rolls; but, generally speaking, I bought the Turkish bread (*ekmek*), baked at Balaclava.

===Chinese===
温文尔雅的读者，你想不想知道，你在克里米亚的亲朋好友，还能从斯普林希尔的女主人手里弄到些什么勾起家园温情的物件？我并不说下列货品一开张就样样齐备，但其中许多确有。确曾有那样的时候：你若向我要假甲鱼汤和鹿肉，我也能拿出罐装的来；但那是克里米亚物资充盈泛滥之时——唉，太迟了，救不回许多早已葬身匮乏的人了。可倘若前年春夏，你正在千方百计炮轰塞瓦斯托波尔、轰得俄国人双耳发聩，「西科尔与戴」商号本乐于为你效劳——寻常货色姑且略去——装点外身的有：亚麻内衣与袜类、马具、帽子、靴子鞋子；填塞内里的有：各样罐装肉食和汤（你简直想象不到，我们最后对罐头食品厌恶到了什么地步）；鲑鱼、龙虾和牡蛎，同样是罐装的——牡蛎加洋葱、黄油、鸡蛋、胡椒和盐，打匀了煎成炸饼，滋味极好；野味、野禽、蔬菜，也是保藏的，还有鸡蛋、沙丁鱼、咖喱粉、雪茄、烟丝、鼻烟、卷烟纸、茶叶、咖啡、牙粉和醋栗果冻。每有船货从君士坦丁堡运抵，我们就大量购进土豆、胡萝卜、芜菁和青菜。啊！抢购青菜的场面可真壮观。热面包卷有时也能买到；但一般说来，我买的是巴拉克拉瓦烤的土耳其面包（*ekmek*）。

===Original===
Or had you felt too ill to partake of your rough camp fare, coarsely cooked by a soldier cook, who, unlike the French, could turn his hand to few things but fighting, and had ridden down that muddy road to the Col, to see what Mother Seacole could give you for dinner, the chances were you would have found a good joint of mutton, not of the fattest, forsooth; for in such miserable condition were the poor beasts landed, that once, when there came an urgent order from headquarters for twenty-five pounds of mutton, we had to cut up one sheep and a half to provide the quantity; or you would have stumbled upon something curried, or upon a good Irish stew, nice and hot, with plenty of onions and potatoes, or upon some capital meat-pies. I found the preserved meats were better relished cooked in this fashion, and well doctored with stimulants. Before long I grew as familiar with the mysteries of seasoning as any London pieman, and could accommodate myself to the requirements of the seasons as readily. Or had there been nothing better, you might have gone further and fared on worse fare than one of my Welch rabbits, for the manufacture of which I became so famous. And had you been fortunate enough to have visited the British Hotel upon rice-pudding day, I warrant you would have ridden back to your hut with kind thoughts of Mother Seacole’s endeavours to give you a taste of home. If I had nothing else to be proud of, I think my rice puddings, made without milk, upon the high road to Sebastopol, would have gained me a reputation. What a shout there used to be when I came out of my little caboose, hot and flurried, and called out, “Rice-pudding day, my sons.” Some of them were baked in large shallow pans, for the men and the sick, who always said that it reminded them of home. You would scarcely expect to finish up your dinner with pastry, but very often you would have found a good stock of it in my larder. Whenever I had a few leisure moments, I used to wash my hands, roll up my sleeves and roll out pastry. Very often I was interrupted to dispense medicines; but if the tarts had a flavour of senna, or the puddings tasted of rhubarb, it never interfered with their consumption. I declare I never heard or read of an army so partial to pastry as that British army before Sebastopol; while I had a reputation for my sponge-cakes that any pastrycook in London, even Gunter, might have been proud of. The officers, full of fun and high spirits, used to crowd into the little kitchen, and, despite all my remonstrances, which were not always confined to words, for they made me frantic sometimes, and an iron spoon is a tempting weapon, would carry off the tarts hot from the oven, while the good-for-nothing black cooks, instead of lending me their aid, would stand by and laugh with all their teeth. And when the hot season commenced, the crowds that came to the British Hotel for my claret and cider cups, and other cooling summer drinks, were very complimentary in their expressions of appreciation of my skill.

===Chinese===
又或者，你病得咽不下营里粗粝的伙食——那是一个士兵厨子胡乱烹的，他不比法国人，除了打仗几乎不会别的——你沿着那条泥泞的路骑到山口，看看西科尔妈妈能给你什么当晚饭；多半你会发现一块上好的羊腿肉——不过说句实话，肥是不太肥的；那些可怜的牲口卸岸时境况凄惨，有一回司令部来了急令，要二十五磅羊肉，我们只好宰了一只半羊才凑够斤两；再不然，你会撞上什么咖喱菜，或是一锅热腾腾的爱尔兰炖菜，洋葱土豆下得足足的，或是几个顶好的肉馅饼。我发现罐头肉这样加工烹调、再用刺激性调料好好「诊治」一番，更合人的胃口。没多久，我对调味的种种诀窍就熟练得不下于伦敦任何一位馅饼贩子，对时令需求的顺应也一样娴熟。倘若实在没有更好的，你哪怕走得更远，也难吃得比我的一份威尔士干酪吐司（Welch rabbit）更差——制做这东西我可是出了名的。倘若你有幸在不列颠旅馆的「大米布丁日」光顾，我担保你骑马回棚屋的路上，心里定会惦着西科尔妈妈费的那些心思，她多想让你尝一口家的味道。即便我再没有别的可夸，我想，光凭在通往塞瓦斯托波尔的大路上、不用牛奶做出来的大米布丁，也够我扬名了。每当我从那间小厨舱里钻出来，热得满脸通红、手忙脚乱，喊一声「今天是大米布丁日，孩子们」，那一片欢呼声啊！有些布丁用大浅盘烤，留给士兵和病号们，他们总说这让他们想起家。你怕是想不到，晚饭还能以油酥点心收尾，可在我的食品柜里，你常常会发现存货不少。一有片刻闲暇，我就洗净双手、挽起袖子擀起酥皮来。常常擀着擀着就被叫去发药；可即便馅饼带上了番泻叶味，布丁尝出大黄味，也从不妨碍它们被一扫而空。我敢说，从来不曾听说哪支军队像塞瓦斯托波尔城前这支英军这样嗜好油酥点心；而我的海绵蛋糕也是出了名的，伦敦任何一位糕点师傅——哪怕是贡特（Gunter）——都足以引以为傲。军官们兴致高涨、满屋子打趣，挤进那间小厨房，全然不顾我的一再抗议——抗议并不总是停留在口头上，他们有时真把我逼得发疯，而铁勺子又是件诱人的武器——把刚出炉的馅饼热腾腾地端走；而那些不中用的黑人厨子非但不帮我的忙，只在一旁龇着一口白牙大笑。到了炎热的季节，成群结队到不列颠旅馆来买我调配的红酒杯饮（claret cup）与苹果酒杯饮（cider cup）和各色消夏冷饮的客人，对我的手艺更是赞不绝口。

===Original===
Now, supposing that you had made a hearty dinner and were thinking of starting homeward﻿—if I can use so pleasant a term in reference to your cheerless quarters﻿—it was very natural that you should be anxious to carry back something to your hut. Perhaps you expected to be sent into the trenches (many a supper cooked by me has been consumed in those fearful trenches by brave men, who could eat it with keen appetites while the messengers of death were speeding around them); or perhaps you had planned a little dinner-party, and wanted to give your friends something better than their ordinary fare. Anyhow, you would in all probability have some good reason for returning laden with comforts and necessaries from Spring Hill. You would not be very particular about carrying them. You might have been a great swell at home, where you would have shuddered if Bond Street had seen you carrying a parcel no larger than your card-case; but those considerations rarely troubled you here. Very likely, your servant was lying crouched in a rifle pit, having “pots” at the Russians, or keeping watch and ward in the long lines of trenches, or, stripped to his shirt, shovelling powder and shot into the great guns, whose steady roar broke the evening’s calm. So if you did not wait upon yourself, you would stand a very fair chance of being starved. But you would open your knapsack, if you had brought one, for me to fill it with potatoes, and halloo out, “Never mind, mother!” although the gravy from the fowls on your saddle before you was soaking through the little modicum of paper which was all I could afford you. So laden, you would cheerfully start up the hill of mud hutward; and well for you if you did not come to grief on that treacherous sea of mud that lay swelling between the Col and your destination. Many a mishap, ludicrous but for their consequences, happened on it. I remember a young officer coming down one day just in time to carry off my last fowl and meat pie. Before he had gone far, the horse so floundered in the mud that the saddle-girths broke, and while the pies rolled into the clayey soil in one direction, the fowl flew in another. To make matters worse, the horse, in his efforts to extricate himself, did for them entirely; and in terrible distress, the poor fellow came back for me to set him up again. I shook my head for a long time, but at last, after he had over and over again urged upon me pathetically that he had two fellows coming to dine with him at six, and nothing in the world in his hut but salt pork, I resigned a plump fowl which I had kept back for my own dinner. Off he started again, but soon came back with, “Oh, mother, I forgot all about the potatoes; they’ve all rolled out upon that —﻿—﻿— road; you must fill my bag again.” We all laughed heartily at him, but this state of things *had* been rather tragical.

===Chinese===
现在，假设你已饱餐一顿，正想着动身回「家」——倘若这字眼能用在那么凄清的住处上的话——你自然急着给棚屋捎点什么回去。也许你预计要进战壕（多少顿我做的晚饭，就是在那些可怕的战壕里，被勇敢的汉子们吃掉的；死亡的信使在他们身边飞掠，他们照样吃得津津有味）；也许你筹划了一场小小的晚宴，想让朋友们尝尝比日常伙食强些的东西。总而言之，你十有八九有个正当的理由，满载着斯普林希尔的慰藉与必需品而归。带上这些东西，你可不会讲究什么仪态。在国内你也许是个响当当的大人物，邦德街若看见你提着一个不比名片盒大多少的包裹，你会羞得无地自容；可在这边，这类顾虑很少来搅扰你。多半你的仆人正蜷在步枪坑里朝俄国人「放罐子」，或在长长的战壕列线上守望巡哨，或是脱得只剩一件衬衫，往那些大炮里铲火药灌炮弹——大炮持续的轰鸣，撕破傍晚的宁静。所以，你若不自己伺候自己，就大有活活饿肚子的风险。可你会解开背囊——倘若你带了的话——让我装满土豆，一面喊：「不要紧，妈妈！」尽管鞍前挂的鸡渗出的卤汁，正浸透我仅能给你的那一小片纸。就这样满载着，你会高高兴兴地踏上泥泞的山坡，往棚屋去；要是你没在那片横亘在山口与你目的地之间、涨溢起伏的泥海里栽跟头，就算你走运。多少滑稽——若非后果堪忧——的事故在那上头发生。我记得有一天，一位年轻军官下山来，正赶上把我最后一只鸡和肉馅饼拎走。他没走出多远，马在泥里挣扎得肚带都断了，馅饼朝一个方向滚进黏土，鸡朝另一个方向飞出去。更糟的是，那马奋力脱身之际，把它们彻底报销了；那可怜人懊丧万分地折回来，求我给他重新置办。我摇头摇了半天，最后，等他一遍又一遍凄凄切切地恳求，说他约了两个同伴六点来吃饭，棚屋里除了咸猪肉之外一无所有，我才让出一只我留给自家晚饭的肥鸡。他又出发了，可很快又折回来：「哎呀妈妈，我把土豆忘得一干二净；全滚到那条——的路上去了；您得再给我把袋子装满。」我们冲他大笑了一场，可这种局面，原本是相当悲惨的。

===Original===
Before I bring this chapter to a close, I should like, with the reader’s permission, to describe one day of my life in the Crimea. They were all pretty much alike, except when there was fighting upon a large scale going on, and duty called me to the field. I was generally up and busy by daybreak, sometimes earlier, for in the summer my bed had no attractions strong enough to bind me to it after four. There was plenty to do before the work of the day began. There was the poultry to pluck and prepare for cooking, which had been killed on the previous night; the joints to be cut up and got ready for the same purpose; the medicines to be mixed; the store to be swept and cleaned. Of very great importance, with all these things to see after, were the few hours of quiet before the road became alive with travellers. By seven o’clock the morning coffee would be ready, hot and refreshing, and eagerly sought for by the officers of the Army Works Corps engaged upon making the great highroad to the front, and the Commissariat and Land Transport men carrying stores from Balaclava to the heights.
