Onward they seemed to go into the dust and smoke, swallowed up by hundreds; but they never returned, and before long we saw workmen levelling parapets and filling up ditches, over which they drove, with headlong speed and impetuosity, artillery and ammunition-wagons, until there could be no doubt that the Malakhoff was taken, although the tide of battle still surged around it with violence, and wounded men were borne from it in large numbers. And before this, our men had made their attack, and the fearful assault of the Redan was going on, and failing. But I was soon too busy to see much, for the wounded were borne in even in greater numbers than at the last assault; whilst stragglers, slightly hurt, limped in, in fast-increasing numbers, and engrossed our attention. I now and then found time to ask them rapid questions; but they did not appear to know anything more than that everything had gone wrong. The sailors, as before, showed their gallantry, and even recklessness, conspicuously. The wounded of the ladder and sandbag parties came up even with a laugh, and joked about their hurts in the happiest conceivable manner.

I saw many officers of the 97th wounded; and, as far as possible, I reserved my attentions for my old regiment, known so well in my native island. My poor 97th! their loss was terrible. I dressed the wound of one of its officers, seriously hit in the mouth; I attended to another wounded in the throat, and bandaged the hand of a third, terribly crushed by a rifle-bullet. In the midst of this we were often interrupted by those unwelcome and impartial Russian visitors﻿—the shells. One fell so near that I thought my last hour was come; and, although I had sufficient firmness to throw myself upon the ground, I was so seriously frightened that I never thought of rising from my recumbent position until the hearty laugh of those around convinced me that the danger had passed by. Afterwards I picked up a piece of this huge shell, and brought it home with me.

===Chinese===
到了正午，炮声戛然而止；我们怀着一种奇异的激动，看见法国人从前方战壕里翻涌而出，人潮似的滚进了马拉霍夫（Malakhoff）。他们仿佛一路钻进尘埃与硝烟，成百成百地被吞没；却再没有退出来，不多时，我们便看见工兵削平胸墙、填平壕沟，炮车弹药车一往无前、风驰电掣地从上面碾过，直到再无可疑——马拉霍夫拿下了，尽管战潮仍在它四周汹涌激荡，伤员被大批大批地从那里抬下来。而在此之前，我们的士兵也已发起攻击，对棱堡那场可怕的强攻正打得如火如荼，且正节节失利。可我很快便忙得无暇旁顾，因为抬下来的伤员比上次强攻时还多；轻伤掉队的，也一瘸一拐、越聚越多地蹭进来，缠住了我们全部的心神。我偶尔挤得出工夫，飞快地问他们几句；可他们也说不出个所以然，只知道一切都砸了。水兵们一如既往，把他们的英勇、甚至亡命显露得淋漓尽致。云梯组和沙袋组的伤员甚至是笑着下来的，用想得出的最快活的口吻拿自己的伤打趣。

我看见第 97 团（97th）的许多军官挂了彩；我尽可能把心思留给这个老团——它在我出生的岛上是那样有名。我可怜的第 97 团！他们的损失惨重极了。我给该团一名嘴上重伤的军官敷了伤；又照看了一位喉部负伤的；还给第三位包扎了手——那条手叫步枪子弹打得稀烂。正忙着，我们不时要被那些不受欢迎却一视同仁的俄国来客——榴弹——打断。有一发落得那样近，我以为自己的时辰到了；虽说我还算沉得住气，扑倒在了地上，却也着实吓得不轻，直挺挺趴着，压根没想到要起来，直到四周的人一阵开怀大笑，才叫我确信危险已经过去了。事后我拾了这块大榴弹的一片碎片，带回了家。

===Original===
It was on this, as on every similar occasion, that I saw the *Times* correspondent eagerly taking down notes and sketches of the scene, under fire﻿—listening apparently with attention to all the busy little crowd that surrounded him, but without laying down his pencil; and yet finding time, even in his busiest moment, to lend a helping hand to the wounded. It may have been on this occasion that his keen eye noticed me, and his mind, albeit engrossed with far more important memories, found room to remember me. I may well be proud of his testimony, borne so generously only the other day, and may well be excused for transcribing it from the columns of the *Times*:﻿—“I have seen her go down, under fire, with her little store of creature comforts for our wounded men; and a more tender or skilful hand about a wound or broken limb could not be found among our best surgeons. I saw her at the assault on the Redan, at the Tchernaya, at the fall of Sebastopol, laden, not with plunder, good old soul! but with wine, bandages, and food for the wounded or the prisoners.”

===Chinese===
正是在这一天，也同每一回这样的场合一样，我看见《泰晤士报》记者顶着炮火，奋笔记录着现场的笔记和速写——表面上一心听着围在他身边那群忙碌人的七嘴八舌，手里的铅笔却从不停下；而在他最忙的时刻，居然还匀得出手来拉伤员一把。或许正是在这个场合，他那双锐利的眼睛注意到了我，他那颗装满远为重大的记忆的头脑，也还腾得出一个角落记得我。他前些天在《泰晤士报》版面上慷慨作出的证词，尽够我引以为豪，也尽够我原谅自己把它从报上抄录下来：——「我见过她冒着炮火下去，为我们受伤的士兵送上她那点微薄的口腹之慰；论照料伤口、接续断肢，在我们最好的外科医生中间，也找不出比她更温柔或更灵巧的手。强攻棱堡时、切尔纳亚河畔、塞瓦斯托波尔陷落之际，我都见过她——满载的，好心的老妈妈！不是赃物，而是酒、绷带，和给伤员与俘虏的吃食。」

===Original===
I remained on Cathcart’s Hill far into the night, and watched the city blazing beneath us, awestruck at the terrible sight, until the bitter wind found its way through my thin clothing, and chilled me to the bone; and not till then did I leave for Spring Hill. I had little sleep that night. The night was made a ruddy lurid day with the glare of the blazing town; while every now and then came reports which shook the earth to its centre. And yet I believe very many of the soldiers, wearied with their day’s labour, slept soundly throughout that terrible night, and awoke to find their work completed: for in the night, covered by the burning city, Sebastopol was left, a heap of ruins, to its victors; and before noon on the following day, none but dead and dying Russians were in the south side of the once famous and beautiful mistress-city of the Euxine.

The good news soon spread through the camp. It gave great pleasure; but I almost think the soldiers would have been better pleased had the Russians delayed their parting twelve hours longer, and given the Highlanders and their comrades a chance of retrieving the disasters of the previous day. Nothing else could wipe away the soreness of defeat, or compensate for the better fortune which had befallen our allies the French.

===Chinese===
我在卡斯卡特山上一直待到深夜，望着脚下大火中的城，被那可怕的景象慑住了，直到凛冽的寒风穿透我单薄的衣裳，把我冻到骨髓里，我才动身回斯普林希尔。那一夜我几乎没有合眼。燃烧的城市把黑夜照成了红彤彤的惨白昼；爆炸声不时传来，把大地震到根子。然而我相信，许多劳累了一整天的士兵，在那可怕的一夜里睡得又香又沉，一觉醒来，发现自己的活计已经大功告成：因为就在夜里，趁着满城大火的掩护，塞瓦斯托波尔——黑海（Euxine）昔日名冠一方、美丽绝伦的女主人之城——只剩下一堆废墟，留给了它的胜利者；到次日正午之前，城南一侧除了死去的和垂死的俄国人，再没有别人。

好消息很快传遍了营地。人人欢喜；可我几乎觉得，俄国人若把撤离再拖延十二个钟头，给高地兵团（Highlanders）和他们的同袍一个挽回前一日惨败的机会，士兵们还会更欢喜。别的什么都抹不平败绩的痛楚，也补偿不了我们的盟友法国人摊上的那份好运。

===Original===
The news of the evacuation of Sebastopol soon carried away all traces of yesterday’s fatigue. For weeks past I had been offering bets to everyone that I would not only be the first woman to enter Sebastopol from the English lines, but that I would be the first to carry refreshments into the fallen city. And now the time I had longed for had come. I borrowed some mules from the Land Transport Corps﻿—mine were knocked up by yesterday’s work﻿—and loading them with good things, started off with my partner and some other friends early on that memorable Sunday morning for Cathcart’s Hill.

When I found that strict orders had been given to admit no one inside Sebastopol, I became quite excited; and making my way to General Garrett’s quarters, I made such an earnest representation of what I considered my right that I soon obtained a pass, of which the following is a copy:﻿—

> “Pass Mrs. Seacole and her attendants, with refreshments for officers and soldiers in the Redan and in Sebastopol.
> “Garrett, M.G.
> “Cathcart’s Hill, Sept. 9, 1855.”

===Chinese===
塞瓦斯托波尔撤离的消息一传来，昨日的疲乏顿时无影无踪。几个星期以来，我一直逢人便打赌：我不但要做从英军战线第一个进塞瓦斯托波尔的女人，还要做第一个把吃食送进这座陷落之城的人。如今，我渴盼已久的时刻到了。我从陆运军团借来几头骡子——我自己的那几头昨天累垮了——装满好东西，在那个值得纪念的星期日清晨，同我的合伙人和另几位朋友早早出发，往卡斯卡特山去。

一听说严令禁止任何人进入塞瓦斯托波尔，我顿时激动起来；径直闯到加勒特将军（General Garrett）的住处，把我认定的权利如此恳切地陈说了一番，很快便弄到了一张通行证，抄录如下：——

> 「准许西科尔夫人及其随行人员通行，携慰劳食品给予棱堡及塞瓦斯托波尔城内之官兵。
> 「加勒特（Garrett），M.G.
> 「卡斯卡特山，1855 年 9 月 9 日。」

===Original===
So many attached themselves to my staff, becoming for the nonce my attendants, that I had some difficulty at starting; but at last I passed all the sentries safely, much to the annoyance of many officers, who were trying every conceivable scheme to evade them, and entered the city. I can give you no very clear description of its condition on that Sunday morning, a year and a half ago. Many parts of it were still blazing furiously﻿—explosions were taking place in all directions﻿—every step had a score of dangers; and yet curiosity and excitement carried us on and on. I was often stopped to give refreshments to officers and men, who had been fasting for hours. Some, on the other hand, had found their way to Russian cellars; and one body of men were most ingloriously drunk, and playing the wildest pranks. They were dancing, yelling, and singing﻿—some of them with Russian women’s dresses fastened round their waists, and old bonnets stuck upon their heads.

===Chinese===
自告奋勇来充当随员的实在太多，一时倒叫我难于启程；但最终我把一处处哨卡全过了，这叫许多军官大为光火——他们正千方百计想混过去而不得——我进了城。那是一年半以前的一个星期日早晨，城的境况如何，我给不出很清晰的描述。许多城区还在熊熊燃烧——爆炸四面八方地响——每一步都暗藏着二十种危险；可好奇心和兴奋还是拽着我们往前再往前。我时时被拦下来，给饿了多少个钟头的官兵递上吃食。另一些人则摸进了俄国人的酒窖；有一伙人醉得极不体面，撒着最野的疯。他们又跳又喊又唱——有几个腰里系着俄国女人的裙子，头上扣着旧软帽。

===Original===
I was offered many trophies. All plunder was stopped by the sentries, and confiscated, so that the soldiers could afford to be liberal. By one I was offered a great velvet sofa; another pressed a huge armchair, which had graced some Sebastopol study, upon me; while a third begged my acceptance of a portion of a grand piano. What I did carry away was very unimportant: a gaily-decorated altar-candle, studded with gold and silver stars, which the present Commander-in-Chief condescended to accept as a Sebastopol memorial; an old cracked China teapot, which in happier times had very likely dispensed pleasure to many a small tea-party; a cracked bell, which had rung many to prayers during the siege, and which I bore away on my saddle; and a parasol, given me by a drunken soldier. He had a silk skirt on, and torn lace upon his wrists, and he came mincingly up, holding the parasol above his head, and imitating the walk of an affected lady, to the vociferous delight of his comrades. And all this, and much more, in that fearful charnel city, with death and suffering on every side.

===Chinese===
许多人向我进献战利品。一切劫掠都被哨兵查禁、没收，士兵们因此倒出手面阔绰起来。有人向我献上一张大天鹅绒沙发；另一位硬把一把高大的扶手椅塞给我——它想必曾在塞瓦斯托波尔哪家书房里风光过；第三位则恳请我笑纳一部大钢琴的一部分。我真正带走的都不起眼：一支装点华丽的祭坛蜡烛，缀满金银的星饰，现任总司令屈尊收下它作了塞瓦斯托波尔的纪念；一把有裂纹的旧瓷茶壶，在太平年月里，它多半曾给多少场小小茶会添过乐子；一口裂了的钟，围城期间它曾声声唤人祈祷，我把它驮在鞍上带走了；还有一把阳伞，是一个醉兵送的。他身上系着一条绸裙，腕上挂着破花边，捏着腔扭着步子走上前来，阳伞举过头顶，学着一副装腔作势的太太的走姿，惹得他的同伴们哄堂大笑。而这一切，还有比这多得多的，都发生在那座可怕的尸骸之城，死亡与苦楚遍布四面八方。

===Original===
It was very hazardous to pass along some of the streets exposed to the fire of the Russians on the north side of the harbour. We had to wait and watch our opportunity, and then gallop for it. Some of us had close shaves of being hit. More than this, fires still kept breaking out around; while mines and fougasses not unfrequently exploded from unknown causes. We saw two officers emerge from a heap of ruins, covered and almost blinded with smoke and dust, from some such unlooked-for explosion. With considerable difficulty we succeeded in getting into the quarter of the town held by the French, where I was nearly getting into serious trouble.

I had loitered somewhat behind my party, watching, with pardonable curiosity, the adroitness with which a party of French were plundering a house; and by the time my curiosity had been satisfied, I found myself quite alone, my retinue having preceded me by some few hundred yards. This would have been of little consequence, had not an American sailor lad, actuated either by mischief or folly, whispered to the Frenchmen that I was a Russian spy; and had they not, instead of laughing at him, credited his assertion, and proceeded to arrest me. Now, such a charge was enough to make a lion of a lamb; so I refused positively to dismount, and made matters worse by knocking in the cap of the first soldier who laid hands upon me, with the bell that hung at my saddle. Upon this, six or seven tried to force me to the guardhouse in rather a rough manner, while I resisted with all my force, screaming out for Mr. Day, and using the bell for a weapon. How I longed for a better one I need not tell the reader. In the midst of this scene came up a French officer, whom I recognised as the patient I had taken to Spring Hill after the battle of the Tchernaya, and who took my part at once, and ordered them to release me. Although I rather weakened my cause, it was most natural that, directly I was released, I should fly at the varlet who had caused me this trouble; and I did so, using my bell most effectually, and aided, when my party returned, by their riding-whips.

===Chinese===
有几条街暴露在港口北岸俄国人的火力之下，走过去十分凶险。我们只得等待、瞅准时机，然后策马狂奔。我们有几个人险些挨着。不止如此，四周火灾仍不断爆出；地雷和炸发地雷（fougasse）也不时莫名其妙地爆炸。我们看见两名军官从一堆废墟里钻出来，烟尘糊了满头满脸，几乎睁不开眼，正是叫这样一场不虞的爆炸崩出来的。我们费了老大劲，才进到城里法军驻守的那一区，在那儿，我差点惹上一场大麻烦。

我落在队伍后面磨蹭了一会儿，带着情有可原的好奇，观赏一伙法国人洗劫一所房子手法之娴熟；等我的好奇心餍足，才发现自己形单影只，我的扈从们已先行了几百码。这本算不得什么，偏偏一个美国水手小伙子，或出于淘气、或出于愚蠢，凑到法国人耳边咬耳朵，说我是俄国间谍；而他们竟不笑话他，反倒信了他的话，动手要逮捕我。好嘛，这样一桩罪名，足以把羔羊逼成狮子；于是我断然拒绝下马，还把头一个上手的士兵的军帽敲瘪了——用的就是挂在我鞍上的那口钟——把事情越弄越僵。这一下，六七个人上来，连推带搡要把我押去卫兵室，我则使出全身力气抵抗，尖声喊戴先生，把那口钟当兵器抡。我多么巴望有件像样些的兵器，这就不必告诉读者了。正闹得不可开交，过来一位法国军官，我认出他正是切尔纳亚河之战后我带回斯普林希尔收治的病人；他立刻站在我这边，喝令他们放人。虽说这样一来多少折了我的理，可一获释放便直扑那个给我惹祸的小无赖，实在合乎天性；我也真这么干了，把我的钟使得淋漓尽致，待我那队人马折返回来，又添上了他们的马鞭。

===Original===
This little adventure took up altogether so much time that, when the French soldiers had made their apologies to me, and I had returned the compliment to the one whose head had been dented by my bell, it was growing late, and we made our way back to Cathcart’s Hill. On the way, a little French soldier begged hard of me to buy a picture, which had been cut from above the altar of some church in Sebastopol. It was too dark to see much of his prize, but I ultimately became its possessor, and brought it home with me. It is some eight or ten feet in length, and represents, I should think, the Madonna. I am no judge of such things, but I think, although the painting is rather coarse, that the face of the Virgin, and the heads of Cherubim that fill the cloud from which she is descending, are soft and beautiful. There is a look of divine calmness and heavenly love in the Madonna’s face which is very striking; and, perhaps, during the long and awful siege many a knee was bent in worship before it, and many a heart found comfort in its soft loving gaze.

On the following day I again entered Sebastopol, and saw still more of its horrors. But I have refrained from describing so many scenes of woe, that I am loth to dwell much on these. The very recollection of that woeful hospital, where thousands of dead and dying had been left by the retreating Russians, is enough to unnerve the strongest and sicken the most experienced. I would give much if I had never seen that harrowing sight. I believe some Englishmen were found in it alive; but it was as well that they did not live to tell their fearful experience.

===Chinese===
这场小小的历险前后占去了不少工夫，等法国兵们向我赔了不是、我也向那位被我的钟敲出个包的回敬了礼数，天色已晚，我们便取道回卡斯卡特山。路上，一个小个子法国兵死缠着要我买一幅画，是从塞瓦斯托波尔某座教堂祭坛上方割下来的。天太黑，看不清他的宝货，但我最终还是成了它的主人，把它带回了家。它约有八到十英尺长，画的我想是圣母（Madonna）。我对这类东西外行，但我想，画笔虽说粗了些，圣母的脸和那些小天使的头——他们布满她降临所自的那片云——却温柔而美丽。圣母脸上那一种神圣的安详、天国的慈爱，十分动人；或许，在那漫长可怕的围城岁月里，多少双膝曾在它面前跪下敬拜，多少颗心曾在它温柔怜爱的注视下寻得安慰。

第二天我又进了塞瓦斯托波尔，见到了它更多的惨状。但我一路已克制着少写悲苦场面，这些我也不愿多作流连。单是回想那座凄惨的医院——撤退的俄国人撇下了成千的死者与垂死者——就足以叫最强的人瘫软、最老练的人作呕。倘若从未见过那副剜心的景象，我情愿付出许多。我相信里面曾发现几个还活着的英国人；但他们没能活下来讲述那段可怕的经历，倒也算是好事。

===Original===
I made my way into the Redan also, although every step was dangerous, and took from it some brown bread, which seemed to have been left in the oven by the baker when he fled.

Before many days were passed, some Frenchwomen opened houses in Sebastopol; but in that quarter of the town held by the English the prospect was not sufficiently tempting for me to follow their example, and so I saw out the remainder of the campaign from my old quarters at Spring Hill.

===Chinese===
我也摸进了棱堡，尽管步步凶险，还从里面拿了些黑面包——像是面包师傅逃走时留在炉子里的。

没过几天，一些法国女人在塞瓦斯托波尔开起了铺子；可英军驻守的那片城区，光景不够诱人，我不肯效法她们，于是余下的征战时日，我便在斯普林希尔的老窝里看了个全程。

## XVIII / XVIII

===Original===
Holiday in the camp﻿—A new enemy, time﻿—Amusements in the Crimea﻿—My share in them﻿—Dinner at Spring Hill﻿—At the races﻿—Christmas Day in the British Hotel﻿—New Year’s Day in the hospital.

===Chinese===
营中假日——新的敌人：时间——克里米亚的消遣——我在其中的份——斯普林希尔的晚宴——在赛马会上——不列颠旅馆的圣诞节——医院里的元旦。

===Original===
Well, the great work was accomplished﻿—Sebastopol was taken. The Russians had retired sullenly to their stronghold on the north side of the harbour, from which, every now and then, they sent a few vain shot and shell, which sent the amateurs in the streets of Sebastopol scampering, but gave the experienced no concern. In a few days the camp could find plenty to talk about in their novel position﻿—and what then? What was to be done? More fighting? Another equally terrible and lengthy siege of the north? That was the business of a few at headquarters and in council at home, between whom the electric wires flashed many a message. In the meanwhile, the real workers applied themselves to plan amusements, and the same energy and activity which had made Sebastopol a heap of ruins and a well-filled cemetery﻿—which had dug the miles of trenches, and held them when made against a desperate foe﻿—which had manned the many guns, and worked them so well, set to work as eager to kill their present enemy, Time, as they had lately been to destroy their fled enemies, the Russians.

All who were before Sebastopol will long remember the beautiful autumn which succeeded to so eventful a summer, and ushered in so pleasantly the second winter of the campaign. It was appreciated as only those who earn the right to enjoyment can enjoy relaxation. The camp was full of visitors of every rank. They thronged the streets of Sebastopol, sketching its ruins and setting up photographic apparatus, in contemptuous indifference of the shot with which the Russians generally favoured every conspicuous group.

===Chinese===
好，大功告成了——塞瓦斯托波尔拿下了。俄国人悻悻地退到了港口北岸他们的堡垒里，从那儿时不时打出几发徒劳的炮弹枪弹，能把塞瓦斯托波尔街上的业余看客吓得四散奔逃，行家却毫不在意。不出几天，营地对着自己的新奇处境就有了说不完的话题——可然后呢？接下来做什么？再打？对北岸再来一场同样可怕而漫长的围攻？那是司令部少数人和国内枢府大员们的公务，电报线在他们之间往来闪烁着多少封电文。与此同时，真正做实事的人们一头扎进了筹划消遣，同一股劲头与活力——那把塞瓦斯托波尔夷为废墟、填成墓园的劲头，那掘出数英里战壕、又顶着亡命之敌把它守住的劲头，那操纵千百门大炮、打得如此漂亮的劲头——又热火朝天地干了起来，一心要歼灭眼下的敌人「时间」，一如他们不久前专心致志要消灭那逃走的敌人俄国人。

所有在塞瓦斯托波尔城前待过的人，都会久久记得那个美丽的秋天——它接续了如此多事的夏天，又如此惬意地开启了征战第二个冬天。唯有挣得享受权利的人，才懂得那样消受松弛。营地里挤满各色身份的访客。他们蜂拥在塞瓦斯托波尔的街上，描画废墟，支起照相器材，对俄国人惯常赏给每一群扎眼人的炮弹全然不屑一顾。

===Original===
Pleasure was hunted keenly. Cricket matches, picnics, dinner parties, races, theatricals, all found their admirers. My restaurant was always full, and once more merry laughter was heard, and many a dinner party was held, beneath the iron roof of the British Hotel. Several were given in compliment to our allies, and many distinguished Frenchmen have tested my powers of cooking. You might have seen at one party some of their most famous officers. At once were present a Prince of the Imperial family of France, the Duc de Rouchefoucault, and a certain corporal in the French service, who was perhaps the best known man in the whole army, the Viscount Talon. They expressed themselves highly gratified at the *carte*, and perhaps were not a little surprised as course after course made its appearance, and to soup and fish succeeded turkeys, saddle of mutton, fowls, ham, tongue, curry, pastry of many sorts, custards, jelly, blancmange, and olives. I took a peculiar pride in doing my best when they were present, for I knew a little of the secrets of the French commissariat. I wonder if the world will ever know more. I wonder if the system of secrecy which has so long kept veiled the sufferings of the French army before Sebastopol will ever yield to truth. I used to guess something of those sufferings when I saw, even after the fall of Sebastopol, half-starved French soldiers prowling about my store, taking eagerly even what the Turks rejected as unfit for human food; and no one could accuse *them* of squeamishness. I cannot but believe that in some desks or bureaux lie notes or diaries which shall one day be given to the world; and when this happens, the terrible distresses of the English army will pall before the unheard-of sufferings of the French. It is true that they carried from Sebastopol the lion’s share of glory. My belief is that they deserved it, having borne by far a larger proportion of suffering.

There were few dinners at Spring Hill at which the guests did not show their appreciation of their hostess’s labour by drinking her health; and at the dinner I have above alluded to, the toast was responded to with such enthusiasm that I felt compelled to put my acknowledgments into the form of a little speech, which Talon interpreted to his countrymen. The French Prince was, after this occasion, several times at the British Hotel. He was there once when some Americans were received by me with scarcely that cordiality which I have been told distinguished my reception of guests; and upon their leaving I told him﻿—quite forgetting his own connection with America﻿—of my prejudice against the Yankees. He heard me for a little while, and then he interrupted me.

“*Tenez!* Madame Seacole, I too am American a little.”

What a pity I was not born a countess! I am sure I should have made a capital courtier. Witness my impromptu answer:﻿—

“I should never have guessed it, Prince.”﻿—And he seemed amused.

===Chinese===
行乐被追逐得如火如荼。板球赛、野餐、晚宴、赛马、演戏，样样都有拥趸。我的餐馆总是客满，不列颠旅馆的铁皮屋顶下，又一次响起了欢快的笑声，办起了一场又一场晚宴。有几种是专为款待盟友而设的，许多显赫的法国人尝过我烹调的本领。有一席上你便可看到他们几位最有名的军官：法兰西皇室的一位亲王、罗什富科公爵（Duc de Rouchefoucault），还有法军中的一名下士——他兴许是全军最出名的人物——塔隆子爵（the Viscount Talon）。他们对菜单（*carte*）赞不绝口；一道接一道端上来时，他们怕也不无惊讶：汤和鱼之后，是火鸡、羊鞍、鸡、火腿、牛舌、咖喱、各色油酥点心、蛋奶冻、果冻、牛奶冻，还有橄榄。有他们在座时，我拿出全副本事，格外得意，因为法国军需系统的内情我略知一二。世道往后会不会知道得更多，我不知道。那套长期以来把法军在塞瓦斯托波尔城前的苦楚掩在幕后的保密制度，会不会终有一日向真相低头，我也不知道。从前每当我看见——即便在塞瓦斯托波尔陷落之后——半饿的法军士兵在我铺子里转悠，连土耳其人都嫌弃不配入口的东西也抢着要，我就约莫猜到那些苦楚的一二；而谁也不能指控土耳其人嘴刁。我不能不信，在某几张书桌或柜子里，锁着终有一天要交给世人的笔记或日记；到了那一天，英军的惨状将在法军闻所未闻的苦难面前黯然失色。不错，他们从塞瓦斯托波尔带走了狮子的那一份光荣。我相信他们受之无愧，因为他们受的苦，比例也大得多。

斯普林希尔的晚宴，很少有客人不举杯祝女主人健康、以酬她的辛劳的；在我上文提到的那次晚宴上，这一祝词赢得的欢呼那样热烈，我觉得不能不用一小篇致辞致谢，由塔隆翻译给他的同胞们听。此后，那位法国亲王又来过不列颠旅馆几次。有一回，几个美国人受到我的接待，据人说，热诚劲儿比不上我待客惯有的风格；他们走后，我对亲王讲起我对美国佬的成见——全然忘了他自己与美国的渊源。他听我说了一小会儿，便打断了我。

「*Tenez*!（喏！）西科尔夫人，我也算半个美国人呢。」

我不是生来的伯爵夫人，多可惜！我敢说，我本会做个顶出色的宫廷侍臣。证据便是我那脱口而出的答话：——

「『这是万万猜不出的，殿下。』——他似乎被逗乐了。」

===Original===
With the theatricals directly I had nothing to do. Had I been a little younger the companies would very likely have been glad of me, for no one liked to sacrifice their beards to become Miss Julia or plain Mary Ann; and even the beardless subalterns had voices which no coaxing could soften down. But I lent them plenty of dresses; indeed, it was the only airing which a great many gay-coloured muslins had in the Crimea. How was I to know when I brought them what camp-life was? And in addition to this, I found it necessary to convert my kitchen into a temporary greenroom, where, to the wonderment, and perhaps scandal, of the black cook, the ladies of the company of the 1st Royals were taught to manage their petticoats with becoming grace, and neither to show their awkward booted ankles, nor trip themselves up over their trains. It was a difficult task in many respects. Although I laced them in until they grew blue in the face, their waists were a disgrace to the sex; while﻿—crinoline being unknown then﻿—my struggles to give them becoming embonpoint may be imagined. It was not until a year later that *Punch* thought of using a clothes-basket; and I would have given much for such a hint when I was dresser to the theatrical company of the 1st Royals. The hair was another difficulty. To be sure, there was plenty in the camp, only it was in the wrong place, and many an application was made to me for a set of curls. However, I am happy to say I am not become a customer of the wigmakers yet.

===Chinese===
演戏的事，我倒没有直接沾手。我若年轻几岁，那几个戏班子多半乐意收我，因为谁也不肯牺牲胡子去扮朱莉亚小姐（Miss Julia）或普通的玛丽·安（Mary Ann）；就连无须的少尉们，嗓子也不是哄得软的。可我借了她们许多衣裳；说实在的，许多色彩鲜亮的细棉纱裙在克里米亚仅有的一次见天日，就是这一回。当初把它们带来时，我哪里知道营中生活是什么光景？除此之外，我发觉有必要把厨房改成临时后台，在那里教皇家第 1 团（1st Royals）戏班的「女士们」学着把裙子摆弄出得体的仪态，别露出笨拙的靴踝，也别叫裙裾绊了自己——直看得那位黑人厨子目瞪口呆，说不定还心里嘀咕。这桩差事在许多方面都难。任凭我把她们的束带勒到脸发青，她们的腰仍是对不住女性这个性别；而那时裙撑（crinoline）尚未问世——我要给她们营造出适度的丰满体态（embonpoint），那番挣扎可想而知。直到一年以后，《笨拙》才想出用衣篮的法子；当年我给皇家第 1 团戏班当服装师时，若得着这么一个提示，花多大代价我都愿意。头发是另一桩难题。营里倒不缺头发，只可惜长错了地方，许多人来求我配一绺卷发。不过，我很庆幸，至今还不曾当上假发匠的主顾。

===Original===
My recollections of hunting in the Crimea are confined to seeing troops of horsemen sweep by with shouts and yells after some wretched dog. Once I was very nearly frightened out of my wits﻿—my first impression being that the Russians had carried into effect their old threat of driving us into the sea﻿—by the startling appearance of a large body of horsemen tearing down the hill after, apparently, nothing. However I discovered in good time that, in default of vermin, they were chasing a brother officer with a paper bag.

My experience of Crimean races are perfect, for I was present, in the character of cantiniere, at all the more important meetings. Some of them took place before Christmas, and some after; but I shall exhaust the subject at once. I had no little difficulty to get the things on to the course; and in particular, after I had sat up the whole night making preparations for the December races, at the Monastery of St. George, I could not get my poor mules over the rough country, and found myself, in the middle of the day, some miles from the course. At last I gave it up as hopeless, and, dismounting, sat down by the roadside to consider how I could possibly dispose of the piles of sandwiches, bread, cheese, pies, and tarts, which had been prepared for the hungry spectators. At last, some officers, who expected me long before, came to look after me, and by their aid we reached the course.

===Chinese===
我在克里米亚的行猎回忆，只限于看一队队骑马的人呐喊怪叫着追逐某条倒霉的狗，呼啸而过。有一回我险些魂都吓掉了——第一反应是俄国人把把我们赶下海的老威胁付诸实施了——只见一大群骑手嚎叫着冲下山来，追的看样子什么也没有。好在我不失时机地发现，他们是因为无兽可猎，改追一位手里拿纸袋的同袍军官。

克里米亚赛马会的经历我堪称完备，因为更重要的那几场集会，我都是以随军女小贩（cantinière）的身份到场的。有几场办在圣诞节前，有几场在后；这题目我索性一次讲尽。把东西运到赛马场就够我费劲的；尤其是十二月的赛马会——设在圣乔治修道院（Monastery of St. George）——我通宵未眠做好一切准备，却怎么也没法让可怜的骡子走过那段崎岖野地，日头当顶时，还离赛场好几英里。最后我认定无望，下了马，坐在路边发愁：这一堆堆三明治、面包、奶酪、馅饼和果挞，本是为饥肠辘辘的看客备下的，如今可怎么打发。终于，几位等我等到焦急的军官寻了过来，靠他们帮忙，我们才赶到赛场。

===Original===
I was better off at the next meeting, for a kindhearted Major of Artillery provided me with a small bell-tent that was very useful, and enabled me to keep my stores out of reach of the light-fingered gentry, who were as busy in the Crimea as at Epsom or Hampton Court. Over this tent waved the flag of the British Hotel, but, during the day, it was struck, for an accident happening to one Captain D﻿—﻿—, he was brought to my tent insensible, where I quickly improvised a couch of some straw, covered with the Union Jack, and brought him round. I mention this trifle to show how ready of contrivance a little campaigning causes one to become. I had several patients in consequence of accidents at the races. Nor was I altogether free from accidents myself. On the occasion of the races by the Tchernaya, after the armistice, my cart, on turning a sudden bend in the steep track, upset, and the crates, containing plates and dishes, rolled over and over until their contents were completely broken up; so that I was reduced to hand about sandwiches, etc., on broken pieces of earthenware and scraps of paper. I saved some glasses, but not many, and some of the officers were obliged to drink out of stiff paper twisted into funnel-shaped glasses.

It was astonishing how well the managers of these Crimean races had contrived to imitate the old familiar scenes at home. You might well wonder where the racing saddles and boots, and silk caps and jackets had come from; but our connection with England was very different to what it had been when I first came to the Crimea, and many a wife and sister’s fingers had been busy making the racing gear for the Crimea meetings. And in order that the course should still more closely resemble Ascot or Epsom, some soldiers blackened their faces and came out as Ethiopian serenaders admirably, although it would puzzle the most ingenious to guess where they got their wigs and banjoes from. I caught one of them behind my tent in the act of knocking off the neck of a bottle of champagne, and, paralysed by the wine’s hasty exit, the only excuse he offered was, that he wanted to know if the officers’ luxury was better than rum.

===Chinese===
下一场集会我的境况好些了，一位好心的炮兵少校给我备了一顶小钟形帐篷，顶顶有用，让我的货色躲开了那些手脚不净的君子——他们在克里米亚跟在埃普瑟姆（Epsom）或汉普顿宫（Hampton Court）一样忙碌。不列颠旅馆的旗子在这顶帐篷上飘扬；不过白天旗子收了起来，因为 D——上尉出了事故，被抬进我的帐篷时人事不省，我赶紧用些干草铺了个卧榻，盖上联合王国国旗，把他救醒了过来。我提这桩小事，是想显一显短短的征战生涯能让人变得多么随机应变。赛马会上的意外给我招来了好几个病人。我自己也不全然免于意外。停战之后在切尔纳亚河边赛马那回，我的货车在陡急的弯道上一翻，装着盘碟的条板箱一路滚啊滚，里头的东西碎了个精光；害得我只得用碎陶片和纸片来分发三明治等等。玻璃杯我救下了一些，不多，几位军官只好把硬纸卷成漏斗杯喝酒。

这些克里米亚赛马会的操办者们，把国内那些熟悉的老场面模仿得惟妙惟肖，令人惊叹。你会纳闷那些赛马鞍具、马靴、绸帽绸衫是从哪儿来的；不过我们同英国的联系，同我初来克里米亚时已大不相同，多少位妻子和姐妹的手指，都在为克里米亚的赛马会赶制骑装。为了让赛马场更像阿斯科特（Ascot）或埃普瑟姆，一些士兵把脸涂黑，扮作「埃塞俄比亚小夜曲团」（Ethiopian serenaders）登台，演得有声有色——只是他们从哪儿弄来的假发和班卓琴，最巧的人也猜不出。我撞见其中一个在我帐篷后头，正敲掉一瓶香槟的瓶颈；酒沫喷得他目瞪口呆，他唯一的辩解是：他想尝尝军官的奢侈品是不是比朗姆酒强。

===Original===
A few weeks before Christmas, happened that fearful explosion, in the French ammunition park, which destroyed so many lives. We had experienced nothing at all like it before. The earth beneath us, even at the distance of three miles, reeled and trembled with the shock; and so great was the force of the explosion, that a piece of stone was hurled with some violence against the door of the British Hotel. We all felt for the French very much, although I do not think that the armies agreed quite so well after the taking of the Malakhoff, and the unsuccessful assault upon the Redan, as they had done previously. I saw several instances of unpleasantness and collision, arising from allusions to sore points. One, in particular, occurred in my store.

The French, when they wanted﻿—it was very seldom﻿—to wound the pride of the English soldiery, used to say significantly, in that jargon by which the various nations in the Crimea endeavoured to obviate the consequences of what occurred at the Tower of Babel, some time ago, “Malakhoff *bono*﻿—Redan no *bono*.” And this, of course, usually led to recriminatory statements, and history was ransacked to find something consolatory to English pride. Once I noticed a brawny man, of the Army Works Corps, bringing a small French Zouave to my canteen, evidently with the view of standing treat. The Frenchman seemed mischievously inclined, and, probably relying upon the good humour on the countenance of his gigantic companion, began a little playful badinage, ending with the taunt of “Redan, no *bono*﻿—Redan, no *bono*.” I never saw any man look so helplessly angry as the Englishman did. For a few minutes he seemed absolutely rooted to the ground. Of course he could have crushed his mocking friend with ease, but how could he answer his taunt. All at once, however, a happy thought struck him, and rushing up to the Zouave, he caught him round the waist and threw him down, roaring out, “Waterloo was *bono*﻿—Waterloo was *bono*.” It was as much as the people on the premises could do to part them, so convulsed were we all with laughter.

===Chinese===
圣诞节前几个星期，发生了法军弹药场那场可怕的大爆炸，夺去了那么多条性命。这样的场面我们从未经历过。哪怕隔着 3 英里，脚下的大地也震得摇摇晃晃；爆炸的威力大到一块石头狠狠砸在不列颠旅馆的门上。我们大家都为法国人难受；不过我以为，自马拉霍夫得手、棱堡强攻失利之后，两军相得，已不似先前那样融洽。我目睹过几起因触到痛处而起的不快与冲撞，有一桩就出在我的铺子里。

法国人想刺一刺英国兵的自尊时——这种时候很少——总爱用那句行话意味深长地说：「马拉霍夫 *bono*——棱堡 no *bono*」；这行话，是克里米亚各国人用来多少对付一下许久以前巴别塔（Tower of Babel）那桩事故的后遗症的。这话当然每每招来反唇相讥，历史也被翻个底朝天，好寻些宽慰英国自尊的东西。有一回，我注意到军工劳役队的一条大汉，领着一个小个子法国朱阿夫兵进我的小卖部，显然有意请客。那法国人看来存心淘气，大约仗着他那巨人同伴一脸的和气，起了点玩笑的心思，末了抛出那句挖苦：「棱堡 no *bono*——棱堡 no *bono*。」我从没见过谁气成那英国人那副无可奈何的样子。有那么几分钟，他简直像在地上扎了根。当然，他要捏碎那个取笑他的朋友易如反掌，可那句话叫他怎么回？忽然，一个好主意撞上心头，他扑向朱阿夫兵，拦腰一抱，把他掀翻在地，吼道：「滑铁卢 *bono*——滑铁卢 *bono*！」店里的人费了好大劲才把两人拉开，我们全都笑得直不起腰。

===Original===
And before Christmas, occurred my first and last attack of illness in the Crimea. It was not of much consequence, nor should I mention it but to show the kindness of my soldier-friends. I think it arose from the sudden commencement of winter, for which I was but poorly provided. However, I soon received much sympathy and many presents of warm clothing, etc.; but the most delicate piece of attention was shown me by one of the Sappers and Miners, who, hearing the report that I was dead, positively came down to Spring Hill to take my measure for a coffin. This may seem a questionable compliment, but I really felt flattered and touched with such a mark of thoughtful attention. Very few in the Crimea had the luxury of any better coffin than a blanket-shroud, and it was very good of the grateful fellow to determine that his old friend, the mistress of Spring Hill, should have an honour conceded to so very few of the illustrious dead before Sebastopol.

So Christmas came, and with it pleasant memories of home and of home comforts. With it came also news of home﻿—some not of the most pleasant description﻿—and kind wishes from absent friends. “A merry Christmas to you,” writes one, “and many of them. Although you will not write to us, we see your name frequently in the newspapers, from which we judge that you are strong and hearty. All your old Jamaica friends are delighted to hear of you, and say that you are an honour to the Isle of Springs.”

===Chinese===
也是在圣诞节前，我在克里米亚头一遭、也是末一遭病倒了。病得不重，我本不打算提，只为显一显士兵朋友们待我的好。我想病是骤然而至的冬天惹的，我的御寒准备太差。不过我很快收到许多同情和暖衣等等的馈赠；而最体贴周到的一份心意，来自工兵矿兵队（Sappers and Miners）的一名士兵——他听信了我死去的传言，竟当真下山到斯普林希尔，来给我量棺材的尺寸。这恭维听着不大吉利，可我实实在在受宠若惊，为这份体贴动了心。在克里米亚，极少有人享用得上比裹尸毡更好的棺材；这位知恩的汉子认定，他的老朋友、斯普林希尔的女主人，理当享有这份殊荣——在塞瓦斯托波尔城前那么多显赫的亡者里，得此待遇的凤毛麟角。他真是好心。

圣诞节就这样到了，随之而来的是家园与家中温情的美好回忆。随之而来的还有家书——有几封不算最愉快——还有远方朋友的祝愿。「祝你圣诞快乐，」一位写道，「并且年年如此。你虽然不给我们写信，报上却常见你的名字，由此我们断定你身子骨硬朗。你牙买加的老朋友们听说你的事迹都欢喜得很，说你是泉水之岛（Isle of Springs）的光荣。」

===Original===
I wonder if the people of other countries are as fond of carrying with them everywhere their home habits as the English. I think not. I think there was something purely and essentially English in the determination of the camp to spend the Christmas-day of 1855 after the good old “home” fashion. It showed itself weeks before the eventful day. In the dinner parties which were got up﻿—in the orders sent to England﻿—in the supplies which came out, and in the many applications made to the hostess of the British Hotel for plum-puddings and mince-pies. The demand for them, and the material necessary to manufacture them, was marvellous. I can fancy that if returns could be got at of the flour, plums, currants, and eggs consumed on Christmas-day in the out-of-the-way Crimean peninsula, they would astonish us. One determination appeared to have taken possession of every mind﻿—to spend the festive day with the mirth and jollity which the changed prospect of affairs warranted; and the recollection of a year ago, when death and misery were the camp’s chief guests, only served to heighten this resolve.

For three weeks previous to Christmas-day, my time was fully occupied in making preparations for it. Pages of my books are filled with orders for plum-puddings and mince-pies, besides which I sold an immense quantity of raw material to those who were too far off to send down for the manufactured article on Christmas-day, and to such purchasers I gave a plain recipe for their guidance. Will the reader take any interest in my Crimean Christmas-pudding? It was plain, but decidedly good. However, you shall judge for yourself:﻿—“One pound of flour, three-quarters of a pound of raisins, three-quarters of a pound of fat pork, chopped fine, two tablespoonfuls of sugar, a little cinnamon or chopped lemon, half-pint of milk or water; mix these well together, and boil four hours.”

===Chinese===
不知别国的人，是否也像英国人这样，走到哪儿都把家居的习惯带在身上。我想不是。全军上下打定主意，要照古老美好的「家园」老例，过 1855 年的圣诞节——这里面有种纯粹而彻头彻尾的英国气。这份心思在那个大日子前几个星期就露了头：办起来的一场场晚宴里，发往英国的订单里，运到的各色供应里，还有向不列颠旅馆女主人提出的许许多多葡萄干布丁和肉馅饼的订单里。这两样东西和做它们的原料的需求，大得惊人。我可以想见，倘若把那个圣诞节在偏远的克里米亚半岛上消耗掉的面粉、葡萄干、醋栗和鸡蛋统计出来，定会吓我们一跳。人人心里似乎都打定了一个主意：时局已然改观，这个节日就要过得欢畅热闹；而一年前的回忆——那时死亡与愁苦是营中的主客——更把这份决心烘得火热。

圣诞节前三个星期，我的时间全叫备节的活计占满了。我的账本，一页页写满葡萄干布丁和肉馅饼的订单；此外，我还卖给那些住得太远、圣诞节当天来不及下山取现成品的人大批原料，对这样的买主，我奉上一张简明的方子供他们参照。读者可会对我的克里米亚圣诞布丁感一点兴趣？方子简单，味道却着实不坏。好不好，您自己评判：——「面粉 1 磅，葡萄干 3/4 磅，肥猪肉 3/4 磅剁细，白糖 2 汤匙，少许肉桂或切碎的柠檬，牛奶或清水半品脱；把这些和匀，煮 4 小时。」

===Original===
From an early hour in the morning until long after the night had set in, were I and my cooks busy endeavouring to supply the great demand for Christmas fare. We had considerable difficulty in keeping our engagements, but by substituting mince-pies for plum-puddings, in a few cases, we succeeded. The scene in the crowded store, and even in the little overheated kitchen, with the officers’ servants, who came in for their masters’ dinners, cannot well be described. Some were impatient themselves, others dreaded their masters’ impatience as the appointed dinner hour passed by﻿—all combined by entreaties, threats, cajolery, and fun to drive me distracted. Angry cries for the major’s plum-pudding, which was to have been ready an hour ago, alternated with an entreaty that I should cook the captain’s mince-pies to a turn﻿—“Sure, he likes them well done, ma’am. Bake ’em as brown as your own purty face, darlint.”

I did not get my dinner until eight o’clock, and then I dined in peace off a fine wild turkey or bustard, shot for me on the marshes by the Tchernaya. It weighed twenty-two pounds, and, although somewhat coarse in colour, had a capital flavour.

Upon New Year’s-day I had another large cooking of plum-puddings and mince-pies; this time upon my own account. I took them to the hospital of the Land Transport Corps, to remind the patients of the home comforts they longed so much for. It was a sad sight to see the once fine fellows, in their blue gowns, lying quiet and still, and reduced to such a level of weakness and helplessness. They all seemed glad for the little home tokens I took them.

There was one patient who had been a most industrious and honest fellow, and who did not go into the hospital until long and wearing illness compelled him. I was particularly anxious to look after him, but I found him very weak and ill. I stayed with him until evening, and before I left him, kind fancy had brought to his bedside his wife and children from his village-home in England, and I could hear him talking to them in a low and joyful tone. Poor, poor fellow! the New Year so full of hope and happiness had dawned upon him, but he did not live to see the wild flowers spring up peacefully through the war-trodden sod before Sebastopol.

===Chinese===
从一大清早直到入夜许久，我和我的厨子们都在忙，尽力应付圣诞吃食的巨大需求。要守齐我们的应承颇有难度，但有几次拿肉馅饼顶替葡萄干布丁，总算应付过去了。挤满人的铺子里，乃至那间热气蒸腾的小厨房里的光景——军官们的仆人进来取主人的正餐——简直无从描摹。有的自己等得不耐烦，有的怕过了约定的开饭钟点、主人不耐烦——恳求、威吓、哄骗、打趣，一齐上阵地把我逼得发狂。少校的葡萄干布丁一钟头前就该得了，怒冲冲的催喊声此起彼伏；这边又有人恳求我把上尉的肉馅饼烤得恰到火候——「真的，他就爱吃烤得透透的，太太。把饼烤得跟您自个儿俊俏的脸蛋一样焦黄吧，亲爱的。」

我自己直到八点钟才吃上饭，然后安安稳稳地美餐了一只上好的野火鸡，或者叫大鸨（bustard）——是在切尔纳亚河边的沼地上为我猎的。它足有 22 磅重，肉色虽说粗了些，滋味却好极了。

元旦那天，我又大煮特煮了一回葡萄干布丁和肉馅饼；这一回是自掏腰包。我把它们送到陆运军团的医院，好让病人们想起他们朝思暮想的家中温情。看着那些昔日的好汉，穿着蓝色病号袍，安安静静躺着，衰弱无助到这般田地，真是叫人难过。他们见了我捎去的那点家乡的念想，个个都欢喜。

有一个病人，一向是最勤恳最本分的人，直到久病缠身、拖无可拖才进医院。我格外上心照看他，却见他已虚弱病重。我陪他到傍晚；临走之前，温情的幻想象已把他的妻子儿女，从英格兰村子里的家，唤到了他的床边，我听得见他低低地、喜滋滋地同他们说话。可怜、可怜的人！那个满怀希望与幸福的元旦在他头上亮起来了，可他没能活着看见野花静静开遍塞瓦斯托波尔城前那片被战火踩烂的草皮。
